VTNE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Spreading pressure was too heavy, so use a light even touch to keep cells intact): Lymphoid cells are fragile, and firm pressure while squashing the slides breaks them into smudged, streaming nuclei. A light, even touch with a gentle pull keeps cells intact and the film thin. Slower spreading, longer air drying and a weaker stain change the look of intact cells but do not repair cells that were already crushed.
  2. The correct answer is A (40 mL of warmed fluid under the loose skin over the dorsal shoulders): The volume is 10 mL/kg x 4 kg = 40 mL. The fluid goes into the loose skin over the dorsal shoulders with the skin tented and the needle directed parallel to the body, ideally warmed to body temperature. Multiplying by 2.2 gives an incorrect 88 mL, deep muscle is not a subcutaneous site, and 4 mL is a tenfold dose error.
  3. The correct answer is A (Neutrophils 60 percent, lymphocytes 32 percent, monocytes 2 percent, eosinophils 6 percent): Healthy cats usually have neutrophil predominance, roughly 35 to 75 percent, with lymphocytes about 20 to 55 percent and eosinophils about 2 to 12 percent. Lymphocytosis tempts because fearful cats can show it, but this cat was bled calmly. A neutrophil-heavy count with few lymphocytes fits stress or corticosteroid effect, and an eosinophil count of 25 percent points to allergy or parasites.
  4. The correct answer is B (The 50 mcg/hr patch, which delivers about 2.5 mcg/kg/hr to this dog): The target is 2 to 3 mcg/kg/hr x 20 kg = 40 to 60 mcg/hr, and the 50 mcg/hr patch gives 50 / 20 = 2.5 mcg/kg/hr. The 100 mcg/hr patch gives 5 mcg/kg/hr, which is above the protocol for a first patch. The 25 mcg/hr patch gives only 1.25 mcg/kg/hr and the 12 mcg/hr patch gives 0.6 mcg/kg/hr, both under the target. Patches also need 12 to 24 hours to reach effect, so other analgesia covers the gap.
  5. The correct answer is A (Respiratory rate 12 to 20 breaths per minute and pulse 70 to 90 beats per minute): A resting adult sheep has a respiratory rate of about 12 to 20 breaths per minute and a pulse of about 70 to 90 beats per minute. Heart rates near 120 to 140 are seen in newborn lambs or in stressed animals. Lower respiratory rates match larger species such as horses, and higher ones indicate stress, heat or disease.
  6. The correct answer is C (Lower the spreader to about 30 to 45 degrees and glide it forward smoothly): A steep spreader angle and a fast push both give a short, thick film with a ragged tail, because the blood has too little time to spread along the edge. Lowering the angle to about 30 to 45 degrees and moving smoothly lets the drop run along the spreader and trail into a feathered edge. A steeper angle with a slower push would still leave the film thick, a bigger drop thickens it further, and alcohol on the slide does not change film length.
  7. The correct answer is B (Put in the drops first and wait about five minutes, then apply the ointment): Solutions go on first because they absorb quickly and are blocked if oily ointment already coats the eye. Waiting about five minutes lets the drops absorb and avoids washing out the first drug. Ointments go last because they stay longer on the surface. Putting ointment first blocks the drops, and mixing products can change the dose and contaminate the tubes.
  8. The correct answer is B (A transfixation ligature passes through the pedicle first, so it cannot slip off): A transfixation ligature has the needle passed through the pedicle before tying, so the loop is anchored in the tissue and cannot slip off. A simple ligature only encircles the pedicle. The extra security comes from the pass through the tissue, which is why this technique is chosen for large or thick pedicles, and the number of throws does not define it.
  9. The correct answer is C (Babcock forceps, atraumatic fenestrated jaws that hold bowel or ovarian pedicles gently): Babcock forceps have rounded, fenestrated jaws that grasp bowel and delicate tissue without crushing it. Allis forceps have small interlocking teeth, so they traumatize tissue and are used on tissue that will be removed. Kelly forceps are hemostats and Rochester-Carmalt forceps are crushing clamps.
  10. The correct answer is A (A modified Knott test, which concentrates microfilariae so they can be identified): The modified Knott test mixes blood with 2 percent formalin to lyse red cells, then spins and examines the sediment, which concentrates microfilariae and allows measurement of morphology. A direct smear misses low counts, an antigen test shows adult female worm antigen only, and antibody tests do not identify circulating larvae.
  11. The correct answer is C (Lower the vaporizer to the least setting that holds depth and tell the veterinarian): Isoflurane causes dose related vasodilation, so with a good cuff and a normal depth, reducing the vaporizer is the first step and the veterinarian decides on a bolus or a pressor. A shock rate in a cat risks overload, atropine is not indicated at a rate of 130, and a systolic of 60 mmHg is below the accepted range.
  12. The correct answer is D (Expected opioid effects: nausea from the trigger zone and slowed heart rate from vagal tone): Vomiting and a lowered heart rate are common early effects of full mu opioids such as hydromorphone, from stimulation of the chemoreceptor trigger zone and vagal tone. The dog is alert with pink membranes and normal refill, so shock and overdose, which would cause sedation and slow breathing, are unlikely, and anaphylaxis usually shows hives, facial swelling, or collapse. Monitoring continues.
  13. The correct answer is C (A kink or flexed limb blocking the line, so straighten the leg and check the clamps): A downstream occlusion alarm means resistance between the pump and the patient. With a plentiful bag and a tightly flexed leg, a kink, bent catheter or closed clamp is most likely, so the technician straightens the limb, checks clamps and the line, and flushes only if directed. An empty bag triggers an upstream or air alarm, and neither battery nor a low rate causes a pressure alarm.
  14. The correct answer is A (Respiratory acidosis from hypoventilation, so start assisted positive pressure breaths): An ETCO2 of 68 mmHg with a slow, shallow rate means CO2 is not being removed, which lowers blood pH and causes respiratory acidosis. The SpO2 is normal, so oxygenation is not the issue, and a fresh canister excludes rebreathing. The response is to ventilate the dog with positive pressure at about 10 mL/kg and adjust the rate until ETCO2 is 35 to 45 mmHg. Bicarbonate does not treat a respiratory cause.
  15. The correct answer is B (Give it on the veterinarian's direct order and repeat the dose back, then record it): An RVT gives emergency drugs under the direction of the veterinarian, and a verbal order is acceptable in an arrest when drug, dose and route are repeated back before administration and then recorded. Confirming afterward leaves no chance to catch an error. Waiting for a written order wastes time in a resuscitation, and giving a drug from a chart without an order goes beyond the delegated scope.
  16. The correct answer is B (Staging needs probing and radiographs under anesthesia, not a look at the crown): Periodontal stage is based on attachment loss, which sits below the gum line. Visible redness and calculus show inflammation but not how much support is gone, so full-mouth probing and dental radiographs under general anesthesia are needed. Calculus volume does not equal bone loss, and antibiotics alone do not remove plaque and calculus or reverse established disease.
  17. The correct answer is C (12 drops per minute, since 12 mL/hr x 60 drops/mL / 60 min/hr equals 12): Drops per minute = (mL/hr x drops/mL) / 60 minutes. That is (12 x 60) / 60 = 12 drops per minute, and with a 60 drops/mL set the drops per minute equal the mL per hour. Leaving out the division by 60 gives 720, while using a 15 or 120 drops/mL factor gives 3 or 24 drops per minute, the wrong rate for this set.
  18. The correct answer is B (Rapidly worsening dyspnea, because a sealed dressing can trap air and cause tension pneumothorax): An occlusive dressing stops air entering through the wound, but if the lung is also punctured, air can keep escaping into the chest and cannot leave, so a tension pneumothorax can develop. Increasing respiratory effort, restlessness or falling oxygen saturation means the dressing must be opened and the veterinarian called. The other problems are less likely or not tied to the seal.
  19. The correct answer is C (Cells in the unseparated sample used glucose, lowering the laboratory value): Red and white cells keep metabolizing glucose in a tube left on the clot, which lowers the value by about 5 to 10 percent per hour and more in a young animal with a high cell turnover. The fresh glucometer reading reflects the true glucose. There is no fixed 50 percent offset, and hemolysis does not lower a glucose measurement.
  20. The correct answer is B (The linea alba first, then subcutaneous tissue and finally the skin): Closure follows the reverse of the incision order: the linea alba or body wall first, then the subcutaneous layer to remove dead space, and the skin last. The incision goes through skin, subcutaneous tissue, then linea alba, so listing that order, or closing skin before deeper layers, is incorrect.
  21. The correct answer is A (Lidocaine toxicity, so stop injecting and give oxygen while calling for help): Cats are sensitive to lidocaine, and 14 mg in a 3.5 kg cat is 4 mg/kg, with vessel injection making it worse. Tremors followed by seizure fit lidocaine toxicity. The first steps are to stop, give oxygen, protect the airway and get a benzodiazepine ordered. The normal glucose argues against hypoglycemia, no opioid was given, and a seizure needs active treatment.
  22. The correct answer is A (Monofilament drags less and has more memory, while braided suture can harbour bacteria): Monofilament is a single strand, so it slides through tissue with little drag, resists bacterial wicking and needs extra knot throws because of its memory. Braided multifilament material has more knot security and easier handling but its interstices can harbour bacteria. Colour has no relation to the choice.
  23. The correct answer is B (180 mL, from converting 39.6 lb to 18 kg and multiplying by 10 mL/kg): Convert first: 39.6 lb / 2.2 = 18 kg. Tidal volume = 18 kg x 10 mL/kg = 180 mL. Using the pound figure as kilograms gives 396 mL, which can overinflate the lungs. A tenfold slip gives 1,800 mL, and a tenth of the rate gives 18 mL, which is too small to ventilate.
  24. The correct answer is C (Hold patient results and troubleshoot with fresh control and reagent, then rerun the controls first): Two consecutive control values beyond the same two standard deviation limit signal a systematic shift, so patient results must not be reported until the cause is found. Fresh control, reagent and calibration status should be checked and controls rerun within range. Using a three standard deviation limit, resetting the mean, or recalibrating without rerunning controls leaves the fault unverified.
  25. The correct answer is B (Forcing the tail past a right angle to the spine, causing pain and fracture risk): The tail should be raised only to about 90 degrees from the spine, because forcing it higher is painful and can fracture a coccygeal vertebra, which would make a cow kick and bellow. The stem describes the tail pulled to 120 degrees, which points directly at this fault. A dorsal puncture site, the type of restraint and a missing tourniquet do not explain a sudden reaction tied to the tail being pulled up.
  26. The correct answer is A (4.5 mL/hr, since 45 mcg/hr divided by 10 mcg/mL gives 4.5): The hourly dose is 3 mcg/kg/hr x 15 kg = 45 mcg/hr. The syringe holds 10 mcg in each mL, so 45 / 10 = 4.5 mL/hr. Dividing by 60 gives 0.75, which is a per-minute value. Reusing 45 ignores the concentration, and using 33 lb gives 99 mcg/hr and 9.9 mL/hr, which would double the ordered opioid dose.
  27. The correct answer is A (240 mL/hr, from 60 mL given over a quarter of an hour): The bolus volume is 10 mL/kg x 6 kg = 60 mL. Fifteen minutes is 0.25 hr, so 60 mL / 0.25 hr = 240 mL/hr. Setting 60 mL/hr would take a full hour, and 4 mL/hr is the per-minute figure. Multiplying by 15 gives a rate that would be a dangerous overdose.
  28. The correct answer is C (Air between probe and skin from too little gel or uneven contact on the clipped coat): Sound does not cross air, so a gap of air under the probe reflects the beam at once and leaves a bright top line over a dark field. More gel or firm contact on clipped skin fixes it. Too shallow a depth would show a partial organ, a high frequency would still show near structures, and high gain brightens the image rather than blackening it.
  29. The correct answer is B (Angle the swab dorsally then forward along the vaginal wall, and roll it onto a slide): The swab is moistened with saline and directed upward first to avoid the urethral opening and clitoral fossa, then moved horizontally along the vaginal wall where the cells reflect hormonal changes. Rolling it gently onto the slide keeps cell morphology intact. Sampling the vulvar floor or skin gives contaminants and squamous cells that do not reflect vaginal staging, and passing a swab through the cervix is not done.
  30. The correct answer is B (Remove organic matter and apply a standard disinfectant for its contact time, then rinse and dry): Feline leukemia virus is an enveloped virus that is fragile outside the cat and is killed by common disinfectants and by drying. Cleaning first removes organic matter that blocks disinfectant, and the product is left on for its labelled contact time before rinsing and drying. A long wait or double strength products are not needed, while washing alone does not disinfect.
  31. The correct answer is C (Primary metabolic acidosis, with a low bicarbonate accounting for the low pH): A low pH with low bicarbonate points to metabolic acidosis, here from ketoacids in diabetic ketoacidosis. The pCO2 of 5.1 kPa is within range, which is common in cats whose respiratory compensation is limited. Respiratory acidosis would show a high pCO2, and alkalosis would show a high pH.
  32. The correct answer is D (Allow quiet time and acknowledge the hard decision, then offer tissues and privacy): Silence, a simple acknowledgment and practical comfort such as tissues give the client space to grieve and respect her decision. Moving straight to procedure steps ignores her emotion in the moment. Suggesting she reconsider undermines a decision she has already made, and a general remark about how other owners feel or how time will help can sound dismissive.
  33. The correct answer is C (Date and patient identification with amount used, balance on hand and administering signature): A controlled drug log tracks every movement so the count can be reconciled: the date, the patient the drug was used on, the quantity, the balance remaining and who administered it. Without a running balance a discrepancy cannot be detected. Lot numbers and expiry dates belong to inventory records, purchase details belong to receiving records, and a supervising signature does not show who actually gave the drug.
  34. The correct answer is D (Anticoagulant rodenticide toxicosis; vitamin K1 therapy with plasma if bleeding is severe): Anticoagulant rodenticides block vitamin K recycling, so factors II, VII, IX and X fall and PT and aPTT lengthen while platelets stay normal. Treatment is vitamin K1, with plasma or blood when bleeding is severe. Disseminated coagulation usually drops platelets and needs an underlying disease, hemophilia A prolongs the aPTT but not the PT, and protamine reverses heparin, which the dog has not received.
  35. The correct answer is C (Wash the wound well with soap and water, then call a doctor or public health): Thorough washing with soap and water for several minutes reduces the virus load, and the client needs prompt medical assessment because rabies prophylaxis decisions are made by physicians and public health. A stray that ran off cannot be observed or tested. A veterinarian does not assess people, and booking a visit for next week loses time that matters for post-exposure treatment.
  36. The correct answer is B (The pack was exposed to the process conditions, but this does not prove sterility): Chemical indicator tape shows that the pack was exposed to the process, such as steam heat, and it separates processed from unprocessed packs. It cannot prove that spores were killed or that the center reached time and temperature, which needs an internal indicator or a biological indicator. Shelf life depends on the wrap and storage, not the tape.
  37. The correct answer is A (Flush each lumen with enzymatic detergent using a syringe and a channel brush): Organic material in a lumen shields microbes from disinfectants and sterilants, so channels must be flushed with detergent and brushed first. Enzymatic detergent breaks down protein. Soaking in disinfectant fixes protein in place, wiping only the outside leaves the lumen dirty, and autoclaving uncleaned items bakes the debris onto the metal.
  38. The correct answer is C (Scan the smear's feathered edge for platelet clumps and estimate the platelet count by hand): Platelet clumps are common in cats and are counted as single large particles or not counted, so an analyzer can report a falsely low value. Checking the feathered edge for clumps and estimating the count from the smear confirms whether it is a true thrombocytopenia. Reporting the result unchecked, recollecting first, or running coagulation tests all skip the quickest confirmation already available.
  39. The correct answer is C (100 mL of the 10 percent stock plus 400 mL of sterile water): C1V1 = C2V2 gives 10% x V1 = 2% x 500 mL, so V1 = 100 mL of stock. Adding 400 mL of water brings the total to 500 mL. Swapping the volumes would produce an 8 percent solution, while 50 mL of stock would give a 1 percent solution. Equal parts of 10 percent stock and water would yield 5 percent.
  40. The correct answer is B (20 mg/mL, because a 2% solution holds 2 g of drug in every 100 mL): Percent solution means grams per 100 mL. A 2% solution has 2 g = 2,000 mg in 100 mL, so 2,000 / 100 = 20 mg/mL. Reading 2% as 2 mg/mL underestimates the drug by a factor of ten. The 10 mg/mL figure applies only to 1%. The 200 mg/mL figure uses the wrong volume. Overdosing lidocaine in a small cat risks seizures and cardiac toxicity.
  41. The correct answer is C (Discard the torn sleeve and disinfect the sensor with a wipe, then fit a new one): Digital sensors are damaged by heat, so they are never autoclaved. The standard approach is a single-use barrier over the sensor for each patient, then cleaning and disinfection with an agent the manufacturer approves, since a torn sleeve means saliva may have reached the surface. A rinse with water does not disinfect, and disinfecting does not replace the barrier on the next patient.
  42. The correct answer is C (Remove feces daily before eggs mature and wash hands, then deworm the kitten on time): Toxocara eggs need roughly two to four weeks in the environment to become infective, so daily feces removal, handwashing and scheduled deworming break the cycle. Monthly cleaning leaves infective eggs, adult worms live in the intestine, and eggs are in feces, not meat.
  43. The correct answer is B (0.15 mL, because 75 mcg divided by 500 mcg/mL gives 0.15 mL): Dose = 5 mcg/kg x 15 kg = 75 mcg. The vial holds 0.5 mg/mL = 500 mcg/mL, so volume = 75 / 500 = 0.15 mL. The 1.5 mL result is a tenfold decimal slip and would overdose the dog. The 0.075 mL result assumes a 1 mg/mL product. The 0.30 mL result doubles the intended dose rate, which would worsen bradycardia and vasoconstriction.
  44. The correct answer is B (67.5 mL, which is 15 mL/kg multiplied by the 4.5 kg body weight): The order is 15 mL/kg and the cat weighs 4.5 kg, so 15 x 4.5 = 67.5 mL (4.5 x 10 = 45 plus 4.5 x 5 = 22.5). Multiplying by the weight in pounds gives 148.5 mL, which treats a kilogram order as a pound order. A full 45 mL/kg shock dose is far larger than one aliquot, and halving the volume under-resuscitates a cat in shock. Cats are given shock fluid in smaller aliquots with reassessment between them, which is why 15 mL/kg is the right quantity here.
  45. The correct answer is D (Scruff firmly behind the head and support the hindquarters with the other hand): A firm scruff often causes a ferret to relax, and supporting the hindquarters prevents strain on the spine. Letting the body hang loose strains the back, pinning it flat with a palm can injure it and invites biting, and gripping the neck instead of the loose skin behind the head can restrict breathing and hurt the animal.
  46. The correct answer is B (ASA I, a healthy patient with no systemic disease having elective surgery): ASA I applies to a healthy patient with no systemic disease, so a healthy young cat for elective spay is ASA I. ASA II needs mild systemic disease, such as obesity or mild heart disease, and ASA III requires severe disease. The E modifier is added only when the procedure is an emergency, not an elective surgery.
  47. The correct answer is A (0.12 mL, from 1.2 mg divided by the 10 mg/mL vial concentration): The dose is 6 kg x 0.2 mg/kg = 1.2 mg. Dividing by the concentration gives 1.2 mg / 10 mg/mL = 0.12 mL. Drawing 1.2 mL would be a tenfold overdose because it reads mg as mL, and 0.6 mL comes from using a 2 mg/mL concentration that is not on this vial. A 0.012 mL answer is a decimal slip and could not be measured accurately in a 1 mL syringe.
  48. The correct answer is B (1,200 mL/min from a 100 mL tidal volume times 12 breaths per minute): Tidal volume = 10 mL/kg x 10 kg = 100 mL. Minute ventilation = 100 mL x 12 breaths/min = 1,200 mL/min. The 120 value skips the weight step. The 2,640 value uses 22 lb, giving 220 mL x 12. The 1,000 value substitutes the weight for the rate. Each wrong value comes from a different slip in the same two-step calculation.
  49. The correct answer is A (Check the endotracheal tube cuff seal and run a circuit leak test before going on): The canister weight is unchanged and the hose is intact, so the likely source of waste gas is a leak at the patient end, such as an underinflated cuff or a loose circuit connection. A leak test and cuff check find it. Raising flow only dilutes the gas, stopping the vaporizer loses anesthetic depth, and replacing an unsaturated canister does not address the leak.
  50. The correct answer is C (An extraoral lateral oblique view, mouth open and beam angled clear of the other arch): In a cat, a small mouth often prevents placing the sensor intraorally. An extraoral lateral oblique view, with the mouth open and the beam angled so the opposite arch is projected clear of the molar, isolates the mandibular teeth without crowding the mouth. A closed-mouth lateral view superimposes both arches, and a ventrodorsal view cannot separate one molar. Forcing a sensor deeper into the mouth risks trauma and still may not capture the roots.
  51. The correct answer is C (Gingiva and periodontal ligament with cementum and the alveolar bone of the jaw): The periodontium is the supporting apparatus: gingiva, periodontal ligament, cementum covering the root, and alveolar bone. Enamel, dentin and pulp belong to the tooth itself. Periodontal disease damages the periodontium, which is why loss of attachment loosens teeth.
  52. The correct answer is B (About two to three days after it forms on the tooth surface): Plaque is a soft bacterial biofilm that begins to mineralize with minerals from saliva within two to three days, which is why daily brushing is recommended. Six hours is too short, and weeks or months would allow calculus to form well before the next brushing.
  53. The correct answer is D (Confirm blood pressure is adequate and keep monitoring, then tell the veterinarian): Opioids raise vagal tone and can slow the heart and cause a dropped-beat AV block. This dog has a normal pressure, pink gums and is alert, so the rhythm is tolerated. The technician confirms perfusion, keeps monitoring and tells the veterinarian, who may decide on an anticholinergic if the pressure falls. Lidocaine treats ventricular rhythms, and compressions or pacing are for arrest.
  54. The correct answer is B (Consider them contaminated and set them aside for reprocessing, then have a replacement opened): Any sterile item that falls or passes below the level of the table is considered contaminated, because the area below the table edge is not sterile. The scissors are removed for reprocessing and a sterile replacement is opened. Soaking in saline does not sterilize an instrument, keeping a contaminated item for emergencies still risks the patient, and contact with the floor or the tips touching something is not required for contamination.
  55. The correct answer is B (0.5 mL, the label dose given regardless of body size): Vaccine doses are fixed by the label and are not weight based, because the antigen dose and not the mg/kg amount drives the immune response. A kitten of eight weeks or more receives the same 0.5 mL as an adult cat. Splitting the dose or scaling it down may under-immunize, while doubling it adds reaction risk and wastes a multi-dose product.
  56. The correct answer is D (Clip dry hair with a cool and sharp number 40 blade held flat using light strokes): Hair is clipped dry with a fine, sharp, cool blade held flat to the skin and light strokes, which gives a close clip without cutting skin. Wet hair clogs the blade and irritates skin, and firm pressure or a steep angle causes clipper burn and nicks. A coarse blade leaves hair that must be clipped again.
  57. The correct answer is B (About 1 to 2 contractions every minute, with a cycle that rises and falls): A healthy cow has roughly 1 to 2 primary contractions per minute, heard as a rising and falling rumble in the left paralumbar fossa. Far fewer indicates hypomotility, and silence suggests atony. Rapid sounds are not the normal rumen pattern.
  58. The correct answer is C (Decline, since diagnosing and prescribing are veterinarian duties): In veterinary medicine, diagnosing, prognosis and prescribing are acts reserved for veterinarians, and technicians work within the limits their provincial legislation sets. A technician may collect and report findings but not decide a diagnosis or treatment. A signature from the practice owner or agreement from a colleague does not carry veterinary authority, and a phone order does not make the diagnosis the technician's to give.
  59. The correct answer is A (Seal the whole tablet inside the pill pocket and hand feed it, then confirm swallowing): A pill pocket is meant to hold the whole tablet fully covered so the cat swallows it without tasting it. Handing it over directly lets the client confirm the dose was taken. A partly exposed tablet will be tasted and spat out, and splitting or dissolving a film-coated tablet changes its absorption and leaves the client unsure how much the cat actually received.
  60. The correct answer is D (Inhaled nonstick coating fumes, so move to fresh air and give oxygen then alert the veterinarian): Overheated polytetrafluoroethylene coatings release fumes that rapidly injure bird lungs and can cause sudden distress and death, and the history of a hot empty pan points to it. The bird needs fresh air, oxygen and prompt veterinary care. Hypocalcemia is a real grey parrot problem, but nothing here supports it, and an infection or metal toxicosis would not appear suddenly after a hot empty pan.
  61. The correct answer is A (Tracheal irritation from an over-inflated cuff and repeated intubation attempts): A cuff inflated to 40 cm H2O and several intubation attempts can injure the tracheal mucosa and cause cough and wheeze a day or two later. The absence of fever and kennel exposure makes infection less likely, and there are no signs of heart failure or regurgitation. The veterinarian should examine the dog. In later anesthetics the leak test endpoint is 20 cm H2O, and intubation is limited to a minimum of attempts.
  62. The correct answer is C (Keep it away from heat and wash hands after contact, then return used patches folded in): Heat raises fentanyl absorption and can cause overdose, so no heating pad should sit on the patch. Owners wash hands, keep children and other pets away, and return used patches folded sticky side together so the clinic can destroy them as a controlled substance. Used patches still hold a large amount of fentanyl, so flushing or household trash puts it where children and pets can reach it, and a patch for a dog is left in place for about three days.
  63. The correct answer is D (Blot the surface exudate with gauze, then press a clean slide gently onto the lesion): For a moist surface lesion, blotting off surface debris and pressing a slide directly onto the tissue gives an impression smear with intact cells. A swab roll suits ear canals or fistulas, not a flat ulcer. Scraping, hard rubbing and heat fixing a wet film damage or distort the cells.
  64. The correct answer is D (Check the pump and line and catheter for interrupted delivery, then assess pain and report): Fentanyl typically lowers the heart rate, so a steady climb with panting and whining suggests lost analgesia, such as an empty bag, a kinked or disconnected line, or an out-of-vein catheter. The technician checks the delivery system, assesses pain and informs the veterinarian. Rates are not changed without an order.
  65. The correct answer is C (Bring the cat in right away, since a blocked male cat can deteriorate): A male cat that strains with little or no urine output may have a urethral obstruction, which can cause life-threatening hyperkalemia and kidney injury within hours. Owners often mistake urinary straining for constipation, so the technician asks about what is produced and does not recommend a laxative. Waiting two days, or trying a diet and fluids at home first, delays treatment the cat needs now.
  66. The correct answer is B (Rapid redistribution gives a short effect, but a fast injection causes apnea): Propofol is highly lipid soluble and redistributes quickly from the brain, so recovery is rapid and repeat doses do not accumulate in dogs. Rapid injection can cause apnea, so it should be given slowly. It has little analgesic effect. It tends to lower blood pressure, so it is not a drug of choice in shock patients.
  67. The correct answer is B (Score pain and ask the veterinarian for the next opioid dose, since analgesia is fading): Heart rate climbs from 88 to 148 and respiratory rate from 18 to 42 as the opioid wears off, with whimpering and licking supporting pain. Normal blood pressure and temperature argue against hypovolemia and hypothermia, which would not raise the heart rate this way. The technician scores pain and requests the next dose now, because scheduled intervals should not override a patient in pain.
  68. The correct answer is C (Cats have natural antibodies, so mismatched blood can cause fatal hemolysis on a first transfusion): Unlike dogs, cats have naturally occurring alloantibodies against the blood type they lack, so type A blood in a type B cat can cause fatal hemolysis even on the very first unit. Typing before every feline transfusion is therefore required, with a crossmatch added. The dog rule of waiting for sensitization does not apply to cats. There is no safe universal donor cat type, and a test dose is not a reliable substitute for typing.
  69. The correct answer is B (Escalate to the veterinarian now, since uncontrolled pain with poor perfusion suggests a surgical lesion): Pain that continues after a full flunixin dose, a heart rate near 88 and prolonged capillary refill are signs of severe disease, often needing surgery or intensive treatment. The veterinarian should be informed at once. A repeat dose of the same NSAID risks toxicity without addressing the cause, a second NSAID adds kidney and gut risk, and walking cannot fix a lesion that is already compromising perfusion.
  70. The correct answer is C (A platelet function defect such as von Willebrand disease, since the count is adequate): The buccal mucosal bleeding time tests primary hemostasis, which depends on platelet number and function and on von Willebrand factor. With a normal count and normal PT and aPTT, a prolonged time points to a platelet function defect such as von Willebrand disease, a breed-associated problem in Dobermans. Factor deficiencies change PT or aPTT, and a normal range does not extend to 10 minutes.
  71. The correct answer is A (39.4 C, because cooling continues for a time and a lower target risks rebound hypothermia): Active cooling is stopped at about 39.4 C (103 F), because body temperature keeps falling for several minutes afterward and cooling below this point risks hypothermia. A target of 38.0 or 37.2 C overshoots, and 40.5 C is still in the range where heat injury continues.
  72. The correct answer is A (Write the order in the chart and controlled log with the time, then get confirmation): A verbal order for a controlled drug is documented at once in the patient chart and in the controlled drug log, with drug, dose, route, time and the person who ordered it, and the veterinarian confirms it afterward. Delaying the log entry, to the end of the day or until the veterinarian returns, breaks running balance accuracy. Logging the dose as the technician's own entry hides who actually ordered the drug.
  73. The correct answer is D (Stain precipitate from old or unfiltered stains; filter or replace the solutions): Stain precipitate appears as scattered dark purple granules that lie over cells and background, while the cell morphology beneath stays crisp. It comes from old, evaporated or contaminated stain solutions and is prevented by filtering and replacing them regularly. Poor drying causes smudged and lysed cells, not an overlay of granules.
  74. The correct answer is C (About 58 mL/hr from dividing 1,400 mL by 24 hours given): The rate is the volume divided by the hours: 1,400 / 24 = 58.3 mL/hr. Using 12 hours gives 117 mL/hr and would finish early, dividing by 60 gives a per-minute style value of 23, and dividing by an 8 hour shift gives 175 mL/hr.
  75. The correct answer is B (Formalin vapors partly fixed the smears, so store slides well away from formalin jars): Formalin fumes can fix cells on nearby slides and interfere with Romanowsky staining, producing pale, poorly detailed nuclei. Cytology slides should be stored in a separate container away from the formalin jar. Trapped moisture, slow drying and a buffer fault can each affect staining, but none explains the change after overnight storage beside the formalin when the stain and times were correct.

The initial step is to recheck for simple recording errors, then report in writing.   77. The correct answer is D (The diaphragm moves forward, so organs are less crowded and motion is reduced): At end expiration the diaphragm sits cranially, so abdominal organs are not crowded by it and the pause between breaths is when the patient is stillest, which limits motion blur. The lungs do not empty and are still partly visible. Inspiration moves the diaphragm caudally and presses organs together.
  78. The presence and regularity of the waveform still confirm that the patient is breathing, and sudden changes or loss of the waveform give early warning of apnea or obstruction.   79. The correct answer is C (Center the light on the site and angle the heads so beams cross from different sides): Overhead lights aimed from different angles over the field fill in shadows cast by the team, because each beam lights the area another beam leaves dark. Low or single lamps create stronger shadows from the surgeon's body. Brightness alone does not remove shadows, and a dark room makes eye strain worse.
  80. The correct answer is A (One and a half teaspoons, because 7.5 mL divided by 5 mL is 1.5): One teaspoon is 5 mL, so 7.5 mL divided by 5 mL is 1.5 teaspoons. Rounding up to a tablespoon would give 15 mL, double the dose, and rounding up to two teaspoons would give 10 mL, a third too much. Dividing by a 15 mL spoon gives half a spoon but does not match a teaspoon. A dosing syringe is still more accurate than household spoons.

An uneven load causes vibration and can damage the rotor or break the tubes. Sealed ends pointing inward would let cells pack toward the open end, and the stem says the speed was usual, while the fill level of a tube does not cause a whole rotor to walk across the bench.
  83. The correct answer is A (Atipamezole, which competitively blocks alpha-2 receptors and reverses the sedation): Dexmedetomidine is an alpha-2 agonist, and atipamezole is its specific antagonist. Naloxone reverses opioids and flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines, so neither works on an alpha-2 agonist. Doxapram stimulates breathing but does not reverse sedation. Reversal also removes the analgesia, so pain control must be planned before the dog wakes.
  84. Drug delays reduce survival, atropine does not treat this rhythm, and a slow rate lowers perfusion.
  85. A bare note of refusal does not show the client was informed. A cost note initialled by the technician, or a veterinarian signing for the client, does not show that the client understood and chose.
  86. The correct answer is A (It triggers strong insulin release from the pancreas, and large doses kill liver cells): In dogs xylitol is rapidly absorbed and is a potent stimulus for insulin secretion, causing glucose to fall within 30 to 60 minutes. Larger doses injure liver cells, causing elevated enzymes and sometimes liver failure. The kidneys are not the main target, osmotic diarrhea is not the mechanism, and glucagon is not involved.
  87. The correct answer is D (Dilute the stock tenfold with saline and give 0.12 mL in the 0.3 mL syringe): A 30 g mouse is 0.03 kg, so 2 mg/kg is 0.06 mg. Stock at 5 mg/mL gives 0.012 mL, too small to draw, so diluting one part stock into nine parts saline gives 0.5 mg/mL and the dose becomes 0.12 mL, which a 0.3 mL insulin syringe can measure. Giving 0.12 mL or 0.1 mL of undiluted stock gives roughly ten to eight times the intended dose.
  88. The correct answer is C (Three times daily, which is about every eight hours): TID stands for three times daily, which is roughly every eight hours. BID means twice daily and tempts because both abbreviations start with the same letter pattern, and a weekly schedule shares the number three but has a different unit. The three in the abbreviation counts doses per day and not hours, and confusing these changes the total daily dose a great deal.

Splints, radiographs and extra oxygen all matter, but each should wait until the dog is hemodynamically stable.

  91. Stress can kill such a cat, so oxygen with minimal handling comes first, and thoracocentesis is both diagnostic and therapeutic. Radiographs, fluids and bloodwork each add stress or risk and can follow stabilization.
  92. The correct answer is B (Enamel, the thin layer that covers the crown above the gum line): Enamel is the hardest tissue in the body, being about 96 percent mineral, and it covers the crown. Dentin lies beneath it and makes up most of the tooth, but it is softer than enamel, and reparative dentin is a form of dentin. Cementum covers the root and is closer to bone in hardness. Enamel cannot regenerate once it is lost.

Acting on the first name given risks the wrong advice. Bringing the dog in is sensible but leaves the product unidentified until arrival, and bait colour is shared across products with different poisons.
  94. Brief, calm restraint with hearing protection for staff is the safest approach. Heavy sedation answers a problem that is not there, releasing at each squeal teaches the pig that fighting works, and a long hold in the corner only deepens the stress.

Cooling continues to drop temperature for several minutes, so a dog cooled past this point can become hypothermic. The technician stops cooling, dries and gently warms the dog, and keeps checking the temperature because rebound hyperthermia and later hypothermia both occur.
  105. The correct answer is A (Too few x-ray photons reached the sensor, so the image is underexposed): Exposure depends on kV, mA, time and distance. With kV and distance unchanged, the shorter time of 0.02 seconds delivers one quarter of the photons of the earlier 0.08 second image, producing a pale, grainy underexposed image. A distance that is too short would overexpose and darken it. Reversing the sensor usually produces a visible grid pattern or very low signal, not a simple pale image.
  106. The correct answer is B (Acquired cheek-tooth malocclusion with spurs; poor intake risks gut stasis and root abscesses): Rabbits have continuously growing cheek teeth. When wear is uneven, sharp spurs form, injure the tongue and cheeks, and cause pain, drooling and reduced hay intake. A rabbit that stops eating is at risk for gastrointestinal stasis, and elongated tooth roots can lead to abscesses.   108. The quadriceps or lumbar epaxial muscles are preferred sites. The femoral nerve lies in the cranial thigh, while the obturator and saphenous nerves lie on the medial side, so none of them sits behind the femur in the caudal muscles.

Multiplying instead of dividing gives a volume four times too large.
  144. The correct answer is C (Explain that diagnosis is the veterinarian's role and offer to relay the question): Diagnosing and prognosis are the veterinarian's responsibilities, so the technician explains this kindly and makes sure the question reaches the doctor, along with the next steps such as aspiration. A guess, even labelled unofficial, is still heard as a diagnosis and may be wrong. Quick reassurance about benign lumps is unreliable, and walking away without acknowledging the concern leaves the client feeling dismissed.
  145. The correct answer is C (Slow collection and delayed transfer let clotting start before mixing with EDTA): A slow difficult draw and delay in transfer let clotting start before mixing. The sample should go into the tube promptly and be inverted gently several times. Hard shaking causes hemolysis, red tops are for serum, and underfilling gives too much EDTA per mL of blood, which shrinks cells.
  146. The correct answer is A (The secondary layer, which absorbs fluid and pads the limb over the contact layer): The primary layer lies against the wound, the secondary layer absorbs exudate and pads, and the tertiary layer holds everything in place and protects it. Each layer has its own function, so the absorbent role belongs to the secondary layer.
  147. The correct answer is A (Place the uncapped needle straight into the sharps container, with no recapping): Recapping with two hands is a leading cause of needlestick injury, so used needles go uncapped into the sharps container immediately. If a cap is needed, a one-handed scoop technique keeps the other hand away from the tip. Bending or hand-recapping creates exposure, and bloodborne agents cannot be judged by how healthy a patient looks.
  148. The correct answer is D (Give a copy of the images in print or digital form, and keep the original): Radiographs are part of the medical record that the clinic must keep, and the client is entitled to a copy to take to another veterinarian. Sending images only to the new clinic ignores the client's own right to a copy, and keeping back the images defeats the request. Deleting the clinic copy would break the record, and the release should be documented in the chart.
  149. The correct answer is C (Drug-induced fibrous overgrowth of the gingiva caused by long-term cyclosporine): Cyclosporine and phenytoin can stimulate gingival fibroblasts and produce firm, generalized overgrowth called gingival hyperplasia. The gums are firm and pink with little plaque and no bleeding, which separates this from inflammatory disease. An epulis is a focal mass, gingivostomatitis ulcerates and bleeds, and periodontitis follows plaque with pocketing and inflammation.
  150. The correct answer is D (Scrub with detergent and rinse, then disinfect for the label contact time and rinse and dry): Disinfectants do not work through organic debris, so detergent scrubbing comes first. The disinfectant must then stay wet for its full label contact time, residue is rinsed off, and the item is dried because damp tubing grows bacteria. Rinsing without scrubbing leaves mucus in the lumen, treating only the outside misses the inside, and a guessed soak with wet storage falls short.
  151. The correct answer is D (Syringe-feed a herbivore critical care diet in small portions, with analgesia): Rabbits in gut stasis need continued, small, frequent assisted feeding of a herbivore critical care diet along with pain relief, since pain suppresses motility. A high fat carnivore diet and a pellet heavy diet both lack the fibre the gut needs. Holding food until the first stool lets stasis deepen even when a prokinetic is given.
  152. The correct answer is A (About 38.3 to 39.2 degrees C, typical of a calm and healthy resting adult dog): Normal canine rectal temperature is roughly 38.3 to 39.2 C. The goat range sits higher and wider and belongs to another species. A value of 39.5 C or above suggests fever or exertion, while 37.0 to 38.0 C is the low range seen when a dog is cooled by anesthesia, not in a calm, resting adult.
  153. The correct answer is D (Keep the cuff inflated and keep the head lower than the body to drain fluid): Ruminants produce large volumes of saliva and can regurgitate under anesthesia, so a cuffed tube with an inflated cuff protects the lower airway from aspiration. The head is kept lower than the body so that saliva and ingesta drain from the mouth. Raising the head or deflating the cuff lets fluid enter the trachea, and a stomach tube does not protect the airway on its own.
  154. The correct answer is D (Apologize and give an honest wait estimate, then offer to wait or reschedule): An apology, a realistic time estimate and a choice respect the client's time and let them decide whether to stay. Saying the doctor will be along shortly is vague and breeds frustration when it proves untrue, and promising ten minutes is a precise guess the clinic cannot meet. Sharing details of the other patient's procedure breaches that client's confidentiality even when the apology is sincere.
  155. The correct answer is B (273 F, found by multiplying 134 by 1.8 and then adding 32): Convert with F = (C x 1.8) + 32 = (134 x 1.8) + 32 = 241.2 + 32 = 273.2, recorded as 273 F. Leaving out the 32 gives 241 F, doubling gives 300 F, and subtracting 32 gives 209 F. Each shortcut gives a log entry that does not match the real cycle temperature.
  156. The correct answer is D (Let the sealed cartridge reach room temperature for the manufacturer's stated time): Cartridge reagents are designed to run at room temperature, and a cold cartridge can give inaccurate results or errors. Leaving the cartridge sealed for the manufacturer's stated time, often 5 to 30 minutes, avoids condensation. Running it cold ignores the problem, water warming risks moisture and uneven heating, and opening the pouch early exposes it to humidity for too short a warm up.
  157. The correct answer is C (Third degree AV block, so alert the veterinarian immediately and keep the dog warm): The tracing shows P waves that do not relate to the slow QRS complexes, which is third degree AV block with a ventricular escape rhythm near 40 per minute. With weak pulses it can drop cardiac output, so the veterinarian must be called at once, and the dog is kept warm while treatment is planned. Warming and waiting delays care, and first degree block would have a regular rate with long PR.
  158. The correct answer is D (Start forced-air warming and warmed fluids, and recheck the temperature every 5 minutes): Anesthesia lowers the set point and blunts shivering, so small patients lose heat steadily and hypothermia slows drug metabolism and recovery. Forced air warming and warmed fluids add heat safely, and frequent temperature checks guide adjustments. Blankets only slow heat loss, a 45 C blanket with hourly checks risks burns, and cutting isoflurane does not address the cause of the temperature trend.
  159. The correct answer is B (Dose-related respiratory depression, bradycardia and reduced gastrointestinal motility): Pure mu agonists depress the respiratory centre, increase vagal tone and slow gut movement, with the size of the effect depending on the dose. Technicians watch respiratory rate, heart rate and defecation for this reason. Tachycardia and tachypnea are not the normal pattern, opioids are known for cardiovascular stability rather than hypertension, and respiratory stimulation is the opposite of what happens.
  160. The correct answer is A (A phenothiazine that blocks dopamine and alpha-1 receptors, giving sedation without analgesia): Acepromazine is a phenothiazine that antagonizes dopamine receptors in the brain and alpha-1 receptors in blood vessels. The result is calm sedation and vasodilation, which can lower the blood pressure, and it gives no analgesia. Alpha-2 agonists, benzodiazepines and ketamine have different receptors and effects. Acepromazine is also used cautiously in some breeds.
  161. The correct answer is B (Keep the dog off heating pads and children from the patch, then fold used patches together): External heat and occlusion increase fentanyl absorption and can cause an overdose, and children can be harmed by contact with or chewing a patch. Used patches still contain drug, so they are folded sticky sides together and disposed of out of reach. Cutting a patch makes the dose unpredictable, and an uncovered discarded patch is a hazard to children and pets.
  162. The correct answer is C (A fecal Giardia antigen ELISA, which finds antigen even when one float misses cysts): Giardia cysts are shed intermittently, so one negative flotation does not rule out infection. A fecal antigen ELISA detects Giardia antigen and is more sensitive than a single float, and zinc sulfate is the preferred flotation medium. Sodium nitrate distorts cysts, trophozoites are found in loose stool not formed stool, and water sedimentation is less effective.
  163. The correct answer is D (Place the rabbit in a quiet, dim enclosure with oxygen and postpone further handling): Rabbits breathe through the nose, so open-mouth breathing is a severe sign, and handling stress can cause fatal collapse. Oxygen in a dim, quiet enclosure treats hypoxia with no restraint. Radiographs, mask restraint and catheterization are all reasonable later, once the rabbit is stable enough to tolerate them.
  164. The correct answer is D (Hold her milk until 96 hours after the last dose, with a 14 day slaughter hold): Milk and meat withdrawal times are separate and both are set by the label. At 60 hours, the 96 hour milk withdrawal has not passed, so the milk must be kept out of the supply for another 36 hours, and the cow cannot go to slaughter for 14 days. Pasteurization does not reliably remove antibiotic residues, and a shorter shared period ignores the label.
  165. The correct answer is A (Ask the prescriber to rewrite the order with a leading zero before giving any drug): An order written as .5 mg/kg with no leading zero can be misread as 5 mg/kg, a tenfold overdose, so the prescriber must rewrite it before anything is drawn up. Giving a dose that seems typical for methadone still rests on a guess about what was meant. Documenting the problem after giving the drug does not undo a possible overdose. Rechecking weight and concentration is sound practice, but the stem says both are already confirmed and the unclear order remains.
  166. The correct answer is A (Third-degree AV block, since atria and ventricles beat independently): Regular P waves at about 140 per minute and regular QRS complexes at about 34 per minute with no fixed relationship mean the atria and ventricles beat independently, which is third-degree AV block. Second-degree block still conducts some P waves with a related PR interval. Flutter produces sawtooth baseline waves and an irregular ventricular response, which the stem does not describe. Sinus bradycardia would show P waves matched one to one with the QRS complexes at the same slow rate.
  167. The correct answer is B (Open-ended: "Tell me what you have noticed about your dog's appetite."): An open-ended request invites the owner to tell the story in their own words, and the owner often volunteers timing, food type and behaviour the technician would not have thought to ask about. A focused question is useful for clarifying later but limits the answer to one detail. A rating scale reduces the history to a single number. An empathy statement supports the owner but does not ask for any information.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Use fine grit paste with light pressure and brief contact of a few seconds per tooth): Polishing smooths the scratches left by scaling, and fine paste with light pressure and a few seconds per tooth does this without removing enamel. Friction creates heat, so long contact can injure the pulp even when the paste and pressure are right. Firm pressure raises friction and removes enamel even in brief passes. A dry cup with no paste has no lubrication, so it heats and scratches the enamel.
  2. The correct answer is C (38.7 to 39.8 degrees C, which is the normal range for a healthy adult pig): The normal rectal temperature of an adult pig is about 38.7 to 39.8 degrees C. Goats run higher, and a sow reading above the pig range would suggest fever. Cattle sit slightly lower, so using their limits would hide a pig that is mildly warm. Horses are lower still, and using that range would call a feverish pig normal.
  3. The correct answer is B (0.075 mL, from a 0.75 mg dose divided by 10 mg/mL stock): Dose = 0.03 mg/kg x 25 kg = 0.75 mg. Volume = 0.75 mg / 10 mg/mL = 0.075 mL, checked as 0.075 x 10 = 0.75. The 0.75 mL value is ten times too high and would overdose the dog. The 0.0075 mL value is ten times too low. The 0.165 mL value uses 55 lb x 0.03 = 1.65 mg and divides by 10.
  4. The correct answer is D (Collimation was too wide, letting extra scatter reach the receptor; close the field): A wide field puts more tissue and table in the beam, and the extra scatter lowers contrast, so closing the collimators to the thorax is the first fix. The question says kVp matched the chart, which removes high kVp as the cause. A short distance changes magnification, not fog. A grid removes scatter and does not add fog.
  5. The correct answer is B (Start ECG monitoring and place IV access so the veterinarian can order sedation and antiarrhythmic drugs): Dark chocolate contains theobromine and caffeine, which cause tremors and tachyarrhythmias. After 5 hours with signs already present, emesis is too late and risks aspiration, and oral charcoal is dangerous in a dog with tremors. ECG monitoring and IV access allow the veterinarian to control seizures and heart rhythm. Sending the dog home is unsafe.
  6. The correct answer is A (600 mL/min, from 30 mL/kg/min multiplied by the 20 kg body weight): Flow equals the protocol rate times body weight: 30 mL/kg/min x 20 kg = 600 mL/min, checked again as 20 x 30 = 600. The 1,320 mL/min value comes from using 44 lb instead of 20 kg. The 200 mL/min value uses a much lower rate than the protocol states. The 2,000 mL/min value belongs to a non-rebreathing circuit, which needs far higher flow because exhaled gas is not recirculated.
  7. The correct answer is C (Rewarm with forced air and oxygen, since shivering sharply raises oxygen demand): A temperature of 35 degrees C is mild to moderate hypothermia. Shivering burns energy and increases oxygen consumption, so oxygen is provided while the dog is rewarmed with an active source that is not in direct contact with the skin. Acepromazine causes vasodilation and worsens heat loss, and a hot pack in contact can burn. The temperature is not normal and needs treatment.
  8. The correct answer is D (Horse and rabbit, since both have hypsodont cheek teeth that keep erupting as they wear down): Horses and rabbits are hypsodont and their cheek teeth erupt throughout life to offset wear, which is why they need abrasive forage and regular dental checks. Dogs and cats are brachydont, with permanent teeth that do not keep erupting, so a dog or cat cannot be one half of this pair.
  9. The correct answer is C (Poorly localized and dull, often with nausea or other autonomic signs): Visceral pain from hollow organs is diffuse, dull and poorly localized, and it is often accompanied by nausea, salivation or heart rate changes because of autonomic involvement. Sharp, well localized pain is somatic. Skin surface pain responds to topical agents, and burning shock-like pain from nerve injury is neuropathic.
  10. The correct answer is B (Connect the exhaust to an active scavenging system and leak test the circuit): Waste gas is captured at the pop-off valve and carried by a scavenging system to the outside, and a leak test of the machine and circuit finds leaks that would let gas into the room. Fans and windows only move the gas around, and a higher flow increases the gas that must be handled. Filling the vaporizer should be done away from the patient, and a cuffed tube lowers leakage during the procedure.
  11. The correct answer is B (The limb was rotated, because the femur was not parallel to the table surface): Condyles that are not superimposed on a mediolateral view show the stifle is rotated, usually because the femur was not parallel to the table. A pad under the hock or foot corrects the position. Flexion changes the joint angle but does not shift one condyle behind the other. Motion produces blur rather than a clean offset, and a kVp error changes density and contrast without altering the geometry.
  12. The correct answer is A (Absorbed drug passes through the portal vein to the liver, where some is metabolized first): The first pass effect describes oral drugs entering the portal circulation and being partly metabolized by the liver before they reach the systemic circulation, which lowers oral bioavailability. Gastric acid and food binding matter for some drugs but do not describe first pass, and kidney excretion happens after systemic distribution.
  13. The correct answer is B (As low as reasonably achievable, such as using aids so nobody holds the patient): ALARA stands for as low as reasonably achievable, so exposure is cut by time, distance, shielding and tools such as sedation, sandbags and troughs so no one holds animals during the exposure. A legal limit is a ceiling, not the aim. Skipping a needed retake gives a non-diagnostic study, and the acronym is not a leave-the-room reminder.
  14. The correct answer is B (4.8 mL, found by multiplying 12 kg by 4 mg/kg and dividing by 10 mg/mL): Dose in mg is 12 kg x 4 mg/kg = 48 mg. Volume is 48 mg / 10 mg/mL = 4.8 mL. Reading 48 mg as 48 mL skips the concentration step, and 1.2 mL uses a quarter of the ordered dose. 2.4 mL would be right only for a 20 mg/mL product, which this vial is not.
  15. The correct answer is D (35 to 45 mmHg, reflecting arterial CO2 in a normally ventilated and stable patient): ETCO2 of 35 to 45 mmHg approximates arterial CO2 in a normal patient. Lower values suggest overventilation or poor perfusion, higher values suggest hypoventilation, and values near zero suggest an airway problem or a disconnection.
  16. The correct answer is D (Prolonged contact with firm pressure at high speed produced frictional heat that threatens the pulp): Twenty seconds on one tooth with firm pressure at high speed generates frictional heat, and the warm tooth afterward shows the pulp is at risk. Polishing should use a few seconds per tooth at slow speed with light pressure. Grit affects how much enamel is removed, but abrasion alone does not explain heat from long contact. Cup flare and retained paste do not account for a warm crown.
  17. The correct answer is A (150 mg, since the bag lasts 12.5 hours and the dog needs 12 mg per hour): Dose per hour = 10 mcg/kg/min x 20 kg x 60 min = 12,000 mcg = 12 mg/hr. The bag runs 500 mL / 40 mL/hr = 12.5 hours, so the bag needs 12 mg x 12.5 = 150 mg, which is 1.5 mL of the 100 mg/mL stock. Adding only 12 mg covers one hour, and 120 mg assumes the wrong run time. The 1,500 mg figure is a tenfold conversion slip.
  18. The correct answer is C (Clip the ventral abdominal hair, apply coupling gel and confirm the fasting time the veterinarian ordered): Hair traps air that blocks the sound beam, so the area is clipped and gel removes the remaining air between the probe and skin. A fast keeps the stomach from filling with food and gas that obscure the liver, so the ordered fasting time should be confirmed. A recent meal adds gas and fluid in the stomach. Bowel purging is not routine for this scan, and sedation is given only when the veterinarian orders it.
  19. The correct answer is A (Ask about atipamezole, because naloxone does not reverse the alpha-2 agonist): Naloxone reverses only opioids, and its effect starts within minutes. The slow heart rate and deep sedation persisting after it point to dexmedetomidine, an alpha-2 agonist, which atipamezole reverses at a dose the veterinarian orders. A tenfold repeat dose does not address the drug involved, flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines that were not used, and waiting leaves a bradycardic patient untreated.
  20. The correct answer is D (Use a grid and raise the mAs to make up for the radiation the grid absorbs): Scatter increases with part thickness, and above about 10 cm a grid removes it to preserve contrast. The grid also absorbs some of the useful beam, so mAs must be raised. Lowering kVp or lengthening distance does not remove scatter enough, and lowering mAs would underexpose the film.
  21. The correct answer is D (Synergism, where the combined effect is greater than the sum of each drug alone): When the combined effect exceeds the sum of the individual effects, the interaction is synergistic. An additive effect would equal the sum, antagonism would reduce the effect, and tolerance describes a diminishing response to repeated doses of a single drug rather than an interaction between two drugs.
  22. The correct answer is B (Dry hands with a sterile towel then don the gown, then glove with the closed technique): After the scrub, hands are dried with a sterile towel, then the gown is put on, and gloves are added last using the closed-glove technique so the cuffs cover the sleeves without bare skin touching the glove surface. Gloving first or drying afterward breaks the order and risks contaminating the gown cuffs or the gloves.
  23. The correct answer is B (Once daily, since healthy puppies should gain weight every day from birth): Healthy neonatal puppies gain weight every day, so a daily weight on the growth chart catches failure to thrive early. Weighing every other day delays recognition of a loss that can become serious within hours. Twice weekly or weekly checks are even slower, so a pup could decline a long time before anyone noticed. Daily weighing is brief and does not add meaningful stress.
  24. The correct answer is A (Luer lock tip, which has a threaded collar that screws the needle hub on): A luer lock syringe has a threaded collar around the tip, so the needle or tubing locks in place with a twist and does not pop off under pressure. Slip tips are smooth and held by friction, catheter tips are long and wide for feeding tubes and irrigation, and eccentric tips are offset to the side of the barrel.
  25. The correct answer is A (A double contrast cystogram, instilling a small volume of dilute iodinated contrast plus air): A small amount of positive contrast pools and coats the mucosa, while air distends the lumen, so masses stand out against both. Dense positive contrast alone can hide a small filling defect. Air alone outlines the lumen but shows little of the wall. An excretory urogram highlights kidneys and ureters first, and bladder detail is variable.
  26. The correct answer is B (Induce in a chamber with oxygen flowing and scavenged exhaust, with continuous observation): Sevoflurane has low blood gas solubility, so induction is quick, and the chamber needs a steady oxygen flow, exhaust connected to a scavenger, and someone watching the animal throughout. Leaving a mouse unobserved risks missing respiratory depression. Room air as the carrier gas can cause hypoxia during induction. Venting waste gas into the room exposes staff, and a scavenging system is available here.
  27. The correct answer is D (Feline panleukopenia virus vaccine, given as part of the FVRCP combination): Core vaccines protect against severe, widespread disease for all cats, and for cats these are panleukopenia, herpesvirus and calicivirus, usually combined as FVRCP, plus rabies where required locally. Vaccines for Bordetella and Chlamydia felis are non-core and given by risk, such as multi-cat housing. The FIV vaccine is not routinely recommended and is not core.
  28. The correct answer is C (Bain for the rabbit and circle for the dog, since tiny breaths struggle with valve resistance): Circle systems have valves and long hoses that add resistance and dead space, which a 3 kg patient with a very small tidal volume cannot overcome. A Bain circuit has little resistance but needs a high fresh gas flow, so it is wasteful in a 40 kg dog, where a circle at low flow conserves gas and heat. The reverse pairing puts the resistance problem on the smaller patient.
  29. The correct answer is B (Repeated C fiber input excites dorsal horn neurons through NMDA receptors, amplifying responses): Wind-up is central sensitization: repeated C fiber stimulation depolarizes dorsal horn neurons through NMDA receptor activation, so later inputs produce larger responses and pain spreads beyond the injury. Lowered thresholds at the injury are peripheral sensitization. Reduced response to opioids is tolerance, and nerve sprouting is a separate peripheral change.
  30. The correct answer is B (Wet fur and an open mouth speed heat loss, so use forced-air warming): Dental patients lose heat through wet fur, evaporation from the open mouth, a cold steel table and long procedure times. Prevention starts early: forced-air warming, towels or a warm pad under the patient, dry fur, and warmed rinse fluid. Raising isoflurane lowers temperature further, and waiting until recovery lets the dog stay cold for the whole procedure, which slows recovery and metabolism of drugs.
  31. The correct answer is A (Reduce the vaporizer setting and check anesthetic depth, then alert the veterinarian): A steady fall in pressure to 70 mmHg on 2.5 percent isoflurane suggests the cat is too deep, since inhalant anesthetics cause dose dependent vasodilation and depress the heart. The first step is to lower the vaporizer, confirm depth, and alert the veterinarian. A canine sized 20 mL/kg bolus is too large for a cat, and drugs need an order.
  32. The correct answer is B (Irrigate the sulcus gently with a blunt cannula and leave it in contact about one minute): Chlorhexidine works by contact and binding, so it must reach the sulcus and stay about a minute before it is rinsed. A blunt cannula places it gently where bacteria remain after scaling. A few seconds of contact is too brief for good binding. High pressure irrigation can push bacteria into the tissue, and leaving the solution in place until recovery risks irritation and swallowing.
  33. The correct answer is D (Feed a measured meal at each injection time and give insulin once the cat eats): Insulin is timed with food so glucose absorption and insulin action overlap. Feeding first confirms the cat will eat before a dose is given. Withholding food or skipping meals invites hypoglycemia, and doubling a dose for a missed meal is dangerous.
  34. The correct answer is C (A partial mu agonist with slow onset, well absorbed by the oral transmucosal route): Buprenorphine binds mu receptors strongly but only partly activates them, and onset takes about 30 minutes. In cats the mouth is slightly alkaline, which lets it absorb well through the buccal mucosa, so a syringe dose placed in the cheek pouch is effective and practical. Swallowing it in food exposes the drug to first pass metabolism, and it is neither a full agonist nor a kappa agonist.
  35. The correct answer is D (Use the pack, since sterility holds until an event such as a tear or moisture occurs): With event-related storage, a pack stays sterile until something compromises it, such as a tear, moisture, a dropped or crushed pack, or a broken seal. This pack is dry and intact with a changed indicator, so it can be used. A fixed 4 week limit belongs to time-related storage, which this clinic does not use. Opening the pack to look inside destroys the sterile barrier, and a spore test checks a sterilizer cycle, not a packed item's age.
  36. The correct answer is B (20 times, from dividing the 10 mL start volume by the 0.5 mL left): The concentration factor is the starting volume divided by the final resuspended volume. Here 10 mL divided by 0.5 mL is 20, so cells and casts are about 20 times more concentrated than in native urine. Subtracting gives 9.5, using the starting volume alone gives 10, and inverting the ratio gives 0.05.
  37. The correct answer is C (25 mL/hr, from multiplying 5 mL/kg/hr by the cat's 5 kg body weight): An mL/kg/hr order is multiplied by body weight: 5 mL/kg/hr x 5 kg = 25 mL/hr. Entering 5 mL/hr ignores the cat's weight and would underhydrate the patient. Using the weight in pounds inflates the rate to 55 mL/hr because the order is written per kilogram. Multiplying by weight twice gives 125 mL/hr, five times the ordered amount, a serious overload for a cat with kidney disease.
  38. The correct answer is C (Tell the veterinarian gentamicin may be damaging the kidneys and request a review before dosing): Gentamicin injures the proximal renal tubules, and a doubled creatinine with casts and low urine output suggests drug related kidney injury. The veterinarian must decide whether to stop or change therapy before another dose is given. Waiting a day to recheck lets more drug reach damaged kidneys. Holding the dose silently is outside the technician's role and leaves the prescriber unaware. Extra fluids support the kidneys but do not make continued dosing safe.
  39. The correct answer is B (Clip against growth with a sharp #40 blade and light pressure, plus a generous margin): A sharp #40 blade used against the direction of growth with light pressure clips close without cutting skin, and a wide margin leaves room to extend the incision. Razors cause micro-abrasions, a narrow margin risks contamination if the incision is extended, and clipping in the surgery room spreads hair into the sterile field.
  40. The correct answer is D (The hose is kinked at the table edge, so straighten it and reroute the hose): Pressure that falls when the folded hose is lifted shows the kink is the obstruction, and the bag test shows the machine side is free. The hose is straightened and routed away from the table edge. Exhausted absorbent raises inspired CO2, not airway pressure, and the cuff or flow would not respond to moving the hose.
  41. The correct answer is D (Sharp enamel points on the upper cheek teeth cut the cheek; floating under sedation fixes it): In a mature horse, the upper cheek teeth grow wider than the lower arcade, leaving sharp buccal enamel points that lacerate the cheek and cause quidding and weight loss. Floating with a mouth speculum and sedation rasps the points smooth. Caps are shed by about 5 years of age, wolf teeth interfere with the bit rather than cause cheek ulcers, and a root abscess would show swelling or a tract rather than cheek ulcers.
  42. The correct answer is C (Mix 20 mL of the antiseptic stock with 180 mL of sterile saline): A 1:10 dilution means one part of stock in ten parts total. For 200 mL that is 200 / 10 = 20 mL of stock, topped up with 180 mL of diluent. Adding 200 mL of saline makes 220 mL at roughly 1:11. Ten milliliters makes a 1:20 mixture, and swapping the volumes produces a very strong solution that could injure wound tissue.
  43. The correct answer is B (Set it aside and use the sterile replacement, then send the dropped scaler for sterilization): An instrument that touches the floor is contaminated and no longer sterile. It is replaced with a sterile one and the dropped instrument is cleaned and sterilized before reuse. Alcohol wipes and chlorhexidine rinses do not sterilize, and using it supragingivally still introduces floor contaminants into the mouth.
  44. The correct answer is B (Species and breed with age, sex and reproductive status): Signalment is the set of identifying characteristics of the patient: species, breed, age, sex and reproductive status, sometimes with colour. Weight, vaccinations and the complaint belong to the history or exam, and diet, housing and travel are lifestyle history. Microchip number, owner contact and appointment reason are administrative details, so they are recorded elsewhere on the form.
  45. The correct answer is D (Shake the bottle well before each dose, because settled particles must be redistributed): In a suspension the drug is not dissolved but is held as fine particles that settle on standing. Shaking before each dose redistributes them so every dose carries the labelled strength. Heating does not dissolve the particles and may degrade the drug, drawing only the top layer gives too little drug, and shaking only once lets the particles settle again.
  46. The correct answer is A (Ask the veterinarian for analgesia now, since fracture pain explains the panting): Panting, whimpering, refusal of food and a high heart rate in a dog with a fresh fracture are pain signs, and with normal effort, colour and SpO2 there is no sign of respiratory disease. Analgesia, usually a pure mu opioid, should be requested promptly. Withholding it only prolongs suffering, oxygen treats a problem he does not have, and food does not address the cause.
  47. The correct answer is B (It blocks sodium and chloride reabsorption in the loop of Henle, raising urine output): Furosemide is a loop diuretic that inhibits the sodium-potassium-chloride transporter in the thick ascending limb of the loop of Henle, so more sodium and water are lost in urine and pulmonary edema clears. Aldosterone blockade is spironolactone, distal tubule action is thiazide, and ADH-like action would reduce urine output.
  48. The correct answer is A (Likely nociception at extraction; report it and ask about a local block or analgesic): Heart rate and systolic pressure were stable through minute 25, and both then rose together by minute 30 while anesthetic depth and fluids were unchanged. That pattern signals a sympathetic response to pain during the extraction. The veterinarian should hear about it promptly so a local block or analgesic can be given. Deep anesthesia and vagal stimulation slow the heart rate, and hypovolemia typically lowers blood pressure.
  49. The correct answer is A (Record findings objectively and report the concern to the veterinarian, not accusing the owner): Fractures of different ages in a young puppy do not fit a single fall, so the technician records objective findings and reports the concern to the veterinarian, who decides on next steps and any reporting under local rules. Telling the owner abuse is suspected can end the animal's care and escalate danger. Going to the police without the veterinarian skips the clinic's process. Photos on a personal phone breach record keeping and privacy practice.
  50. The correct answer is B (It blocks neurokinin-1 receptors, so substance P cannot trigger the vomiting center): Maropitant is a neurokinin-1 receptor antagonist. By blocking substance P, the final common trigger, it acts on both central and peripheral pathways. Ondansetron blocks 5-HT3, metoclopramide blocks D2 and promotes motility, and meclizine is an H1 antihistamine. Each of these is a real antiemetic with a different mechanism.
  51. The correct answer is C (The dose is 180 mg or 0.6 capsule, so ask for 100 mg capsules): The dose is 18 kg x 10 mg/kg = 180 mg. One 300 mg capsule is 16.7 mg/kg and 0.6 of a capsule cannot be measured accurately, so the correct step is to request a smaller strength such as 100 mg capsules, where two capsules (200 mg, about 11 mg/kg) land within about 10 percent of the ordered dose. Splitting a capsule to 150 mg is 17 percent under the ordered dose and the contents cannot be divided accurately. Two 300 mg capsules is 600 mg, over 33 mg/kg, and 5.4 capsules comes from using the capsule strength in place of the dose.
  52. The correct answer is A (30 kg, found by dividing 66 lb by 2.2 lb per kilogram): One kilogram equals 2.2 lb, so pounds are divided by 2.2: 66 / 2.2 = 30 kg. Dividing by 2 gives 33 kg, which overstates the weight by 10 percent. Using 1.8, the factor from temperature conversion, gives about 37 kg and a larger overdose. Multiplying by a rounded 0.4 gives 26 kg and would underdose the dog.
  53. The correct answer is B (Fluid overload from a rate too high; slow or stop it and notify the veterinarian): Weight gain, peripheral and facial edema, serous nasal discharge and a rising respiratory rate over a day of infusion indicate volume overload. The infusion is slowed or stopped and the veterinarian is told, since pulmonary edema may follow. Anaphylaxis appears quickly after starting a fluid, dehydration would show weight loss, and phlebitis is limited to the catheter site.
  54. The correct answer is C (Give calcium gluconate slowly IV with ECG monitoring and start fluids before induction): A potassium of 8.2 mmol/L with peaked T waves and a falling heart rate is life-threatening and makes anesthesia risky. Calcium gluconate protects the myocardium from the effect of potassium within minutes, and fluids start correction while the ECG is watched. Anesthetic drugs depress the heart and can cause arrest if given first. Dextrose alone does not shift potassium without insulin, and delaying treatment is unsafe.
  55. The correct answer is C (The 0.12 mL given equals 1.2 mg or 0.1 mg/kg, twice the ordered 0.05 mg/kg dose): The order of 0.05 mg/kg for 12 kg is 0.6 mg, and at 10 mg/mL that is 0.06 mL. The record shows 0.12 mL, which delivers 1.2 mg or 0.1 mg/kg, double the ordered dose. The 0.01 mL per kg figure assumes a different concentration, and comparing mL with mg is not valid. The technician should report it so the patient is monitored.
  56. Only visible excess is wiped from the outer ear. Massaging before the drops are in spreads nothing. A cotton swab in the canal packs debris and risks the drum. Holding the ear upright without massage leaves drops in the vertical canal.
  57.
