Here the dog does not react to palpation of the incision and vital signs are normal, which argues against breakthrough pain. The veterinarian can order a sedative or partial reversal. More hydromorphone would worsen the picture, and bladder or glucose causes have no supporting findings in the case.
  58. The correct answer is B (Within the premedication injection, given well before the first skin incision): Pre-emptive analgesia means the drug is on board before surgical stimulation begins, which blunts the nociceptive barrage that drives wind-up. Giving the opioid with the premedication does this and also lowers the anesthetic requirement. Dosing at closure, extubation or after recovery all treat pain that has already been established, which is harder to control and needs more drug.
  59. Manual ventilation for true hypoxemia is not supported by the normal findings. Lowering the isoflurane does not address a probe problem.
  60. The correct answer is C (A Giardia antigen test on fresh feces, because flotation can miss intermittently shed cysts): Giardia cysts are shed intermittently and are easy to miss on flotation, so an antigen test on fresh feces is the logical next step. Repeating the same method on the same sample adds little, parvovirus is an acute disease of unvaccinated animals, and heartworm does not cause diarrhea.
  61. The correct answer is C (Tell the team and step back so the gown and gloves can be changed): A gown sleeve that touches a non sterile surface is contaminated, and the safe response is to say so right away and re-gown and re-glove with help from the circulator. Alcohol does not restore sterility to fabric, and a towel only hides the problem. Brief contact or a nearly finished case does not make the sleeve sterile again.
  62. The correct answer is B (Atrial fibrillation, with absent P waves and an irregularly irregular response): A fast, irregular rhythm with no P waves and a pulse deficit is atrial fibrillation, because many beats are too weak to produce a palpable pulse. Sinus tachycardia is regular with P waves, flutter is regular, and third-degree block produces a slow ventricular rate.
  63. With the cuff deflated, fluid and debris enter the airway and trigger coughing. Tube trauma and early extubation can cause coughing, but the stem gives no sign of either.   64. Holding the stone about 100 to 110 degrees to the face and stroking the lateral surface restores that edge without changing the blade form. A steep or perpendicular stone alters the edge angle, and flat rubbing on the face thins the blade without sharpening.
  65. The correct answer is A (Citrate first then the serum tube, and finally the EDTA tube): The usual order is citrate, then serum, then heparin, then EDTA, then fluoride. Citrate comes before serum so clot activator does not contaminate the coagulation sample, and EDTA goes late because carryover raises potassium and chelates calcium and magnesium, ruining the serum chemistry results.
  66. The correct answer is B (0.3, found by dividing the urine protein by the urine creatinine concentration): The urine protein to creatinine ratio is protein divided by creatinine, both in the same units: 45 / 150 = 0.3. Because creatinine is concentrated to a similar degree as protein, the ratio corrects for how dilute or concentrated the urine is, which a dipstick cannot. A result of 0.3 is below the usual 0.5 concern value for dogs. Inverting, subtracting or multiplying gives numbers with no clinical meaning.
  67.   68.   69. The correct answer is D (Intestine or other delicate soft tissue, where a taper point parts fibers without cutting): A taper point needle has a round body that narrows to a smooth point with no cutting edges, so it parts fibers in delicate tissue such as intestine without tearing. Skin, footpad and scar tissue are tough and need a cutting needle with a sharp edge to pass through. Cartilage also resists a taper point. Cutting edges in delicate tissue would tear it, which is why the taper point is chosen there.
  70. A band with no finger room can restrict breathing, a short cone lets the dog reach the leg, and a loose band lets the dog back out of it.

Giving a quarter dose and reassessing prevents overload if the patient responds. Half of the dose or the full dose gives far too much at once, and 5 mL/kg is too small to treat shock.
  72. The correct answer is C (An opioid such as buprenorphine as ordered, avoiding NSAIDs until kidney function and hydration recover): Obstructed cats are in pain and need analgesia alongside fluids and treatment of hyperkalemia, and an opioid is the usual choice.   73. The correct answer is B (Keeping it below its preferred temperature range slowed metabolism and drug clearance): Reptiles depend on external heat, and below their preferred temperature range metabolism, circulation and drug clearance slow dramatically, so recovery is prolonged. A cool room and a metal table with no heat source cause this. Positioning and dehydration can add problems, but nothing in the stem points to them. Fasting is routine for many reptiles and does not explain a two hour delay.

  75. The correct answer is A (Dorsoventral, because the lungs stay aerated and the heart rests against the sternum): In sternal recumbency the lungs inflate fully and the heart stays near the sternum, which gives the standard appearance of the cardiac silhouette and lung fields. In dorsal recumbency the abdominal organs press on the diaphragm, the lungs are less inflated and the heart shifts. Ease of holding is not the reason, and the beam crosses the same thickness either way.

  79. Saying communal returns mixed ashes misstates what communal means. Cremating several pets in one chamber with ashes returned is a partitioned service, not private. The dose is 0.01 mg/kg x 10 kg = 0.1 mg, so 0.1 mL is drawn up. Treating the product as 0.1 mg/mL gives a tenfold overdose, while the other two misreadings give a dose far too small or a volume far too large.

No part of the chart is public, including the vaccination page. Telling the client how the dog is doing also discloses patient information without consent. A promise from the person asking does not replace the owner's permission, so a courteous refusal is correct.
  84. It must be copied from the actual vial or carton, not estimated from invoices. Manufacturer name, dose volume and route are useful record details but do not identify a batch.

Staying inside 2 SD does not make a shift acceptable. Random error shows scatter on both sides, and imprecision would widen the spread rather than move it all one way.

Boiling water reaches only 100 degrees C and does not kill spores. The 134 degree cycle needs higher pressure of roughly 30 psi and a shorter exposure of about 3 to 4 minutes, so mixing parameters from the two cycles produces a cycle that is not valid.

  98. The dorsal pedal artery is a real pulse site but a smaller vessel on the lower limb, not the femoral. Pressing firmly with the whole palm over the thigh collapses the vessel and hides the pulse.   100. Bathing with lukewarm water and mild detergent removes the product, while temperature and seizure activity are monitored for the veterinarian, who will direct drugs such as methocarbamol.   103.   104. The correct answer is C (Stand packs on edge with gaps between them, and tilt or invert the basin and jar): Steam must displace air and reach every surface. Packs stood on edge with space between them let air escape and condensate drain, and hollow containers are tilted or inverted for the same reason. Stacking or crowding blocks steam, upright containers trap air and water, and pressing wrapped surfaces together prevents penetration and leaves packs wet.

  109. Dust or debris blocks the light that exposes the film, so the spot appears white, and cleaning the screen removes it.   121. The correct answer is B (Put each in its own labeled formalin container, filled to about ten times tissue volume): Each specimen goes in its own labeled container with the patient, site and date, and the fixative volume should be roughly ten times the tissue volume so formalin penetrates fully. Pooling samples prevents the pathologist from knowing which tissue is which. Too little formalin leads to poor fixation, and freezing causes ice crystal damage that ruins histology.
  122. The correct answer is C (-ectomy, meaning surgical removal of all or part of a structure): The suffix -ectomy means excision, as in ovariohysterectomy or splenectomy. Each of the other endings is a real surgical suffix, but each describes a different action. The suffix -otomy means cutting into a structure without removing it, -ostomy means creating an opening, and -plasty means repair or reshaping. Knowing the suffix helps the technician predict which instruments and supplies a procedure needs.
  123. The correct answer is D (Decompensatory shock, where failed compensation causes bradycardia with hypotension and poor perfusion): The stages move from compensatory (fast heart rate, bright gums, bounding pulses) to early decompensatory (pale gums, longer refill, weak pulses, still tachycardic) to decompensatory. In the last stage the heart rate falls, gums turn gray, temperature drops and pressure collapses because compensation has failed. This dog's bradycardia and gray gums put it in that final stage.
  124. The correct answer is B (A-delta fibers, which are thinly myelinated and conduct quickly): A-delta fibers are thinly myelinated and conduct quickly, producing the sharp first pain that triggers the withdrawal reflex. C fibers are unmyelinated and slow, and they carry the dull, aching second pain. A-beta fibers carry touch and A-alpha fibers serve motor and proprioceptive function, so neither carries nociception.
  125. The correct answer is B (Ethylene oxide is absorbed by porous items and is toxic, so aeration clears residual gas): Ethylene oxide is toxic, irritating, and carcinogenic, and it is absorbed by plastics and rubber, so residue can injure tissue if not allowed to dissipate. Aeration removes it before use. The step is not for cooling or drying, as the process is low temperature and has no steam condensate, and it has no connection with incubating a biological indicator.
  126. The correct answer is A (Isolate in a separate room with dedicated gown, gloves and boots and a peroxygen disinfectant): Parvovirus is a nonenveloped virus that survives for months in the environment and is shed in large amounts in feces. It needs full separation, dedicated protective gear and a peroxygen or bleach disinfectant with adequate contact time. Alcohol does not inactivate it, quaternary ammonium products are unreliable against it, and soap removes dirt but does not inactivate the virus.
  127. The correct answer is A (Roll the swab gently on a clean slide, then air dry and stain it): Rolling the swab keeps cells and organisms intact, and air drying preserves them for staining. Rubbing ruptures cells, flushing removes the organisms the veterinarian wants to see, and a flame can distort morphology.
  128. The correct answer is C (Underexposure from a thin-patient technique; reset kVp and mAs for 24 cm): Quantum mottle comes from too few photons reaching the receptor, and a light, grainy image on a thick dog matches a thin-patient technique. The fix is to use the chart row for 24 cm. Motion produces blur, not grain, and overexposure would look dark. Removing the grid lowers scatter control, and repeating the same technique still gives too few photons.
  129. The correct answer is A (Draw 100 mL of 50% dextrose and add 400 mL of compatible fluid): C1V1 = C2V2 gives 50% x V1 = 10% x 500 mL, so V1 = 100 mL of stock. The diluent is 500 - 100 = 400 mL. Adding 500 mL of diluent makes 600 mL of an 8.3% solution, a 200 mL stock draw makes a 20% solution, and drawing 500 mL of stock inverts the ratio and makes a very hypertonic solution.
  130. The correct answer is C (Shared areas became contaminated, so use a covered cart and the shortest route then disinfect): Parvovirus is shed in feces and persists on floors and hands, so every shared area the patient crosses becomes a hazard to unvaccinated animals. Infectious patients should be moved by the shortest route, on a cart or in a carrier, by gowned staff, and surfaces they touch are disinfected afterward. Paw washing and footbaths do not remove virus from a hallway.
  131. The correct answer is D (Used instruments were not cleaned and sterilized, so organisms pass between patients): Instruments must be cleaned, inspected, packaged and sterilized between patients, because wiping does not remove or kill organisms and the set would carry them to the next patient. Labelling is a documentation matter and not the main error. Moisture and lint from damp gauze are real minor concerns, but they are secondary to the transfer of organisms between patients. A set that looks clean is still not sterile.
  132. The correct answer is A (Notify the veterinarian of the liver changes and low albumin before anesthesia): A markedly raised ALT indicates hepatocellular injury and low albumin suggests reduced hepatic synthetic function, both of which change drug metabolism and clotting. Low albumin means more free drug, so doses often need to be lower, not higher. The veterinarian must review the panel before premedication. A bright appearance does not rule out liver disease, and ALT comes from the liver, not red cells.
  133. The correct answer is D (Respiratory acidosis from hypoventilation; alert the veterinarian and support breathing): The pH is low with a high pCO2 and a normal bicarbonate and lactate, which is respiratory acidosis from inadequate ventilation, most likely from sedation. The patient needs airway support, oxygen and help with ventilation, and the veterinarian must be told now. Waiting an hour to recheck delays care for a dog that is not intubated. Bicarbonate does not correct a carbon dioxide problem, and a metabolic cause would show a low bicarbonate.
  134. The correct answer is B (Place a smoke evacuator nozzle near the tip and provide suitable respirators): Surgical smoke carries fine particles, chemicals and possibly viral material, so the hazard should be controlled at the source with a smoke evacuator close to the tip, and staff should be given suitable respiratory protection as part of the employer's hazard controls. Opening a door only spreads the plume, higher power creates more smoke, and ordinary surgical masks do not seal against fine particles however many are worn.
  135. The correct answer is C (Drug names with amounts and concentration plus pump rate and the date, time and initials): A CRI bag may be handled by several staff over many hours, so the label must let anyone confirm what is in it and how it should run. That means the drugs and amounts, the final concentration, the set rate, when it was made and who made it. Each shorter label leaves out something a colleague needs to catch a mix-up, and a pump screen or treatment sheet can be wrong or out of reach.
  136. The correct answer is C (Keep monitoring, since reflex bradycardia with a good pressure is expected here): Alpha-2 agonists raise vascular tone, and the baroreceptor reflex then slows the heart, so a low rate with good pressure and perfusion is an expected effect. Anticholinergics given on top of an alpha-2 agonist can cause hypertension and arrhythmias. Naloxone reverses opioids rather than alpha-2 agonists, and a fluid bolus does not address the cause. Atipamezole would be the reversal if the veterinarian decided intervention was needed.
  137. The correct answer is A (Give 100 percent oxygen by mask, alert the veterinarian and prepare to reintubate): Laryngospasm closes the glottis and can cause rapid hypoxemia, especially in cats. The first step is to deliver oxygen and get the veterinarian, with equipment ready to re-induce and re-intubate if the spasm persists. Waiting is dangerous with falling saturation. Sedation can help later under veterinary direction but oxygen comes first. Forced pressure rarely opens the larynx and fills the stomach with gas.
  138. The correct answer is C (The exact product name and active ingredient, plus amount used and first application date): The owner has linked the lesions to a product, so its exact name, active ingredient, amount and timing let the veterinarian judge contact irritation, an allergic reaction or toxicity, and the container can be checked. Grooming, vaccine status and diet are part of a full dermatology history but do not address the suspected cause. Specific product details also guide whether other pets in the home need attention.
  139. The correct answer is D (Falling output with a rising heart rate suggests hypovolemia, so notify the veterinarian): The output falls from 1.8 to 0.3 mL/kg/hr, well under the 1 mL/kg/hr target, while heart rate climbs. Together they point to poor perfusion from volume loss or ongoing losses such as third spacing. The trend, not a single value, drives the call to the veterinarian. A full bag or pain could add to the picture but do not account for both changes.
  140. The correct answer is B (3.5 mg/dL, from dividing the table creatinine by 88.4): The table lists creatinine in umol/L. Dividing 310 by 88.4 gives 3.5 mg/dL, markedly above the usual canine interval of about 44 to 133 umol/L. Multiplying gives an impossible value, and shifting the decimal by a power of ten misses the true factor.
  141. The correct answer is C (Grade 2, because calculus covers between a quarter and a half of the visible crown): The index measures how much of the crown surface is covered. When calculus covers a band that reaches roughly 40 percent of the crown, the score is grade 2. A marginal band alone does not equal full coverage, so grade 3 overstates it. Grade 1 understates a deposit that reaches well toward the tip. Gingival redness is a separate finding and does not replace the calculus score.
  142. The correct answer is C (It rates muscle over the spine, skull and pelvis separate from fat cover): The body condition score judges fat cover, while the muscle condition score is judged by palpating muscle over the spine, skull, scapulae and wings of the ilia. Seniors can lose muscle while carrying fat, so a plump cat can still be wasted. The muscle score is not a weight formula and does not use the 9 point body scale. The ribs and abdomen are the sites for judging fat, not muscle.
  143. The correct answer is D (Ketamine, an injectable anesthetic controlled under the Controlled Drugs and Substances Act): Ketamine is listed under the Controlled Drugs and Substances Act, so it is locked up and every use is logged. Propofol, dexmedetomidine and acepromazine are used in anesthesia but are not controlled substances under that Act, so they are stored with ordinary prescription drugs. Clinic policy may add extra security, but the legal requirement applies to ketamine.
  144. The correct answer is D (Acute febrile reaction, so stop the unit and keep the line patent while alerting the veterinarian): The temperature rose from 38.5 to 40.0 C, a 1.5 C rise, with a higher heart rate and vomiting at 30 minutes, which fits an acute transfusion reaction. The transfusion is stopped at once, the line is kept patent with saline, and the veterinarian is told. Slowing and continuing risks a worse reaction, and overload would show respiratory signs rather than fever.
  145. The correct answer is A (Caudal ventral abdomen slightly off midline, with the bladder steadied and the needle entering it): The bladder lies in the caudal ventral abdomen, so cystocentesis is done there, slightly off the midline, with the bladder isolated by one hand and the needle passed into its wall. This gives a sterile sample for culture. A urethral catheter is a different collection method and not a needle site. The inguinal canal holds vessels and no bladder, and the pubic bone blocks a needle placed directly over it.
  146. The correct answer is C (Recount the vial with a second staff member and compare it with every logged entry): A discrepancy in a controlled drug count is investigated at once, starting with a recount witnessed by a second person and a check of each log entry for arithmetic or recording errors. The log is never altered to match, the remaining drug is not wasted before the investigation, and waiting for a monthly audit leaves a possible loss unaddressed. If it is unresolved, the veterinarian is informed.
  147. The correct answer is D (Vitamin C deficiency, since guinea pigs cannot make it and old pellets lose potency): Guinea pigs lack the enzyme to make vitamin C and need a daily dietary source, and the vitamin degrades in old stored pellets. Deficiency causes bleeding gums, swollen painful joints, a rough coat, and reluctance to move. Vitamin D, calcium, and vitamin E problems do not produce hemorrhagic gums with joint swelling together.
  148. The correct answer is D (The circulator fetches it and opens the package so the scrub technician lifts it out): The scrub technician stays sterile at the table, while the circulator is non sterile and moves around the room. The circulator opens the outer wrapper without touching the contents, and the scrub technician lifts the inner item out. A scrubbed person leaving the field or a non sterile person touching the inner packet breaks the sterile chain.
  149. The correct answer is B (Blocking COX-1 lowers protective prostaglandins that maintain gastric mucus and flow): COX-1 is constitutively expressed and produces prostaglandins that sustain gastric mucus, bicarbonate and mucosal blood flow, so inhibiting it removes that protection and invites ulcers. COX-2 is induced mainly at sites of inflammation, so blocking it mostly reduces pain and swelling. The histamine and wall-thickening ideas are not how NSAIDs act.
  150. The correct answer is A (Decrease the vertical angle so the beam is perpendicular to the bisecting line): Aiming the beam nearly perpendicular to the sensor, rather than perpendicular to the bisector of the angle between tooth and sensor, foreshortens the tooth. Lowering the vertical angle so the beam is perpendicular to that bisecting line restores true length. A steeper angle repeats the error, a horizontal move changes overlap rather than length, and exposure time affects density only.
  151. The correct answer is C (Turn to lateral recumbency with the muzzle tilted slightly down, and confirm the cuff is inflated): Dental water and debris pool at the back of the mouth. Lateral recumbency with the muzzle slightly down lets fluid run out of the mouth, and an inflated cuff seals the trachea. A dorsal, level-head position lets fluid collect at the pharynx and a deflated cuff allows it to pass the tube. A raised muzzle drains fluid toward the larynx. A gauze pack can supplement a cuff but does not replace one.
  152. The correct answer is B (Keep the tube in until swallowing, then monitor sternal with head extended): Brachycephalic airways collapse easily when the dog relaxes, so the tube stays until the dog is swallowing and holding its head up. Then it is placed sternal with the head and neck extended and watched closely. Early extubation, sedation and a flexed neck all raise the risk of obstruction.
  153. The correct answer is C (1 to 2 mL/kg/hr, which reflects normal renal perfusion and hydration): Adequate urine production is generally 1 to 2 mL/kg/hr. Below about 0.5 mL/kg/hr suggests oliguria, so 0.25 to 0.5 is too low to be a target. A range of 4 to 6 mL/kg/hr is polyuric, and 10 to 20 mL/kg/hr is a fluid rate and not an output.
  154. The correct answer is B (Half a tablet, since 12.5 mg is needed and each tablet holds 25 mg exactly): Dose = 1.25 mg/kg x 10 kg = 12.5 mg. Tablets = 12.5 mg / 25 mg per tablet = 0.5 tablet. The weight is already in kilograms, so multiplying by 2.2 gives 27.5 mg and about 1.1 tablets, which overdoses the dog. Inverting the division gives 2 tablets, and skipping the weight multiplication gives 1.25 / 25 = 0.05 tablet.
  155. The correct answer is B (An engineering control, a scavenging system carrying waste gas from the circuit outdoors): Engineering controls that remove the hazard at the source rank above administrative controls and PPE, so an active or passive scavenging system that carries waste gas outdoors is the best choice. A paper mask does not stop gas, signs depend on behaviour, and an open window gives uncontrolled airflow.
  156. The correct answer is D (The charcoal has adsorbed waste gas to its limit, so replace the canister before reuse): A charcoal canister gains weight as it adsorbs halogenated gas, and a gain over the labelled limit means it is saturated and must be replaced to prevent waste gas escaping. The CO2 absorber is a separate component, charcoal cannot be dried and reused, and delaying leaves staff exposed.
  157. The correct answer is A (A catheter gives secure venous access so the solution reaches the vein fast): The euthanasia solution is given rapidly into a vein, and a catheter keeps access secure in a cat with poor veins and prevents perivascular leakage, which can be painful. The solution is a rapid bolus, not a slow drip. A catheter does not replace sedation, which is a separate drug choice, and its purpose here is reliable delivery rather than rehydration.
  158. The correct answer is D (The dependent lung partly collapses, hiding nodules unless that lung is uppermost on the other view): In lateral recumbency the lung closest to the table loses air from its own weight, so its nodules lose contrast against soft tissue. Turning the dog puts the other lung down and the first lung back up with full inflation. Magnification of the upper lung is true but is not why two laterals are taken. The vertebral heart score uses one lateral, and each lateral shows all lung lobes.
  159. The correct answer is D (The doubled exposure time gave too much radiation, so the film turned dark): Overexposure sends too much radiation to the film, which then develops dark with reduced contrast. The passed light test, fresh solutions and correct developer temperature and time rule out fogging and processing problems. High kilovoltage can lower contrast, but the stated error was the doubled exposure time. A safelight fault was cleared by the light test, and overdevelopment is ruled out by the correct time.
  160. The correct answer is A (Alert the veterinarian first, since barium may be aspirated and another contrast may be needed): Inhaled barium can cause severe aspiration pneumonia, and a vomiting patient may regurgitate and inhale the dose. The veterinarian decides if another agent, a tube or a different study is safer. Diluting barium degrades the study, a rapid dose raises aspiration risk, and giving a drug without an order is outside the technician's role.
  161. The correct answer is B (Pad evenly with cotton, then wrap at moderate tension working up from the hoof): A lower limb bandage uses a padded layer under an elastic wrap, applied evenly from distal to proximal at moderate tension, with padding extending beyond the wrap ends. Excess tension can cause tissue necrosis, proximal-to-distal wrapping pushes swelling downward, and exposed elastic edges rub the skin.
  162. The correct answer is D (The urethral opening on the vestibular floor, a small papilla at the vestibulovaginal junction): The urethral orifice opens on the ventral floor of the vestibule, at the vestibulovaginal junction, and the catheter is directed toward it. The dorsal wall leads to the vagina, the clitoral fossa is a blind depression that can trap the tip, and the cervix is cranial and unrelated to urine collection.
  163. The correct answer is D (Serotonin syndrome from tramadol with fluoxetine; stop the tramadol and bring the dog in promptly): Tramadol has serotonergic activity, and combining it with an SSRI such as fluoxetine can produce serotonin syndrome with agitation, tremor, diarrhea and vocalizing. This is an emergency, so the tramadol is held and the dog is seen at once. Opioid sedation would not cause agitation and tremor, a dose increase would worsen the syndrome, and a home sugar remedy would only delay care.
  164. The correct answer is A (Put a towel between cat and blanket, and recheck rectal temperature often): Hypothermic patients have poor skin perfusion and reduced pain response, so heat sources need a barrier and frequent temperature checks to avoid burns and overshoot. Direct high heat can burn, wet immersion costs heat through evaporation, and shivering is a sign of rewarming demand, not recovery.
  165. The correct answer is B (Lower the isoflurane since dexmedetomidine reduces anesthetic needs, and tell the veterinarian): Dexmedetomidine lowers the inhalant requirement, and 2 percent isoflurane is probably too deep: eyes centred and flaccid jaw tone fit that. Lowering the vaporizer is the quickest correction. The bradycardia is an expected alpha-2 effect, and atropine can cause hypertension and arrhythmia. A fluid bolus does not address depth. Atipamezole during surgery abruptly removes analgesia and is a decision for the veterinarian.
  166. The correct answer is A (Remove it from stock as nonsterile, then rewrap and resterilize the contents with fresh wrap): A wet or damp wrap lets moisture carry bacteria through the fabric, so the barrier is compromised even if the tape changed color, since tape only shows steam exposure. The pack must be pulled and reprocessed. Drying on the shelf, wiping the outside, or using it for a clean case all assume the contents stayed sterile, which cannot be shown.
  167. The correct answer is C (It vents excess gas from the breathing circuit into the scavenging system, not the room): The adjustable pressure-limiting or pop-off valve releases excess gas from the circuit, and when it is connected to a scavenging system the gas is carried out of the room. It does not remove carbon dioxide, which is the soda lime canister's job, or filter vapour, which a charcoal canister does. It also has no control over fresh gas flow, which is set at the flowmeter.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Listen without interrupting and acknowledge the frustration, then offer to review the estimate): Letting the client finish and naming the frustration lowers tension, and offering to walk through the estimate and bring in the practice manager gives a path forward. Defending the bill before the client is heard usually raises the volume. A discount offer is not within a technician's authority, and pushing the issue to email makes the client feel ignored.
  2. The correct answer is A (Advise the owner to bring the cat in tonight, and notify the veterinarian of the report): One brief episode can follow anesthesia, but repeated vomiting with lethargy, hiding, and refusing water suggests a complication such as pain, ileus, or internal bleeding. The cat needs prompt examination and the veterinarian must be told. Prolonged fasting in cats risks hepatic lipidosis, human drugs are unsafe, and calling it normal delays care.
  3. The correct answer is C (Recognize a methimazole adverse reaction, stop dosing and notify the veterinarian): Facial excoriation and low platelets are recognized serious adverse effects of methimazole, usually in the first weeks of therapy. Dosing is held and the veterinarian is told so the cat can be examined and treated. Halving the dose is a veterinary decision and keeps the drug going. A collar does not address the cause, and waiting for a later recheck risks bleeding from thrombocytopenia.
  4. The correct answer is D (Alert the veterinarian since fever raises absorption, and prepare naloxone for overdose): Fever raises skin blood flow and speeds fentanyl absorption, so this sleepy, bradycardic dog may be overdosing. The veterinarian must be told immediately, with respiratory monitoring and naloxone available, and patch removal is ordered by the veterinarian. A second patch worsens the overdose. Ice delays review of a possible emergency, and patch release does change with heat.
  5. The correct answer is C (The 1 mL tuberculin syringe, which has fine 0.01 mL graduations for small volumes): Accuracy depends on graduation spacing. A 1 mL tuberculin syringe is marked in 0.01 mL steps, so 0.15 mL can be read exactly. A 3 mL syringe is marked in 0.1 mL steps, so 0.15 mL has to be estimated between marks. A 12 mL syringe is marked in far coarser steps and a larger barrel is less accurate for small volumes, and the usual size is not the most accurate one.
  6. The correct answer is D (Squeeze a skin fold and scrape deeply with oil until capillary oozing appears): Demodex mites live deep in hair follicles, so the skin must be squeezed to push them out and scraped until capillary blood appears. A light dry scrape lifts only surface scale and misses them. Light pinnal margin scrapes suit Sarcoptes, which burrow superficially. Surface tape collections also miss follicular mites.
  7. The correct answer is C (Forced-air warming with a towel layer under the cat, since direct contact can burn): Hypothermic patients are rewarmed with an active, controlled source such as forced air, with a layer of toweling between the patient and any conductive pad. The hazard of warming is thermal burn, because anesthetized or sedated animals cannot move away and have reduced skin perfusion. Hot water bottles in direct contact and microwaved towels give uneven heat. A cotton blanket alone retains heat but does not add enough for a cat at 35.8 degrees C.
  8. The correct answer is B (Treat as nonsterile and recall packs, check settings and retest with a new biological indicator): Chemical tape only shows exposure to heat or steam, while the biological indicator tests whether spores were killed, so growth signals a sterilization failure. The load is quarantined and recalled, the sterilizer function and loading are checked, and the test is repeated. Relying on tape, waiting to use packs, or changing tape brands leaves possibly unsterile packs in use.
  9. The correct answer is D (A periapical abscess of the maxillary fourth premolar root draining through the skin below the eye): The upper carnassial has three roots close to the face, so a root abscess can track through bone and skin just below the eye. The fractured crown, calculus and pain on chewing point to the tooth as the source. A salivary mucocele would be fluctuant and lack tooth disease, and dacryocystitis or nasal tumours do not typically show a fractured carnassial. Dental radiographs and extraction or root canal treatment are needed.
  10. The correct answer is C (Sterilization destroys all microbes including spores; disinfection lowers pathogens but may leave spores): Sterilization eliminates every form of microbial life, including bacterial spores, and is required for items that enter sterile tissue. Disinfection reduces the number of pathogens on inanimate items to a safe level but may not kill resistant spores. Defining sterilization without spores leaves out its key feature, and visible dirt removal is cleaning. Steam under pressure is a sterilizing method, and chemical soaks usually only disinfect.
  11. The correct answer is D (Zinc sulfate, since its density floats cysts and preserves their shape): Zinc sulfate at a specific gravity near 1.18 floats Giardia cysts while keeping them recognizable. Sodium nitrate is a good routine solution for most ova but its osmotic effect collapses Giardia cysts, so they are missed or misread. Plain water has too little density to float cysts and distorts them.
  12. The correct answer is B (Pain from the hoof injury; request veterinary-directed analgesia and reassess lameness after treatment): Refusing to bear weight, lying more and losing interest in feed are pain behaviours in pigs, and a lameness grade of 4 of 5 is severe. The veterinarian should be asked about analgesia and the response reassessed using the same lameness score. Giving leftover drugs without an order is outside the technician's role and may be the wrong dose. Changing the feed ignores the injury, and observation alone leaves a painful animal untreated.
  13. The correct answer is B (Little or no fasting, since rabbits cannot vomit and fasting risks gut stasis): Rabbits cannot vomit, so there is little aspiration risk from a full stomach, while fasting can stall the gut and lead to stasis. Most rabbits are given little or no fast and are offered hay and water until close to the procedure. Dog-style fasting and a 24 hour fast both risk gut stasis, and a large meal just before anesthesia is not recommended either.
  14. The correct answer is C (3.36 x10^9/L, a regenerative left shift because mature neutrophils still outnumber bands): Absolute bands are 24.0 x 0.14 = 3.36 x10^9/L, far above the usual 0 to 0.3. Segmented cells are 24.0 x 0.70 = 16.8 x10^9/L, so mature neutrophils outnumber bands, which defines a regenerative left shift. A degenerative shift needs bands to equal or exceed segmented cells, usually with leukopenia. Toxic change alone does not set that category.
  15. The correct answer is D (Active inflammation, since fibrinogen is high while PCV and total protein stay normal): Fibrinogen is an acute phase protein that rises with inflammation in horses, and a value of 8 g/L is well above the range of 2 to 4 g/L. A PCV of 0.35 L/L and a total protein of 70 g/L are normal, which argues against dehydration or protein loss. The swollen limb supports an active inflammatory process.
  16. The correct answer is A (Alert the veterinarian at once and prepare a stomach tube and anti-bloat product): A distended left abdomen after clover points to frothy rumen bloat, which can kill within hours by pressing on the diaphragm. The veterinarian is called immediately and equipment is prepared, with the front end kept higher than the rear to help gas escape. Passing a tube alone risks harm to a distressed goat and is a veterinary task here. Recumbency worsens breathing, and an oral product with a wait delays the emergency call.
  17. The correct answer is A (Correct the strength to match the bottle and add the expiry, then recheck the label): The label shows a strength that does not match the stock bottle and has no expiry date, so there are two separate errors. A label with the wrong strength can cause a double dose, and an expiry date is a required label element. Both must be corrected and rechecked before dispensing, and a verbal warning is not a substitute for a correct label.
  18. The correct answer is C (Alert the veterinarian and place an IV catheter, then prepare warm fluids and warming): Bradycardia, hypothermia and weak pulses in a cat are typical of decompensatory shock, and the cause is poor perfusion, so the fix is volume and warmth rather than a heart-rate drug. IV access and warmed fluids begin right away with gentle, gradual external warming. Direct high heat burns poorly perfused skin, and delaying fluids until the temperature rises allows the shock to worsen.
  19. The correct answer is B (80 kVp, an increase of about 15 percent from the original 70 kVp): The rule of fifteen says a 15 percent kVp increase has the same effect on density as doubling the mAs, so halving the mAs needs a 15 percent kVp increase. 70 x 1.15 = 80.5, so 80 kVp is the nearest setting. Lowering kVp would reduce density the wrong way and change contrast. A 30 percent rise would overexpose, and 8 percent would leave the film too light.
  20. The correct answer is D (The lavender-top EDTA tube, which binds calcium to stop clotting): A CBC needs whole blood, so the sample goes in an EDTA tube, which prevents clotting by binding calcium and keeps cell morphology. Red tops clot for serum, citrate tubes are for coagulation tests, and fluoride is for glucose.
  21. The correct answer is B (Flush with a small volume of heparinized saline every 4 to 6 hours): An idle catheter clots when blood sits in the lumen, so a small volume of dilute heparinized saline every 4 to 6 hours keeps it patent. Once daily leaves the catheter to clot, a large volume every 2 hours adds fluid and handling risk, and undiluted heparin is a dosing error that can cause bleeding.
  22. The correct answer is D (Skip a repeat dose and call the clinic for a different flavour, form or route): The amount lost is unknown, so repeating the dose risks an overdose and withholding it risks under-treatment. The veterinarian can adjust the flavour, change to a compounded or injectable form or change timing. Stopping therapy without direction invites treatment failure, and hiding the whole day's dose in a meal gives an unreliable dose if the ferret leaves any of it.
  23. The correct answer is D (Loose skin between the shoulder blades, tented with the needle parallel to the body): A subcutaneous injection goes into loose skin, most often over the dorsal neck and shoulders, where the skin can be tented and the needle placed parallel to the body. The semitendinosus and epaxial muscles are intramuscular sites. Loose flank skin can hold a subcutaneous injection, but a steep needle toward the abdomen risks entering the body cavity.
  24. The correct answer is A (Hyperkalemia likely from the two drugs and reduced kidney excretion, and an ECG is needed): Potassium of 6.4 mmol/L is high, and spironolactone is a potassium-sparing diuretic while ACE inhibitors reduce potassium excretion, a combination that raises potassium especially when creatinine is elevated. A slow heart rate with lethargy suggests cardiac effects, so an ECG and prompt veterinary review are needed now. Delaying the report to an evening recheck is unsafe. Supplementing potassium would worsen a high value, and calling the problem simple dehydration overlooks the potassium.
  25. The correct answer is C (The correct volume is 0.08 mL; hold the syringe and confirm the order with the prescriber): Dose = 0.2 mg/kg x 4.0 kg = 0.8 mg, and 0.8 mg / 10 mg/mL = 0.08 mL. The drawn 0.8 mL contains 8 mg, a tenfold overdose caused by a misplaced decimal point. The syringe is held and the veterinarian informed so the error is reported and the order confirmed. Giving it, quietly redrawing it, or halving the dose all skip a correct, documented fix.
  26. The correct answer is D (37.5 to 38.5 C, pulse 28 to 44, respiration 8 to 16 breaths per minute): A resting adult horse has a rectal temperature of 37.5 to 38.5 C, a pulse of 28 to 44 beats per minute and a respiratory rate of 8 to 16 breaths per minute. Values of 60 to 80 beats per minute are normal for a foal or a small ruminant but are tachycardia in an adult horse. Higher respiratory rates and temperatures resemble dog values.
  27. The correct answer is A (Palpate the iliac wings and find the L7-S1 space, then clip and aseptically scrub): A line drawn between the cranial edges of the iliac wings crosses the spinous process of L7, and the lumbosacral space is the depression just behind it. The site is clipped widely and scrubbed aseptically, because introducing bacteria into the epidural space can cause serious infection. The sacrococcygeal space and L4-L5 are other sites and not the lumbosacral landmark. Leaving the hair on the site brings bacteria into the needle path.
  28. The correct answer is C (Drain and wipe the tank clean, then refill with fresh solution mixed per the label): Cloudy solution with debris in the tank is saturated, so it redeposits soil on instruments and weakens cavitation. The tank should be drained, wiped and refilled with fresh solution mixed to the label, usually daily or whenever visibly soiled. Skimming and topping up leave dirty solution in place, a longer cycle cannot clean in a saturated bath, and heat does not dissolve the debris. The scalers are then cleaned in the fresh bath before rinsing and drying.
  29. The correct answer is B (3 mL/hr, from 60 mg/hr of lidocaine at 20 mg/mL): 50 mcg/kg/min x 20 kg = 1000 mcg/min, which is 1 mg/min or 60 mg/hr. Dividing 60 mg/hr by 20 mg/mL gives 3 mL/hr. The value of 0.05 is the per-minute volume, 6 uses half the real concentration, and 60 skips division by the concentration entirely.
  30. The correct answer is B (Record the 0.6 mL drawn and the 0.1 mL waste with a witness, balance 7.8 mL): Ketamine is a controlled substance under the CDSA, so every volume removed from the vial must be accounted for. The opening balance of 8.4 mL less the 0.6 mL drawn leaves 7.8 mL, and the 0.1 mL waste is documented with a witness. Logging only the 0.5 mL given hides the waste and makes the count appear 0.1 mL too high. A drawn syringe is never returned to the vial, and entries are made when the drug is used.
  31. The correct answer is D (Twenty-six deciduous teeth, erupting between about two and six weeks of age): Kittens have 26 deciduous teeth, which erupt from about 2 weeks for the incisors to about 6 weeks for the premolars. Adult cats have 30 teeth, puppies have 28 deciduous teeth, and permanent teeth erupt at three to six months of age.
  32. The correct answer is D (3 mL/hr because 50 mcg/min equals 3000 mcg or 3 mg per hour): The dose is 5 mcg/kg/min x 10 kg = 50 mcg/min, and 50 x 60 = 3000 mcg/hr, which is 3 mg/hr. At 1 mg/mL that equals 3 mL/hr. The other values come from a decimal slip in the mcg to mg step, from skipping the minute to hour conversion, or from treating mcg as mL.
  33. The correct answer is C (Crystal violet then iodine then alcohol decolorizer then safranin, with water rinses between steps): The Gram stain applies crystal violet as the primary stain, iodine as a mordant that fixes it in Gram positive cell walls, alcohol to decolorize, and safranin to counterstain Gram negative bacteria. Changing the order, such as decolorizing before iodine or counterstaining first, gives wrong colour results.
  34. The correct answer is B (About 8 percent dehydrated, giving a deficit near 320 mL): Tacky membranes, a slow skin tent and mildly sunken eyes with normal perfusion fit about 8 percent dehydration, a moderate level. Deficit in mL is weight in kg x percent x 1000, so 4 x 0.08 x 1000 = 320 mL. Mild dehydration (5 percent) shows only dry gums, while 12 percent includes weak pulses and prolonged capillary refill. Without the 1000 factor the answer is 0.32 L and not 32 mL.
  35. The correct answer is A (Antibody absorption is best in the first hours and ends by about 24 hours): The foal's gut absorbs whole immunoglobulins only briefly, with absorption best in the first hours and closing by about 24 hours. Because the placenta does not transfer antibodies, a foal that nurses late risks failure of passive transfer. A three day window would make timing flexible, which it is not, and waiting for manure would miss the window. Colostrum given after about 24 hours supplies nutrition, but the cut off is later than 6 hours, so early feeding still matters most.
  36. The correct answer is C (It is calibrated to one agent's vapor pressure, so output differs from the dial): A precision vaporizer is designed and calibrated for one agent's vapor pressure, so the dial setting matches the delivered concentration only for that agent. Putting in another agent gives an output higher or lower than the dial reads, which risks an overdose or a light plane. The agents are keyed to prevent filling errors. Wick reactions and layering do not occur.
  37. The correct answer is C (Guinea pig, whose cheek teeth and incisors both grow throughout its life): Guinea pigs and chinchilla have open-rooted cheek teeth that grow continuously, so they need constant abrasive wear from hay. Rats, mice and hamsters have continuously growing incisors only, and their molars have closed roots. Rabbits also have open-rooted cheek teeth but are lagomorphs, not rodents.
  38. The correct answer is B (Wash hands after handling pets and soil, deworm puppies on schedule and pick up feces): Toxocara eggs shed in puppy feces become infective in soil after a few weeks, so prompt pickup, hand washing and a regular deworming schedule break the cycle. Puppies shed far more than adult dogs, so avoiding adult dogs misses the source, and leaving feces lets eggs become infective in the yard. Giving children dewormers after contact is not a recommended prevention measure.
  39. The correct answer is A (1.2 mL, since 0.6 mg divided by 0.5 mg/mL is 1.2): The dose is 6 kg x 0.1 mg/kg = 0.6 mg. At 0.5 mg/mL the volume is 0.6 / 0.5 = 1.2 mL. Multiplying by the concentration shrinks the volume, treating the concentration as 1 mg/mL halves it, and dosing per pound inflates the dose more than double.
  40. The correct answer is B (Ask the surgeon to stop the traction, then give an anticholinergic if ordered): Traction on viscera triggers a vagal reflex that slows the heart. Stopping the stimulus usually resolves it, and the veterinarian may order an anticholinergic such as atropine or glycopyrrolate if the rate stays low. Deepening isoflurane lowers blood pressure and heart function, epinephrine is for arrest, and a bolus or position change does not address the reflex.
  41. The correct answer is D (Retest for FIV antibody after the kitten reaches 6 months of age): Kittens can carry maternal FIV antibodies for up to 6 months, so a positive antibody result at 12 weeks may not mean infection. The accepted step is to retest after 6 months of age, or use PCR where available. Repeating tomorrow gives the same antibody result, and treating or euthanizing on this result is premature.
  42. The correct answer is A (Flow-by 25 to 40 percent, mask 35 to 55 percent and cage 40 to 60 percent): Flow-by is the least efficient method because oxygen mixes with room air, giving roughly 25 to 40 percent. A snug mask gives about 35 to 55 percent and an oxygen cage about 40 to 60 percent. Placing the cage lowest ignores that a closed cage holds oxygen well, and swapping the flow-by and mask ranges reverses their efficiency. The highest values overstate what typical flow rates deliver.
  43. The correct answer is D (Score the pain, then give a full mu opioid as ordered by the veterinarian): A rising heart rate and pressure, vocalization and guarding after surgery with no opioid given point to pain. A pain score confirms it, and a full mu opioid, as ordered by the veterinarian, gives the quickest relief. Acepromazine sedates but does not relieve pain, and naloxone would remove analgesia that is not even present. An oral NSAID takes too long to act as a lone drug.
  44. The correct answer is C (100 to 120 compressions per minute, pressing the chest by one third to one half): Current guidance is 100 to 120 compressions per minute, to a depth of one third to one half of the chest width, with full recoil between compressions and the hands over the widest part of the chest. Slower or shallower compressions give low output, pressing two thirds of the width risks rib fractures, and rates over 120 reduce filling time.
  45. The correct answer is C (Tooth 108, the fractured carnassial with exposed pulp and a draining tract): A fracture that exposes the pulp is painful and gives bacteria a direct path, and a draining tract points to an abscess, so tooth 108 is imaged first. The charted canine has only grade one mobility with mild recession, which is less urgent. The molar fracture involves enamel without pulp exposure, and calculus on the incisor is the least pressing finding. All of them still need images later in the full-mouth series.
  46. The correct answer is B (Furosemide increases urinary potassium loss, so the weakness fits hypokalemia and the dose needs review): Furosemide is a loop diuretic that increases renal potassium loss, and hypokalemia causes muscle weakness and a stiff gait. A value of 2.9 mmol/L is clearly below the reference range, so it is reported to the veterinarian, who may adjust the dose or supplement potassium. Calling the change expected would delay care for a weak patient, dehydration does not explain a low potassium, and no calcium abnormality is reported.
  47. The correct answer is C (A cornual nerve block with lidocaine before cautery, plus an NSAID afterward): Cautery burns tissue supplied by the cornual nerve, so a lidocaine cornual block placed before the procedure prevents the immediate pain, and an NSAID such as meloxicam reduces the inflammatory pain that lasts for hours or days. Sedation calms the calf but does not block nociception. Antibiotics do not treat pain, and cooling offers only brief relief.
  48. The correct answer is B (Flail chest, so lay the dog injured side down with oxygen and analgesia as ordered): A chest wall segment that moves opposite to the rest of the chest is flail chest, caused by multiple adjacent rib fractures. Placing the injured side down splints the segment and favors the healthy lung, and oxygen plus analgesia relieve work of breathing. Tight wrapping restricts the chest, and dorsal recumbency worsens dyspnea. Pneumothorax and effusion do not produce paradoxical movement of a segment.

Shifting the decimal gives 0.15 mL, a tenfold underdose. Turning kilograms into pounds gives 33 lb and 3.3 mL, and inverting the fraction gives 0.67 mL, so both are wrong.
  50. The correct answer is B (Raise the vaporizer a little and ask the veterinarian about extra analgesia now): Movement, a rising heart rate and a high blood pressure at the first stimulus signal a light plane with inadequate analgesia. A central eye and jaw tone also point to a light plane, and a deep patient would have a low pressure and a slow rate. The response is to raise the vaporizer a little and ask the veterinarian for added analgesia. Fluids, a lower vaporizer or waiting do not address the cause.
  51. The correct answer is C (Extend the splint to hold the stifle above and the hock below the fracture still): A splint must immobilize the joint above and the joint below the fracture, here the stifle and the hock, otherwise muscle pull moves the broken ends. Ending at the fracture or splinting one joint leaves motion at the break. Pulling to realign the bone can damage soft tissue and is not part of first aid splinting.
  52. The correct answer is C (The amount given and discarded with patient and date, plus initials and the updated balance): Ketamine is a controlled drug under the Controlled Drugs and Substances Act, so every millilitre must be accounted for, including what is given and what is discarded. The controlled drug log records the patient, date, amounts, initials and the running balance so that counts reconcile. Recording only the dose given or only the volume drawn up leaves part of the drug unaccounted.
  53. The correct answer is A (Continue compressions while the defibrillator charges, then deliver one shock and resume at once): The tracing is ventricular fibrillation, a shockable rhythm. Compressions continue during charging to keep perfusion, a single biphasic shock is delivered, and compressions resume immediately because the rhythm is checked only after two minutes. Atropine treats slow rates, pulse checks interrupt compressions, and synchronized shocks need a QRS to sync to, which fibrillation lacks.
  54. The correct answer is D (A lumbar fracture from a hindlimb kick when the rear end is unsupported): Rabbits have powerful hind legs and a light, fragile skeleton, so a sudden kick with the rear end dangling can fracture the lumbar spine. The hindquarters should be supported against the handler's body. Neck, femur and chest injuries are real handling risks, but they come from tight head restraint, gripping a leg or squeezing the chest, not from an unsupported rear end.
  55. The correct answer is C (Warn that pentobarbital remains can poison scavengers, so bury deeply): Pentobarbital remains in the tissues after euthanasia and can poison dogs, wildlife and scavengers that dig up the remains, so deep burial is advised along with checking local bylaws, or another aftercare choice such as cremation. The drug does not break down within days. Rinsing the body does not remove drug from tissue, and a shallow grave can be dug up easily.
  56. The correct answer is C (The tabletop surface above table level, while the drape hanging below table level is not sterile): Only the top of the drape above table level stays sterile, because the portion hanging below can touch non sterile surfaces and cannot be seen by the scrub person. Counting the hanging drape as sterile risks reaching below the table. The center-only and arm's reach ideas are not recognized boundaries.
  57. The correct answer is A (Confirm the oxygen supply, reading cylinder pressure and confirming a reserve cylinder with adequate oxygen): Oxygen powers the machine: it carries the vaporized agent, drives the flowmeter and fills the circuit for the leak test. If supply is inadequate, nothing else can be tested or relied on, and a patient could be left without oxygen. Vaporizer filling, the leak test and the scavenger and bag checks are all required, but each follows once supply is confirmed.
  58. They are recorded and repeated before closing a cavity and again at skin closure. Sterility is confirmed by indicators and processing records, tracing who opened a pack is a different task, and inventory reordering is a by-product at most.
  59. A slight lateral tilt or manual shift of the uterus relieves the compression. Pain would raise pressure, and atropine is wrong when the rate is already high.
  60. The correct answer is D (Place the cat in the oxygen cage, close the door and watch from outside): A dyspneic cat can decompensate quickly when restrained, so the least stressful method that still delivers oxygen is best. An oxygen cage supplies enrichment without handling and allows observation from outside. A snug mask, towel restraint for flow-by, and cannula placement all require restraint that raises oxygen demand. Flow-by is excellent for a cat that tolerates it, but this cat does not.
  61. The correct answer is C (The male urethra is long and narrows sharply in the penis, trapping plugs): In male cats the urethra is long and narrows to a very fine diameter in the penile portion, so crystals and mucous plugs lodge there. Female urethras are shorter and wider. The prostate in cats is small and is not the usual cause of blockage.
  62. The correct answer is B (Calibration, which sets the instrument response using standards of known concentration): Calibration sets the instrument's response using standards of known value so measured numbers correspond to true concentrations. Quality control then runs known-value control material to confirm performance, verification confirms an individual result, and a delta check compares a result with the patient's prior values. Each is a different laboratory activity.
  63. The correct answer is D (Housing a contagious patient with susceptible animals; isolate it with dedicated PPE and equipment): Kennel cough spreads by aerosol and contact, so a contagious respiratory patient is isolated from immunosuppressed animals, with dedicated gowns, gloves and equipment. Masks without separation do not stop airborne spread, vaccination takes time to work, and alcohol wipes do not separate patients.
  64. The correct answer is B (Fomepizole, which blocks alcohol dehydrogenase so toxic metabolites are not formed): Ethylene glycol is converted by alcohol dehydrogenase into glycolic and oxalic acid, which injure the kidneys. Fomepizole blocks that enzyme and works best when given within a few hours of ingestion, so it is the antidote. Calcium and bicarbonate may be used as supportive care but do not stop toxic metabolite formation. Charcoal binds ethylene glycol poorly, and 90 minutes after ingestion it does not replace the antidote.
  65. The correct answer is A (A simple interrupted pattern in the external rectus sheath, so one failed suture cannot open it): The body wall layer must hold against abdominal pressure, and the linea alba and external rectus sheath carry that strength. A simple interrupted pattern spreads tension and lets a single failed stitch stay isolated. The peritoneum and fat have little holding strength, and a skin only closure leaves the fascia to heal on its own, risking dehiscence and herniation.
  66. The correct answer is D (Treat the load as non sterile, check the wraps and run a full new cycle): A cycle must run the full programmed exposure of temperature, pressure and time to be considered complete. An interrupted cycle cannot be completed by adding minutes, because the interruption breaks the exposure sequence. Indicator tape changes colour with brief exposure and does not prove sterilization, and residual heat adds nothing. The packs should be rewrapped as needed and run again.
  67. The correct answer is B (Hold induction and inform the veterinarian, then obtain the owner's signature or documented authorization): Informed consent must be obtained and documented before anesthesia and the procedure begin. With the signature missing, induction is held, the veterinarian is informed, and the owner is contacted for a signature or properly documented authorization. Veterinarian approval is not the owner's consent, consent obtained after induction protects nobody, and cancelling the day is more than is needed when documented authorization can be obtained.
  68. The correct answer is D (Inform the veterinarian of the feeding history so the case can be rescheduled): A recent meal increases the risk of vomiting and aspiration during induction and recovery, and the decision to proceed rests with the veterinarian. For an elective procedure the safest plan is postponing until the stomach has emptied. A cuffed tube helps but does not remove the risk, a prokinetic does not guarantee an empty stomach, and withholding water does nothing for food already eaten.
  69. The correct answer is D (Put the thicker lumbosacral end under the cathode side, where the beam intensity is higher): The heel effect makes the beam weaker on the anode side because x-rays are absorbed in the heel of the target, so the cathode side delivers more photons. The thicker lumbosacral region needs that stronger beam, and the thinner thoracolumbar region suits the weaker anode side. Placing the thick end under the anode reverses the benefit. Turning the tube across the spine removes the gradient from the long axis, and raising kVp for the whole film does not balance thick and thin ends.
  70. The correct answer is A (Use only prescribed drugs after training, and discard old leftover vials): Owners may give injectables only under the veterinarian's prescription and after being shown the correct route, dose, site, sharps disposal and withdrawal times. Leftover or expired product is unsafe even when it was refrigerated, and teaching a technique does not replace a prescription. A blanket ban is also wrong, since owners routinely give prescribed insulin or vaccines. The technician refers the request to the veterinarian.
  71. The correct answer is A (Pull back on the plunger and check for blood in the hub before injecting): Bupivacaine given into a vessel can cause seizures and severe cardiac toxicity, so each aliquot is preceded by aspiration to confirm the needle is not intravascular. Blood in the hub means the needle is repositioned. A fresh needle and a pause between injections do not detect a vessel, and the lidocaine dose ceiling does not apply to bupivacaine, which has a lower toxic dose.
  72. The correct answer is C (1 to 3 mm, with a tight margin and no bleeding on gentle probing): The normal canine gingival sulcus measures about 1 to 3 mm, and a healthy margin does not bleed on gentle probing. In cats the normal depth is shallower, about 0.5 to 1 mm. Readings of 3 to 5 mm in a dog suggest a developing pocket, and deeper readings indicate periodontitis with attachment loss, not breed variation.
  73. The correct answer is D (Use low power with a steady water spray at the tip, and keep the tip moving): The scaler tip vibrates and heats the tooth, so water flow is needed to cool the tip and carry away debris; heat transmitted to the pulp can cause thermal injury, and a cat's teeth are small with thin enamel. Use the lowest power that removes calculus, a continuous spray, and constant light movement of the side of the tip, never the point. Reducing the water, or holding the tip still, concentrates heat on one tooth.
  74. The correct answer is A (Lubricate the suppository and insert it past the sphincter, then hold the tail down a minute): A lubricated suppository is placed past the anal sphincter, and holding the tail down with the anus closed for about a minute prevents expulsion. A shallow placement is pushed out, a melted base is hard to handle and absorbs unpredictably, and dry insertion irritates tissue.
  75. The correct answer is B (A prescription struvite diet that acidifies urine, fed wet to raise water intake): Struvite crystals form in alkaline urine, and the table shows a pH of 8.0. A diet that acidifies urine, restricts magnesium and phosphorus, and encourages water intake dissolves and prevents struvite. Alkalinizing diets worsen it, dry food lowers water intake, and kidney diets do not control urine pH.
  76. Finishing the tooth wastes time. Changing soda lime may be reasonable later but continuing to scale does not address the problem.   77. The correct answer is A (80 mL/hr and 960 mL total over the 12 hours): Rate = 20 kg x 4 mL/kg/hr = 80 mL/hr. Total = 80 mL/hr x 12 hr = 960 mL, checked as 960 / 12 = 80. Using 24 hours doubles the total because the order runs only 12 hours. Halving the rate gives 40 mL/hr, and doubling it gives 160 mL/hr, so each of those totals is also wrong.
  78.   79.   80. The ranges are not interchangeable and a heart rate in the cat range would be tachycardia in a large dog.
  81. The correct answer is B (Rising intracranial pressure producing the Cushing reflex of hypertension with reflex bradycardia): A falling heart rate with rising blood pressure is the Cushing reflex, a sign of rising intracranial pressure and possible herniation, especially with unequal pupils. Shock gives tachycardia and low pressure, pain tends to raise both, and an opioid overdose lowers pressure and breathing rate. The veterinarian should be alerted at once and the head kept elevated.
  82. Pressing at the thoracic inlet fills the vein cranially so it can be seen and entered. A midline stick risks the trachea, the pulse beside the trachea belongs to the carotid artery, and occluding high on the neck leaves the sampling site empty.
  83. Clinical scores and prognosis belong in the medical record rather than in the consent.   84. The correct answer is B (Red cells keep using glucose, so the value is falsely low; separate serum within 60 minutes): Red cells and white cells continue glycolysis in unseparated blood, lowering glucose by roughly 5 to 10 percent per hour at room temperature. A fed, healthy dog at 2.1 mmol/L points to a pre-analytical error, not true hypoglycemia. Separating serum from the clot within 30 to 60 minutes, or using a fluoride tube, prevents it. A red-top tube has no anticoagulant to dilute the sample.
  85. The correct answer is D (Rub corn syrup on the gums or offer it orally, then notify the veterinarian): A glucose of 2.1 mmol/L with weakness and tremors is hypoglycemia from an insulin overdose, and a dog that can swallow can take concentrated sugar by mouth right away while the veterinarian is notified. Withholding food or giving more insulin worsens the crash. Undiluted 50 percent dextrose is hypertonic and damages peripheral veins, so it is diluted and given under veterinary direction.
  86. A horizontal beam lateral with the animal upright keeps them in place. Beam direction does not change shell penetration or exposure time, and the shell cannot be moved out of the beam.

Cephalosporins can cross-react in some patients and the technician cannot substitute a drug, and a reduced dose does not remove the risk of a reaction. The safest action is to withhold the dose and have the veterinarian review the order.
  88. The chart may later be used by the veterinarian or authorities.   89. The correct answer is B (The coating or matrix controls slow release, so cutting it can release the whole dose quickly): Extended-release products rely on a coating or matrix that meters the drug out over hours. Breaking the tablet can cause dose dumping, delivering a large amount at once with toxicity risk and then too little later. Uneven drug distribution and mouth irritation are not the main concern. A score line does not make an extended-release tablet safe to split, so only tablets labelled as splittable should be divided.

The record documents the leak and the missing pack but no oversized tube or traumatic intubation. Inhalant vapour is a minor irritant compared with water and debris. A properly inflated cuff and a throat pack reduce this risk.
  91. The lead foil lies behind the film, away from the beam, so the foil side never faces the tube. The dot is on the beam side, not the back, and it does not indicate the patient's side.
  92. The correct answer is A (Fire or burn risk, so blot the pooled alcohol and let the skin dry before cautery): Alcohol is flammable, and electrosurgery sparks can ignite pooled alcohol or vapour and burn the patient. The solution is blotted from skin folds and the table, and the skin is allowed to dry fully before cautery is activated. Low power still produces sparks, a drape over the pool traps vapour, and a saline rinse leaves a wet field that does not address the flammable alcohol.
  93.
