  94. The correct answer is D (Apply a forced air warming blanket and give warmed IV fluids, and cover exposed limbs): The chart shows a steady fall from 38.1 to 35.5 degrees C, which is hypothermia, and it is best corrected by an active warming blanket, warmed fluids, and insulating exposed areas. Hot bottles on shaved skin can burn and are hard to control. Cold lavage removes heat, and deeper anesthesia lowers metabolic heat production further.
  95. The correct answer is B (Chloride and mineral residue cause pitting, so rinse with deionized water and dry before packing): Saline contains chloride, and hard tap water leaves minerals that attack the protective surface of stainless steel, producing pits and rust, especially when instruments dry slowly. Using deionized or distilled water for the final rinse and drying fully prevents it. Lubricant, autoclave temperature, and ultrasonic frequency are not the source of chloride and mineral residue.
  96. The correct answer is A (Malassezia yeast; the findings fit a yeast otitis needing veterinary treatment): Small oval, budding, peanut or footprint shaped purple cells among keratinocytes are Malassezia yeast, a common cause of pruritic, greasy brown otitis in Cocker Spaniels. Cocci are smaller and round, rods are elongated, and mites are large visible parasites with legs. Cytology guides treatment, so the veterinarian chooses the antifungal approach.
  97. The correct answer is B (1.12 mL because 28 lb times 2 mg/lb is 56 mg, divided by 50): The dose is per pound, so no conversion is needed: 28 lb x 2 mg/lb = 56 mg. Dividing by 50 mg/mL gives 1.12 mL. Converting to 12.7 kg would apply only to a per kilogram dose and gives 0.51 mL, while 11.2 mL is a decimal slip and 56 mL ignores the concentration.
  98. The correct answer is B (Close the pop-off and squeeze to 20 cm H2O, then inflate until the leak stops): A leak test is done with the pop-off valve closed, applying 20 cm H2O on the manometer by squeezing the bag. Air is added to the cuff in small increments until no gas escapes at that pressure, which gives a seal with the lowest cuff pressure. A fixed volume ignores the patient's trachea, and testing at 40 cm H2O or with the pop-off open misses the intended endpoint. Reopen the pop-off valve afterwards.
  99. The correct answer is C (Cold has slowed metabolism and drug clearance, so warm toward the preferred zone): Reptiles are ectotherms, so metabolic rate, heart rate and drug clearance depend on body temperature. A cool room with no heat source slows the heart and delays recovery, so the dragon is warmed gradually toward its preferred temperature zone with controlled external heat. Fluids, naloxone and dextrose address problems the record does not show, and none of them corrects the cold that is slowing drug clearance.
  100. The correct answer is C (A normal grief response in which owners may blame staff or themselves): Anger is a common and normal stage of grief, and owners may direct it at the veterinary team or at themselves, so the technician listens without defending. Denial involves refusing to accept the loss, bargaining involves attempts to negotiate for more time, and acceptance is calm adjustment. Recognizing the anger as grief helps staff respond with patience and not take it personally.
  101. The correct answer is D (Gas, then fat then fluid or soft tissue then bone then metal): From least to most opaque the order is gas, fat, fluid or soft tissue, bone or mineral, then metal. Gas absorbs the least radiation and is black, while metal absorbs the most and is white. Each wrong list swaps one neighbouring pair, such as fat before gas or metal before bone.
  102. The correct answer is A (Tape over the catheter is constricting the limb, so loosen it or replace it): A single cold, slow-refilling limb with normal systemic perfusion points to local constriction from tight tape. The tape is loosened or replaced and the limb rechecked. Shock would affect all limbs and vital signs, and phlebitis shows heat and pain at the vein.
  103. The correct answer is B (Record its size, monitor for spread and restrict activity since mild bruising is common): A small, stable, flat bruise beside a dry incision in a bright, eating dog with pink gums and normal heart rate is a common finding from tissue handling. Document it, mark its edges, restrict exercise, and watch for growth, pallor, or swelling. Hemorrhage would show pale gums, a fast heart rate, and a swelling abdomen, heat is not used early, and sutures are not removed.
  104. The correct answer is C (15 mL/hr, because 150 mcg/hr divided by 10 mcg/mL gives that rate): Hourly dose is 30 kg x 5 mcg/kg/hr = 150 mcg/hr. At 10 mcg/mL the rate is 150 / 10 = 15 mL/hr. Dividing by 60 would only apply to a mcg/kg/min order, 150 mL/hr forgets the concentration, and 0.5 mL/hr forgets the 30 kg weight.
  105. The correct answer is A (Atipamezole IM at a volume the veterinarian calculates, for dexmedetomidine effects): The slow heart rate and deep sedation are typical of dexmedetomidine, which is reversed by atipamezole, a specific alpha-2 antagonist. Naloxone reverses opioids only, flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines only, and doxapram stimulates respiration without reversing sedation. The dose is calculated by the veterinarian, and reversing with the dog comfortable also removes some of the analgesia from the butorphanol.
  106. The correct answer is C (Fluid and debris drain out of the mouth instead of pooling in the pharynx): Water, saliva, blood and plaque debris collect during scaling, and a head-down tilt lets them run out of the mouth by gravity instead of toward the pharynx and larynx. This lowers the aspiration risk even with a cuffed tube in place. Gravity does not push fluid toward the throat in this position, a head-down tilt tends to increase venous congestion, and the tilt does not straighten the airway.
  107. The correct answer is C (Leave hay and water available until premedication and clear the mouth of food before induction): Rabbits cannot vomit, so fasting offers little anesthetic benefit and long fasts risk gut stasis and hypoglycemia. Food stays available until premedication, and the mouth is checked for retained food before intubation. Dog-style 12 hour fasts and antiemetics do not apply, and removing hay does not empty the cecum, which only slows gut motility further.
  108. The correct answer is A (Expected inflammation, so limit exercise and report any enlargement or heat or pain or discharge): Mild, soft, nonpainful swelling and bruising are common for a few days after castration, particularly in active dogs, and are managed with limited exercise. A growing, firm, hot, painful, or draining scrotum would suggest hematoma or infection. This dog shows none of those signs, so re-exploration, drainage, or suture removal is not warranted.
  109. The correct answer is A (Pseudohyperkalemia from potassium leaking out of red cells that are rich in potassium): Akita and some other Japanese breeds have red cells with high intracellular potassium, which leaks into serum when the sample is left unseparated. A healthy dog with a normal ECG and normal sodium does not fit Addison's disease or oliguric kidney injury. EDTA contamination would lower calcium, which is normal here.
  110. The correct answer is D (It is a neuroactive steroid that gives smooth induction, with dose-related apnea possible): Alfaxalone is a neuroactive steroid acting on GABA receptors, with a smooth induction and dose related respiratory depression, so be ready to ventilate. The other descriptions match ketamine, thiopental and dexmedetomidine.
  111. The correct answer is B (One part concentrate in 32 parts total, so 31 parts water and 31 mL per litre): A 1:32 dilution means 1 part concentrate in 32 parts of final solution, so 31 parts water, and a litre needs about 1000 / 32, or 31 mL, of concentrate. Reading it as 1 part to 32 parts of water is a common slip that gives about 30 mL and a slightly weaker mix, inverting the ratio gives a mostly concentrate mix, and a 32 percent solution is about ten times too strong.
  112. The correct answer is A (Epinephrine given intramuscularly, because it reverses vasodilation and bronchospasm within minutes): Facial swelling, vomiting, tachycardia and hypotension soon after a vaccine are anaphylaxis, and epinephrine is the first drug. It constricts vessels and opens airways within minutes. Antihistamines and corticosteroids are adjuncts that act more slowly and do not restore blood pressure. Atropine treats bradycardia, and this dog has a fast heart rate.
  113. The correct answer is C (Stop the infusion and remove the catheter, then notify the veterinarian): Swelling and a cool limb during infusion point to fluid leaking into the surrounding tissue because the catheter is out of the vein or the vein has leaked. The infusion must stop, the catheter is removed, and the veterinarian is told. Continuing, flushing or massaging adds more fluid to the tissue.
  114. The correct answer is B (At the peak of inspiration, when the lungs are fully inflated and well outlined): Thoracic films are made at the peak of inspiration so the lungs are as large and air-filled as possible, giving the best contrast between lung and heart. At end expiration the lungs are small and look denser, which can mimic disease. Exposing midway through inspiration or during early expiration gives incomplete inflation or motion blur and a less reliable image.
  115. The correct answer is B (Inflate the cuff to just seal at 20 cm H2O and add a moist throat pack): A cuff inflated just enough to stop an audible leak, at about 20 cm H2O, seals the trachea against water and debris without overpressure on the mucosa. A moistened throat pack catches debris in the pharynx, and recording it on the anesthetic record helps ensure it is removed before extubation. Inflating by feel or to 40 cm H2O risks tracheal injury, and a pack alone cannot replace a cuff seal.
  116. The correct answer is D (Collect fresh feces from the middle of a new stool and test promptly or refrigerate): Antigen kits work best on fresh feces taken from the core of a new stool and run promptly or refrigerated for a short time. A yard sample is contaminated with soil and may not even be the puppy's, so scraping its surface does not fix that. Formalin interferes with immunoassay reagents, and diluting or straining the stool is not part of the kit method.
  117. Early removal risks obstruction. Jaw tone and palpebral return occur at a light surgical plane only. Pulling the tube with an inflated cuff injures the larynx and trachea.
  118. Phoning advice to the owner or noting the correction later both sound responsible but leave the dog without timely assessment. Waiting a day for bloodwork delays treatment that may be time critical, and the record must be corrected by a visible, signed entry rather than concealed.
  119. The correct answer is A (78 kVp, found by adding 2 kVp for each of the 4 extra centimetres of thickness): The thickness increase is 16 cm minus 12 cm, which is 4 cm. At 2 kVp per cm the increase is 4 x 2 = 8 kVp, and 70 + 8 = 78 kVp. The 102 kVp answer multiplies by the whole thickness, which would be a huge jump. The 74 kVp and 86 kVp answers use 1 and 4 kVp per cm, so each misreads the stated rule. mAs stays at 10 as the rule says.
  120.   121. The technician informs the veterinarian, gives the ordered drug and reassesses once it has had time to work, usually within an hour. Giving the drug without a close recheck cannot show whether it worked, and holding it to keep the routine interval leaves the cat in pain. Sedation can hide signs of pain without treating it.
  122. The correct answer is A (A dog that swallowed liquid drain cleaner and is now drooling and pawing at its mouth): Vomiting a caustic substance re-exposes the esophagus and mouth to the corrosive, so emesis is contraindicated for drain cleaner and similar corrosives. Recent chocolate, ibuprofen and grape ingestion in an alert dog are classic situations where the veterinarian may induce emesis. Other contraindications include petroleum products, sedation or seizures, and already vomiting patients.
  123. The correct answer is D (Use a padded trough and secure the limbs loosely, keeping the chest free to expand): A padded trough supports the dog in dorsal recumbency, and loosely secured limbs let the thorax expand and keep venous return open. Tight tie-downs pull on the chest and shoulders and reduce ventilation. A bare table concentrates pressure on the spine and elbows, and a head-down tilt makes the abdominal organs press against the diaphragm, which reduces tidal volume.
  124. The correct answer is C (Cover with towels and soak with fresh 1:10 bleach, then discard in a biohazard bag): Blood spills are covered with absorbent material, soaked with a freshly diluted disinfectant such as 1:10 bleach, left for the label contact time, and then cleaned up. Broken glass is lifted with tongs or a dustpan into a sharps container, and contaminated material goes into a biohazard bag. Dry wiping, alcohol pads or regular trash do not meet WHMIS or biosafety practice.
  125. Treatment includes analgesia, warmth, fluids, prokinetics when ordered, and syringe feeding of a critical care diet, with the veterinarian informed. Fasting deepens stasis, sugary treats upset cecal flora, and withholding analgesia leaves pain driving the anorexia.
  126. The correct answer is B (Sandbags over the thorax and pelvis with tape securing the extended limbs): Sandbags and tape fix the body and limbs in place so no hand is near the beam. A trough supports a patient on its back for ventrodorsal views, so it would roll the dog out of the lateral position. A handler steadying the pelvis still places hands at the field edge, and wedges that hold the dog sternal produce a different view than the lateral position requested.
  127. The correct answer is A (Tachypnea is rapid breathing, dyspnea is labored breathing and apnea is absent breathing): Tachypnea means a rapid respiratory rate, dyspnea means labored or difficult breathing, and apnea means breathing has stopped. Each of the other choices gets the first term right and then slips on one of the other two. Swapping dyspnea and apnea, or calling dyspnea slow breathing, or calling apnea slow breathing, are common mix-ups that matter in a record the owner will read.
  128. The technician must hold the drug and report, not decide alone. Lower doses keep the risk, giving it with food protects the stomach and not the kidneys, and carprofen is also an NSAID with renal risk.
  129. The correct answer is B (Observe a sustained ETCO2 waveform on the capnograph along with chest rise with each breath): A repeated ETCO2 waveform with each breath shows exhaled gas coming from the lungs, so it is the most reliable confirmation, supported by chest rise and direct visualization. SpO2 stays high for minutes after pre-oxygenation even with an esophageal tube. Fogging can occur with esophageal tubes. Palpation can find the trachea or the tube but a single firm structure is not proof of placement.
  130. The correct answer is B (Inflammation, then proliferation with granulation then maturation with remodeling): Healing begins with the inflammatory phase, which clears debris and bacteria. The proliferative phase follows, with granulation tissue, wound contraction and epithelialization. The maturation phase is last, remodeling collagen over weeks to months into a stronger scar. The stages overlap, but the order does not change, and each alternative moves one phase out of place.
  131. Hair or dry gel reduces the surface area, so current density rises and heats the skin under the plate. A drape contact would burn at the drape, plate distance is not the problem, and pooled alcohol is a fire risk rather than a cause of a burn at the plate.
  132. The correct answer is A (150 minutes, from 1500 psi x 0.3 L/psi = 450 L divided by 3 L/min): Remaining volume is 1500 psi x 0.3 L/psi = 450 L. Time is 450 L / 3 L/min = 150 minutes. Using 660 L ignores that the cylinder is not full, dividing psi by flow skips the conversion factor, and reporting 450 gives litres rather than minutes.
  133. A decimal slip gives 150, dividing by the factor gives a number in the thousands, and 0.018 belongs to the opposite conversion direction. A value near 15 mmol/L is above the feline reference range and above the renal threshold.
  134. The correct answer is B (10 mL, because 20 mEq divided by 2 mEq/mL equals 10): The bag is 1 L, so the total needed is 20 mEq. Dividing 20 mEq by the 2 mEq/mL concentration gives 10 mL. Using 1 mEq/mL gives 20 mL, multiplying by 2 gives 40 mL, and doubling the concentration gives 5 mL. The first two overshoots could cause dangerous hyperkalemia.
  135. The correct answer is A (It absorbs exhaled carbon dioxide so exhaled gas can be safely rebreathed): In a rebreathing circuit, exhaled gas returns to the patient, so the carbon dioxide must be removed or it builds up. Soda lime reacts chemically with CO2, forming carbonate, water and heat. The heat and water are by-products, not the purpose, and it is not a bacterial filter. Waste gas is collected at the pop-off valve by a scavenger, sometimes a charcoal canister.
  136. The correct answer is D (0.28 L/L, which falls below the 0.37 to 0.55 L/L reference interval): A percentage PCV converts to L/L by dividing by 100, so 28 percent is 0.28 L/L.   137. The correct answer is C (102.0 degrees F, from 38.9 x 1.8 = 70.0 and then adding the 32 constant): 38.9 x 1.8 = 70.02, and adding 32 gives 102.0 degrees F. Dropping the 32 gives 70.0, using (F - 32) / 1.8 gives 3.8, and multiplying by 5/9 instead of 9/5 gives 53.6.
  138. The correct answer is A (Type B cats carry strong natural anti-A antibodies, so type A blood causes acute hemolysis): Cats have naturally occurring alloantibodies. Type B cats carry strong anti-A antibodies, so even a small volume of type A blood can cause fatal hemolysis the first time. Type A cats have only weak anti-B antibodies and react less severely. Unlike dogs, no prior sensitization is needed, so cats are typed before any transfusion.
  139. The correct answer is C (Orbital tightening, with a narrowed eye opening and squinting): The Feline Grimace Scale scores ear position, orbital tightening, muzzle tension, whisker position and head position. Narrowed eyes with orbital tightening score as a pain indicator. Reduced grooming and a hunched posture are useful behavioural clues but are not items on this scale. Upright forward ears and loosely curved whiskers are normal findings that score low.
  140. The correct answer is B (Light with low density, because development was incomplete at the lower temperature): Developer chemistry slows down when it is cold, so at the chart time the silver halide crystals are not fully reduced, and the film is light with reduced density and contrast. Cold developer does not act faster, so a dark film is wrong. Brown stain suggests oxidized or exhausted chemistry, and fog suggests overdevelopment, light leaks or old film.
  141. The correct answer is C (Draw 0.2 mL, because 10 kg multiplied by 0.2 mg/kg gives 2 mg at 10 mg/mL): 22 lb divided by 2.2 gives 10 kg. The dose is 10 kg x 0.2 mg/kg = 2 mg, and at 10 mg/mL that is 2 / 10 = 0.2 mL. Dosing from pounds doubles the dose, multiplying by 2.2 inflates the weight to 48.4 kg, and drawing 2 mL ignores the 10 mg per mL concentration, which would be a tenfold overdose.
  142. The correct answer is A (50 mg, found by multiplying the 25 mg/mL concentration by the 2 mL volume): Amount of drug = concentration x volume = 25 mg/mL x 2 mL = 50 mg. Dividing gives a half-strength result and using the label value alone ignores that two millilitres were drawn. A decimal shift gives a tenfold error in the other direction. A quick check is that two mL of a 25 mg/mL solution must hold more than 25 mg.
  143. An alpha-2 agonist, an opioid and ketamine together produce reliable sedation and analgesia within minutes. Oral and subcutaneous acepromazine are slow and variable, and an intravenous route needs a catheter the cat will not tolerate. Once sedated, the catheter and monitoring are placed.
  144. The correct answer is D (Report to the veterinarian and continue the course unless told otherwise): Mild soft stool is a common antibiotic side effect, and this dog is bright and hydrated. The veterinarian should be told so a probiotic or change of drug can be considered, while the course continues unless directed otherwise, and giving doses with food can help. Stopping early risks relapse and resistance, human anti-diarrheals can be unsafe, and withholding water risks dehydration.
  145. The correct answer is C (Run at 5 mL/hr for 15 minutes, then increase only if vital signs remain stable): The start rate is 20 kg x 0.25 mL/kg/hr = 5 mL/hr. The slow start limits how much incompatible or reactive blood enters before a reaction shows. The rate rises only after vitals are checked and found stable. A per-minute setting is 60 times too fast, 100 mL/hr skips the safety period, and skipping vital checks hides a reaction.
  146. The correct answer is A (Recount with a second person and check the day's log entries for an error): A discrepancy must never be fixed by changing the record. The first step is verification: recount with a second person and check the entries for arithmetic or transcription errors. If the difference remains, the technician reports it to the veterinarian or practice manager, who follows the clinic's procedure and legal obligations for controlled drugs under the CDSA. Waiting only makes the cause harder to trace.
  147. The later rise can be a counter-regulatory rebound, so raising the dose could worsen things. The veterinarian decides on a dose reduction, and the technician should not change timing or dose.
  148. The correct answer is A (Both kidneys, outlined by the fat around them): In a cat with a normal amount of fat the kidneys are outlined by retroperitoneal fat and are visible on plain films. The adrenal glands and normal ureters are too small to be seen without contrast or ultrasound, and the normal pancreas blends with other soft tissue so it cannot be distinguished.
  149. The veterinarian needs to know, large bore IV access is placed, and blood products are made ready before induction. Calling the picture chronic disease or expected delays the blood the dog may need now. Inducing before access is in place removes the safest route for resuscitation.
  150.   152. Kelly forceps clamp vessels with ratcheted jaws, Babcock forceps grip tissue with broad ring jaws, and Metzenbaum scissors cut. None has a single blunt hooked tip on a long shaft.
  153.   154. The correct answer is C (The needle holder jaws crushed the strand and weakened it at the clamped point): Grasping suture material with instrument jaws crushes or nicks the strand, and it will then fail when tension is applied at that point. Extra throws do not break a suture, a thick suture is stronger rather than weaker, and resterilizing is a separate concern that would not explain two breaks at one spot. Handle only the needle and the free end.
  155. The correct answer is D (Start active cooling with tepid water and a fan, then alert the veterinarian): Exercise-related hyperthermia is treated with active cooling using tepid water and airflow, stopped at about 39.5 C, while the veterinarian is informed. Wet towels in a closed kennel trap heat, ice water causes vasoconstriction and shivering, and antipyretics do not work because this is not a fever.
  156. The correct answer is C (Collect product details with tablets missing and time and weight, then consult the veterinarian): The veterinarian needs the agent, strength, how many tablets are missing, how long ago and the dog's weight to judge the toxic dose and decide on decontamination. NSAIDs such as ibuprofen are toxic to dogs even at modest doses. Inducing vomiting is a possible step, but only on veterinary direction and with the facts in hand. Feeding a meal does not neutralize the drug, and waiting for signs loses the treatment window.
  157. The correct answer is A (Close the pop-off valve and occlude the wye, fill to 20 cmH2O and watch it hold): A leak test needs a closed system: the pop-off valve closed and the wye occluded. Oxygen is added until the manometer reads 20 cmH2O, then the flow is stopped and the pressure should hold for about 10 seconds. With the valve open, pressure cannot build. With the wye open, gas escapes there. A test at 60 cmH2O would risk damaging bags, hoses and seals and does not match the protocol.
  158. The correct answer is D (45 drops per minute, because with this drop factor the drops equal the mL/hr rate): Drops per minute equals mL/hr times the drop factor divided by 60 minutes. Here 45 x 60 / 60 = 45 drops per minute, which is why a 60 drops/mL set makes the two numbers match. Dividing without the drop factor gives 0.75, multiplying without dividing by minutes gives 2,700, and 45 x 20 / 60 = 15 would only be right for a 20 drops/mL set.
  159. The correct answer is D (0.12 mL, from 0.01 mg/kg times 12 kg giving 0.12 mg at 1 mg/mL): The dose is 0.01 mg/kg x 12 kg = 0.12 mg. The vial holds 1 mg in every mL, so 0.12 mg is 0.12 mL. The 1.2 mL answer is the ten-times higher dose used later in prolonged arrest, not the ordered dose. Using pounds gives 0.01 x 26.4 = 0.26 mL, and a misplaced decimal gives 0.012 mL, which would be far too little to help.
  160. The correct answer is C (The cuff is too wide and reads falsely low, so use one about 6 cm wide): The cuff width should be about 40 percent of the limb circumference, which is 0.4 x 14 cm = 5.6 cm, so a 6 cm cuff fits. An 8 cm cuff is about 57 percent of the circumference, and a cuff that is too wide transmits pressure poorly and reads falsely low. A cuff that is too narrow reads falsely high, so the reverse option has the direction wrong. Limb position was already at heart level.
  161. The correct answer is B (Score at set intervals and after each analgesic dose, using one tool and charting it): Pain scoring is most useful when done on a schedule and after each analgesic dose using the same validated tool, so changes can be compared and treatment adjusted. Pausing overnight leaves a gap when breakthrough pain can appear. One score per shift misses trends, and charting only treated scores hides how the dog was doing before. Different tools on each shift make scores impossible to compare.
  162. The correct answer is C (Keep the collar on constantly and check the incision twice daily, restricting activity until recheck): Licking is the commonest cause of incision failure, so the collar stays on at all times, including overnight, and the incision is inspected twice daily for redness, swelling, or discharge. Exercise is restricted until the recheck. Ointments encourage licking, and bathing wets the wound and introduces bacteria before healing is complete.
  163. The correct answer is D (Run the manufacturer's warm-up exposures at low settings, stepping up toward the working technique): A cold anode can crack under a sudden high-heat exposure, so a stepped warm-up of low exposures protects the tube. Starting at maximum load is the very thing that causes damage. A dosimeter badge records staff dose and says nothing about tube output. Cleaning is good practice but has no effect on output.
  164. The correct answer is C (4.8 mcg/kg/min, from the 7.2 mg/hr the pump delivers): The bag holds 60 mg / 500 mL = 0.12 mg/mL, and at 60 mL/hr the dog receives 7.2 mg/hr = 7200 mcg/hr, or 120 mcg/min. Dividing by 25 kg gives 4.8 mcg/kg/min, and 4.8 x 25 x 60 returns 7200 mcg/hr. The 288 value is the per hour dose never converted to minutes, 120 never divides by weight, and 0.29 is the dose in mg/kg/hr.
  165. The correct answer is D (Low body fat limits propofol redistribution, so clearance in sighthounds is slow): Propofol is redistributed into fat and tissue, and a lean sighthound has few fat stores for this, so its plasma level falls slowly and recovery is prolonged. Cats are the species with weak glucuronidation, and coat colour and kidney damage are not accepted causes.
  166. The correct answer is A (Hypoglycemia from fasting and disease; notify the veterinarian and give dextrose): A glucose of 2.1 mmol/L is well below the ferret range, and the trembling, drooling, and pawing are classic signs of hypoglycemia, which is likely after fasting in an animal with insulinoma. The veterinarian is told and dextrose is given as directed. Nausea, dental pain, or shivering do not explain a glucose that low.
  167. The correct answer is D (Squeeze a skin fold and scrape with a dulled blade until capillary oozing appears): Demodex mites live deep in the hair follicles, so the skin is squeezed to push them toward the surface and scraped until capillary blood appears. A dulled blade with mineral oil keeps mites on the slide. A superficial scrape suits mites living on the surface, such as Sarcoptes, and often misses follicular mites. Tape and swab preparations sample surface debris and yeast, not follicle contents.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Do not run it; recollect and label the sample at the patient side): An unlabelled tube cannot be reliably tied to a patient, and a mix-up could lead to a wrong diagnosis or treatment. The sample must be recollected and labelled immediately with patient name, owner, date and time at the patient side. Guessing or having the owner confirm the tube does not meet identification standards.
  2. The correct answer is C (Deflate the cuff and withdraw the tube 2 to 3 cm, then recheck): A tube that is too long passes the carina and enters a bronchus, so only one lung is ventilated. That gives unilateral breath sounds and a falling SpO2. The cuff is deflated and the tube is withdrawn a few centimetres until both sides of the chest rise, then the cuff is reinflated. Advancing the tube makes the problem worse, and flow or size changes do not fix the position.
  3. The correct answer is B (The liver metabolizes most drugs and the kidneys excrete them): The liver is the main site of drug metabolism, converting drugs into forms that are easier to excrete. The kidneys are the main route of excretion for most drugs and their metabolites, while bile and feces are secondary. Plasma enzymes handle only a few drugs, and the kidneys do little metabolism for most. This is why liver or kidney disease may require dose changes.
  4. The correct answer is C (Scrub with gauze for the full labeled contact time, commonly several minutes): Antiseptics need adequate mechanical scrubbing and contact time to reduce skin flora, and the label gives the required time. Chlorhexidine also has residual activity if it is not removed prematurely. Short scrubs, early drying or repeated quick rinses do not meet the contact time and leave more bacteria on the skin.
  5. The correct answer is D (62.5 mL/hr, from 1500 mL per day divided by 24 hours): Daily volume = 25 kg x 60 mL/kg/day = 1,500 mL, and 1,500 mL / 24 hr = 62.5 mL/hr. Dividing by 60 gives 25, which would be mL per minute scaling and not an hourly rate. Dividing by 12 doubles the rate, and dividing 60 by 25 inverts the operation and gives a trivial rate.
  6. The correct answer is A (Common glucocorticoid effects, so keep water available and tell the veterinarian): Polyuria, polydipsia, panting and polyphagia are expected glucocorticoid effects, often dose related. The patient should have access to water, and the veterinarian should hear about it because a dose change may be possible. Restricting water, calling it an allergy, or holding a steroid after two weeks without direction misreads a predictable drug effect in a bright, eating dog. Early diabetes is possible but is not the first conclusion.
  7. The correct answer is B (0.36 mL/hr, from 18 mcg/hr divided by the 50 mcg/mL concentration): The dose is 3 mcg/kg/hr x 6 kg = 18 mcg/hr. Dividing by the concentration gives 18 mcg/hr / 50 mcg/mL = 0.36 mL/hr, and a recheck by multiplying 0.36 x 50 returns 18 mcg. The 21.6 value multiplies by 60 when no unit change is needed, the 0.006 value divides by 60 for the same reason, and 18 mL/hr forgets that each milliliter already holds 50 mcg.
  8. The correct answer is B (Tell the employer so duties change and an abdominal badge is worn, with no patient holding): The employer can only reduce the dose if they know about the pregnancy, so declaring it allows duties to be adjusted and a second badge worn at the abdomen under the apron to record fetal dose. Holding patients is the highest-dose task and is avoided. An apron alone does not remove the need for changes, a collar badge does not record fetal dose, and handing off holding without extra monitoring leaves the fetal dose unrecorded.
  9. The correct answer is A (It blocks NMDA receptors in the spinal cord, reducing wind-up): At sub-anesthetic doses ketamine is an NMDA receptor antagonist, so it dampens wind-up and central sensitization and improves opioid analgesia in established or expected severe pain. It does not act at mu receptors, it does not block cyclooxygenase like an NSAID, and sodium channel blockade is how lidocaine works.
  10. The correct answer is B (The concentration preventing movement to a noxious stimulus in half of patients, lowered by sedatives): MAC is the minimum alveolar concentration at which 50 percent of patients do not move in response to a standard noxious stimulus. Sedatives, opioids and hypothermia lower it, so less inhalant is needed. It is not a vaporizer limit. The 95 percent figure is a different measure, and hypothermia lowers MAC rather than raising it.
  11. The correct answer is A (Fold the near corner over the tray with a turned-back tab, then the side corners): An envelope fold covers the near corner first with a small tab turned back, then the side corners, then the far corner, finishing with a second wrap folded the same way. The tab lets the circulator open the pack without touching the sterile contents. Folding the sides first misplaces the layers, a flat fold without a tab makes aseptic opening difficult, and metal pins puncture the wrap.
  12. The correct answer is C (This is a normal lymphocyte-predominant differential for a guinea pig): Guinea pigs and rabbits normally have a lymphocyte-predominant differential, with lymphocytes making up around half or more of white cells. Applying dog or cat ranges would suggest lymphocytosis. A stress leukogram shows neutrophilia with lymphopenia, which is the opposite of this pattern.
  13. The correct answer is A (SpO2 falls and breath sounds are quieter over the left chest than over the right side): A tube that is too long passes the carina and enters the right mainstem bronchus, so only the right lung is ventilated. The unventilated left lung still has blood flow, so oxygenation falls, and left-sided breath sounds and chest movement are reduced. Capnography is not flat or high, because gas still reaches the right lung. Pulling the tube back until the tip sits near the thoracic inlet corrects the problem.
  14. The correct answer is C (Brick-red injected membranes with a capillary refill time under 1 second and a bounding pulse): In early compensated shock in dogs, catecholamines produce a hyperdynamic state: bright red membranes, rapid refill under 1 second, a fast heart rate and a bounding pulse. As compensation fails, membranes turn pale and refill slows to over 2 seconds with weak pulses, and in the late stage they become gray with refill over 3 seconds or none.
  15. The correct answer is A (An allergic reaction from a type I hypersensitivity response to donor plasma proteins): Hives and facial swelling point to an allergic, type I reaction. A febrile non-hemolytic reaction needs a rise of more than 1 degree C, and this rise was only 0.4. Pale yellow plasma argues against hemolysis, and clear lungs with normal breathing argue against circulatory overload. The transfusion is paused and the veterinarian is told.
  16. The correct answer is D (0.5 drops per second, which is about one drop every two seconds): 120 mL/hr x 15 drops/mL = 1800 drops/hr. Dividing by 60 gives 30 drops per minute, and dividing by 60 again gives 0.5 drops per second. Reporting 30 forgets the second conversion, 2 inverts the ratio, and 0.03 uses only the volume per second and leaves out the drop factor.
  17. The correct answer is A (Give it with food, and stop it and call if vomiting or black stool appears): Giving carprofen with food reduces gastric upset, and owners must stop the drug and call the clinic at the first sign of vomiting, black tarry stool, lethargy or reduced appetite, since these signal ulceration or kidney injury. Waiting for two missed meals, continuing through vomiting, or adding an antacid all let a developing ulcer go unchecked.
  18. The correct answer is D (Apply a forced-air warming blanket over the cat and warm the IV fluids): Forced air blankets warm effectively without burning, and warmed fluids reduce further heat loss. Heating pads and hot water bottles can burn anesthetized skin, and a towel alone only slows loss. The drifting heart rate is a typical effect of hypothermia.
  19. The correct answer is B (Stop carprofen now and have the dog examined today for gastric ulceration): Vomiting plus black tarry stool (melena) on an NSAID points to gastrointestinal ulceration, which can bleed and perforate. The drug must be stopped and the dog seen the same day. Another dose with food will not protect an ulcerated stomach. A second NSAID carries the same risk, and prednisone given with an NSAID greatly increases ulcer risk, even though the dog is still eating.
  20. The correct answer is B (Sedate as ordered and support the whole body with positioning aids, without forcing the limbs): Rabbits have a light skeleton and powerful hind legs, so a struggling rabbit can fracture its own lumbar spine. Veterinary-ordered sedation and supports such as foam wedges or tape position the animal without force. Pressing the hind legs flat, holding the rabbit down by the lower back without sedation, and scruffing with limbs stretched all raise the injury risk.
  21. The correct answer is D (100 mg, because a 2% solution holds 20 mg in every mL): A 1% solution is 10 mg/mL, so 2% is 20 mg/mL. Then 5 mL x 20 mg/mL = 100 mg. A decimal slip either way gives 10 or 1000 mg. Dividing by 4 kg gives mg/kg, which is a dose per kilogram rather than the content of the syringe.
  22. The correct answer is D (Record the loss in the controlled drug log with a witness and tell the responsible veterinarian): Opioids are controlled under the Controlled Drugs and Substances Act, so a loss must be recorded accurately in the controlled drug log, ideally with a witness, and reported promptly to the veterinarian who is responsible for the drugs and for any required report to Health Canada. Logging alone and waiting a week, returning the vial without accounting for the missing volume, or moving the drug to another container all leave the loss unexplained.
  23. The correct answer is A (Expel the bubble promptly and cap the syringe, since air alters oxygen and carbon dioxide): Room air has a PO2 near 21 kPa and almost no CO2, so a bubble in contact with the sample moves the PO2 toward air and lowers the PCO2 as gases equilibrate. The bubble must be expelled promptly and the syringe capped, and an iced sample is used only if analysis will be delayed. Heparin is the right anticoagulant, and venous blood cannot replace an arterial sample for oxygen.

Calming the dog or holding the mouth gently closed lets a clean tracing be recorded. Real VT would not stop when panting stops, and mains interference gives a regular fine fuzz instead.
  25. The correct answer is D (Abdominal pain consistent with colic, typical of visceral discomfort): Pawing, flank watching and an elevated heart rate are classic signs of abdominal pain in horses. Laminitis causes a rocking-back stance and reluctance to walk, pleuropneumonia produces cough, fever and shallow breathing, and boredom behaviours do not raise the heart rate to 70.
  26. The correct answer is A (Heinz bodies, which are denatured hemoglobin from oxidative injury and explain the anemia): Onion toxicity is an oxidant injury that denatures hemoglobin into Heinz bodies, which stain as small pale blue round projections on the red cell with new methylene blue and cause hemolysis. Howell-Jolly bodies are dark nuclear remnants. Hemoparasites are more often stained on a Wright-type smear, and reticulocyte aggregates form a dark blue network.
  27. The correct answer is C (Residual gas on the items can irritate or chemically burn the patient's tissue): Ethylene oxide is absorbed by rubber and plastic, and the aeration period lets it escape. Items removed too early still release gas, which causes tissue irritation or chemical burns in the patient and exposes staff. The sterilization step itself was done, so the item is not simply non sterile, and ethylene oxide acts at low temperature, not high heat.
  28. The correct answer is C (Hands stay inside the gown cuffs, so skin never touches the glove outside): In closed gloving the fingers are pushed through the cuff only after the glove is in place, so skin never contacts the outside of the glove and the cuff stays covered. This lowers the chance of contaminating the glove. It is done after the gown, so it does not save time by reversing the order, it does not change drying needs after the scrub, and it is done by the wearer rather than a second person.
  29. The correct answer is B (Mineral deposits and detergent residue left by incomplete rinsing and hard water): Chalky or milky spots usually come from hard water minerals or detergent that dry onto the steel when the final rinse is skipped. Rust looks brown or orange and begins at worn spots. Heat damage discolors steel differently, and instrument milk lubricant leaves a film rather than dry white spots.
  30. The correct answer is B (Motile first-stage lungworm larvae that migrate out of the feces into warm water): The Baermann technique suspends feces in gauze over a funnel of warm water. Motile larvae such as Aelurostrongylus abstrusus in cats crawl out and settle in the stem, where they are collected and examined. Eggs and cysts are found by flotation, so a negative float does not exclude lungworm.
  31. The correct answer is C (Explain that the veterinarian must see the dog and authorize any new prescription): A prescription for a controlled drug is the veterinarian's decision and requires a current professional relationship with the patient. A technician cannot authorize or renew it, and an expired prescription is not valid. A bridging supply, a phone report of stability or an old prescription sent elsewhere does not change this. The technician takes a message for the veterinarian and books a recheck if needed.
  32. The correct answer is D (Cats show neurologic and cardiac toxicity such as tremors, seizures and arrhythmias): Lidocaine toxicity affects the central nervous system and heart, and cats are more sensitive than dogs, so tremors, seizures and arrhythmias can appear at doses that dogs tolerate. The total dose is limited to about 2 mg/kg, and the volume is calculated before blocks. Methemoglobinemia is a classic concern with other local anesthetics, lidocaine is not an opioid, and a dog-based threshold would far overshoot the safe cat dose.
  33. Trimming a suture, bandaging, or a deterrent spray alone does not stop access or document the damage.
  34. The correct answer is B (A tenfold decimal error: 0.5 mg needs 0.5 mL, so 5 mL delivers 5 mg): Dose = 0.1 mg/kg x 5 kg = 0.5 mg. At 1 mg/mL that is 0.5 mL, so 5 mL contains 5 mg, ten times the ordered dose. A large syringe makes small volumes harder to read but is not the calculation error, a volume is judged against concentration rather than by what seems typical, and the weight is already in kg.
  35. Rechecking at 15 and 30 minutes and with each rate increase catches reactions early, when most appear. Routine two-hour checks or a single check after an hour are too sparse.

The patient does not shield the thyroid, a missing apron exposes the trunk, and bare hands near the field are exposed to scatter even with a ring badge.

From 10:15 to 10:30 the rate was 60 mL/hr, giving 15 mL. The total is 45 mL. Using only one rate, or an hourly average as a volume, gives the wrong answer.
  38. The correct answer is D (Intravenous, because the drug goes directly into the blood without absorption): Intravenous delivery places the drug straight into circulation, so onset is within seconds and the catheter is already in place. Intramuscular tempts as the next fastest injection but needs minutes for absorption. Intranasal and transmucosal routes depend on mucosal absorption, which is slower and less predictable. In shock, blood is shunted away from muscle and mucosa, which slows these routes further.
  39. The correct answer is B (Films right after dosing, then at about 15 and 30 minutes and 1 and 2 hours): Barium moves quickly in a cat, so films need to be close together early to see the stomach, duodenum and loops. The usual series is immediate, 15 minutes, 30 minutes, 1 hour and 2 hours, extended if transit is slow. One late film misses the details of the stomach and small bowel. Long interval films suit other situations, and one 30 minute film cannot show emptying.

The catheter stays open for fluids and drugs. Finishing the dose adds more triggering agent. Drugs go on the veterinarian's order, and a fast flush still delivers contrast already in the line.
  41. The correct answer is B (Ideal body condition, with ribs felt without excess fat and a visible waist): A score of 5 on the 9-point scale is ideal: ribs are easy to feel without excess fat, a waist shows from above and the abdomen tucks up from the side. Thin descriptions with easily seen vertebrae belong to scores around 3, and an exaggerated waist with visible ribs sits just below ideal. A rounded abdomen with ribs hard to feel marks the overweight scores of 6 and above. Naming the score correctly helps the owner set a realistic weight goal.
  42. Pink gums, a refill time of 1.5 seconds and strong pulses argue against significant hemorrhage, so scoring pain and asking for analgesia comes first. Preparing for bleeding tempts because the spleen is involved, but the perfusion findings do not support it yet. The tissue is kept moist under sterile saline gauze while the veterinarian is called at once. Pushing it back carries contamination into the abdomen.   44. The plan is to inform the veterinarian, give IV fluids to maintain renal perfusion, monitor blood pressure, and avoid NSAIDs. Dividing by 88.4 shows 190 umol/L is about 2.15 mg/dL, not 1.9. Distemper tempts because it affects the airway, but it is a virus and the stem asks for a bacterial agent. Lungworm also causes coughing, yet it is a parasite and does not spread through kennel air. The skin bacterium is common in dogs but does not cause a honking tracheobronchitis outbreak.

Demodex is a cigar-shaped mite with short legs, Cheyletiella has large hooked palps, and Sarcoptes is round with dorsal spines. The location, debris colour and body shape together identify the ear mite.

Waiting, discharging, or testing wastes time while the reaction may worsen.

Atipamezole at 5 mg/mL and an equal volume gives 0.4 mL x 5 mg/mL = 2 mg, which is the labelled 10 times ratio by dose in mg, so the volumes match. Scaling the volume by the concentration ratio double counts it, and halving the volume is not part of the label directions.

The result is reported so the veterinarian can plan fluids and further tests. Water must not be withheld from an azotemic dehydrated dog.
  50. Multiplying by 6 would apply to a 10 second strip, the paper speed does not enter the count method, and a multiplier of 15 belongs to a 4 second strip.
  51. Switching to distilled or deionized water in the reservoir stops the staining.   53. Lower frequencies penetrate more and are used for deep large dogs, not fine bladder wall detail. A cardiac phased array is made for imaging between ribs.

The Labrador tempts as a large breed that eats fast, but its chest is less extreme, so its risk is lower than the Great Dane's. Long-backed and toy breeds lack the chest conformation that lets the stomach rotate. Large meals and exercise afterward add risk but do not change which dog ranks highest.

  60.   61. The correct answer is B (About 40 percent of the limb circumference, which avoids a falsely high or low reading): Cuff width is chosen at about 40 percent of the limb circumference, which occludes the artery evenly. A cuff that is too narrow reads falsely high and one that is too wide reads falsely low, so a very wide cuff is not more accurate. The probe is only the sensor and does not set the cuff size. Limb length is not the measurement used, even though the ratio sounds right.
  62. The first step is to turn off the vaporizer, give 100 percent oxygen and give gentle mask breaths. Deepening the agent worsens the apnea, and a blind intubation risks injury before oxygen is given.   63. The correct answer is A (Use warm saline with gentle low pressure through a soft catheter placed in the vertical canal): When the tympanic membrane cannot be seen, warm sterile saline at low pressure from the vertical canal is the safest way to loosen debris. Firm pressure or a catheter advanced deeper tempts as faster cleaning but can perforate the drum or push infection into the middle ear. Peroxide tempts because it fizzes, but it irritates tissue and is harmful if it reaches a damaged middle ear. Warm fluid also avoids the dizziness that cold fluid can cause.

A cuff that is too narrow overestimates pressure, one that is too wide underestimates it, and covering the whole limb gives unreliable results.
  67. Positioning aids or sedation ordered by the veterinarian let the view be taken without anyone in the field.   68. The correct answer is B (One and one quarter tablets giving 62.5 mg, the nearest quarter to the 60 mg dose): The dose is 12 kg x 5 mg/kg = 60 mg, and 60 mg / 50 mg per tablet = 1.2 tablets. Because tablets can be quartered, 1.25 tablets (62.5 mg) is the closest measurable amount. Rounding down underdoses, inverting the ratio gives 0.83, and using 26.4 lb in place of kg gives 132 mg, more than double the dose.
  69. The correct answer is C (The product is corrosive and can cause severe skin burns, eye damage and metal damage): The WHMIS 2015 corrosion pictogram shows liquid attacking a hand and a metal surface, meaning the product can burn skin, damage eyes and corrode metals. The skull and crossbones means acute toxicity, and the flame over a circle means oxidizer. The chest silhouette means a longer-term health hazard such as cancer or organ damage. Each of those is a real pictogram, but none shows liquid acting on a hand and metal.
  70. The correct answer is A (0.9 percent sodium chloride with potassium chloride added, as directed): Persistent vomiting of gastric contents causes loss of chloride, hydrogen and potassium, producing a hypochloremic, hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis. Saline supplies chloride and sodium and potassium is added. LRS is a reasonable general choice but contains less chloride, dextrose in water has no electrolytes, and hypertonic saline is for emergency use.
  71. The correct answer is A (Depleted fixer left unexposed silver halide on the film, so it stays cloudy and stains): Fixer removes unexposed silver halide and hardens the emulsion, so exhausted fixer leaves films cloudy and milky and later produces yellow-brown staining. Weak developer gives faint, low-contrast images, and overdevelopment gives dark films, so neither produces a milky film. Underexposure gives pale images but does not cause cloudiness or stains in storage. The several months of use in the same fixer point to depletion.
  72. The correct answer is C (Stop handling the animals and restrict movement, then have the veterinarian notify the CFIA immediately): Blisters on the tongue and hooves with fever and lameness in several cattle suggest a foreign animal disease, which must be reported to the CFIA immediately. The team stops handling animals and restricts movement of animals, people and equipment while the veterinarian notifies. Sending samples to a private lab first delays the report, and isolation alone with a call to a provincial association is the wrong body on the wrong timeline. Vaccinating before reporting would delay control of a notifiable disease.
  73. The correct answer is C (Offer small portions of bland, digestible food often once vomiting settles): A full meal soon after anesthesia and abdominal surgery can overload an unsettled stomach. Small, frequent, bland meals are better tolerated and keep nutrition going. Prolonged fasting and large rich meals both delay recovery, and repeating the same meal invites more vomiting.
  74. The correct answer is A (Tooth resorption, an idiopathic process in which odontoclasts destroy dentin and enamel): Feline tooth resorption, formerly called feline odontoclastic resorptive lesions, is a common condition in which odontoclasts resorb dentin and enamel, often beginning near the gum line. The cause is not known, so it is called idiopathic. Caries is uncommon in cats, hypoplasia is developmental, and abrasion is mechanical wear.
  75. The correct answer is D (Stop the infusion and correct the rate to 10 mL/hr, then notify the veterinarian): The kitten received about ten times its ordered volume, and crackles suggest fluid overload. The infusion must be stopped, the rate corrected, and the veterinarian informed so treatment such as a diuretic can be ordered. Tapering still overloads the kitten, a diuretic without stopping the pump keeps adding fluid, and a quiet correction hides an error that needs follow up.
  76. The correct answer is A (The 10 unit mark, because 4 units of U-40 is 0.1 mL): U-40 insulin has 40 units per mL, so 4 units is 0.1 mL. A U-100 syringe has 100 units per mL, so 0.1 mL falls on the 10 unit mark. Drawing to 4 units would give only 0.04 mL (1.6 units of U-40), and the other values come from using the ratio wrongly. A mismatch like this is the reason a U-40 syringe is used with U-40 insulin.
  77. The correct answer is A (Warm the lamb gradually to normal temperature, then tube colostrum once it is warm): A cold, limp lamb is hypothermic and cannot digest or suckle. Gradual warming with a dry towel and a safe heat source comes first, then colostrum by tube. Feeding before warming risks aspiration and poor digestion, a very hot bath burns skin, and waiting worsens hypothermia.
  78. The correct answer is B (Chronic osteoarthritic pain, so recommend an exam and a pain scoring tool): Reduced jumping and stiffness that eases with movement is a classic presentation of osteoarthritis, a common chronic pain condition owners mistake for aging. The best response is a veterinary exam and a structured owner pain score, leading to a multimodal plan. Dismissing it delays treatment, nothing suggests recent trauma, and a neurologic cause is not supported by this history.
  79. The correct answer is D (Short leash walks or swimming increased gradually, alongside the weight-loss diet): An obese dog with stiff hips starts with short, low-impact activity such as leash walks or swimming, increased gradually, while diet drives most of the weight loss. Waiting until a fifth of body weight is lost or confining the dog costs muscle and delays progress. Jogging on pavement tempts as a calorie burner but loads stiff joints too soon. Gradual activity alongside the diet protects the joints and keeps the dog moving.
  80. The correct answer is B (Report the 8 percent loss to the veterinarian with the history and a condition score): An 8 percent loss in three months on the same scale despite a normal appetite is a real change that could signal metabolic, GI or other disease, so it is reported to the veterinarian with the history and a body condition score. Calling it scale variation ignores the data, increasing food treats the sign without a cause, and a weight-loss diet assumes the dog was overweight when the exhibit does not say so.

Pausing the CRI, giving oxygen and supporting ventilation protects the patient while the veterinarian is reached, and titrated partial reversal can restore breathing while keeping some analgesia. Mixing it with litter or dropping it in a sharps container tempts as common waste handling, but neither is authorized destruction. Biomedical waste with an unchanged log leaves an unexplained balance. Documented, witnessed disposal keeps the record complete.

The protocol calls for rescue at a score of 5 or higher with at least 3 hours between doses, and both are met. Rechecking later or waiting for 7 prolongs pain once the stated threshold is reached. A 2-0 size is far too large and traumatic for feline skin. Braided material in skin can wick bacteria, and 6-0 is an ophthalmic size that can cut through or fail to hold skin edges together.

The label shows hazards and basic precautions but not the full first aid, storage and spill detail. A brochure is marketing material and is not required to be complete. An occlusive dressing traps moisture over unbroken skin, and cream without pressure relief leaves the cause in place. Massage damages tissue.

  100. Sinus tachycardia and supraventricular tachycardia both keep a steady beat to beat interval, which does not fit the variable pulse strength. Dividing by the body weight gives 50 / 15 = 3.3 mL/kg/hr. Dividing only by weight gives 20 mL/kg, which has no time component, and dividing only by time gives 50 mL/hr, which has no weight component. A smear review lets the technician count nucleated red cells per 100 white cells and apply a correction, and also gives a true manual differential.   105. The correct answer is A (Grade 2, because calculus covers between a quarter and a half of the crown surface): The calculus index grades coverage of the crown: grade 1 is under 25 percent, grade 2 is 25 to 50 percent, and grade 3 is more than 50 percent. Forty percent falls in grade 2. Gingivitis is scored on its own scale and does not change the calculus grade. The index looks at the whole visible crown rather than one region of it, and the heavy grade starts at half the crown, not one third.
  106. The correct answer is B (Laryngospasm, so give oxygen by mask and apply a drop of lidocaine to the folds): Cats have reactive larynges, and touching them at a light plane without topical lidocaine often produces laryngospasm. The folds close, the tube cannot pass, and oxygenation falls. The response is oxygen by mask, a drop of lidocaine on the folds, and a second attempt once the spasm relaxes. Forcing the tube damages the larynx. Atropine addresses heart rate rather than a closed airway, and bronchospasm is a lower airway problem that does not stop the tube at the folds.
  107. Colostrum production pulls calcium from blood faster than the cow can mobilize it. Low magnesium usually causes excitability, ketosis tends to appear weeks later, and toxic mastitis would show an abnormal udder.

Avian eosinophils also stain orange but have round granules. Naming a cell by color alone, or applying mammalian neutrophil morphology, leads to the wrong cell type.

  111. For 10 kg it comes to about 394 kcal per day. An exponent of 1.5 grossly overestimates, a subtracted constant underestimates, and pounds give a wrong value.
  112. The cephalic is small and slow, the saphenous is superficial and low volume, and the femoral is deep, harder to hold off, and prone to hematoma.
  113. Anticonvulsants do not fix low glucose, and the cool, trembling puppy does not suggest heat stroke. A 500 mL bag at 40 mL/hr lasts 12.5 hours, so the bag needs 12 x 12.5 = 150 mg, which is 150 / 100 = 1.5 mL of the 100 mg/mL stock. One hour of drug is only 0.12 mL, and 15 mL or 3.0 mL come from a wrong concentration or a wrong run time.
  115. Leaving it five days lets bacteria grow in a soaked bandage. Weekly changes and a three day interval are both too long for a wound still being debrided, and the problem is the interval rather than the thickness of the layers.

  117. The correct answer is C (6 mg, since 66 lb over 2.2 is 30 kg times 0.2): Pounds are converted to kilograms by dividing by 2.2, so 66 / 2.2 = 30 kg. The dose is then 30 kg x 0.2 mg/kg = 6 mg. Using 66 directly gives 13.2 mg, multiplying by 2.2 gives about 29 mg, and dividing by 4.4 yields 15 kg and a 3 mg dose, all of which come from a conversion mistake.
  118. The correct answer is B (Collect a fresh warm stool or rectal swab and examine a saline smear at once): Trophozoites are fragile and are recognized by their motility, which fades within about 30 minutes of passage and is lost on chilling. A fresh warm sample or rectal swab mixed with a drop of saline is needed. Iodine kills motility, and flotation solutions collapse trophozoites. Heating an old sample to 60 degrees C kills the organisms.
  119. The correct answer is C (0.4 mL, because the 4 mg dose divided by 10 mg per mL gives this volume): Dose in milligrams is 0.2 mg/kg x 20 kg = 4 mg. Volume is dose divided by concentration: 4 mg / 10 mg per mL = 0.4 mL. Drawing 4 mL treats milligrams as milliliters and gives a tenfold overdose. The mental foramen blocks the lower jaw, and a site over the first molar is too far back to reach the nerve that supplies the maxillary canine.

A 20 mL/kg/hr rate can precipitate overload in a cat, and higher doses deepen hypotension. Treating the murmur as irrelevant ignores the added risk.

It does not change the potassium concentration, which is lowered by insulin with dextrose, bicarbonate or by relieving the obstruction.   127.   128. The correct answer is B (Phenobarbital, a long-acting barbiturate with a duration suited to twice daily dosing): Long-term seizure control needs a drug with a long half-life and oral dosing, which is why phenobarbital is used. Amobarbital and butabarbital are intermediate-acting sedatives that wear off too quickly to hold seizure control through a day, and they are not used as veterinary maintenance anticonvulsants. Pentobarbital is a short-acting anesthetic or euthanasia agent. Duration of action is the key difference among these choices.

The sensor was placed correctly, so bending is not the cause. Tube head distance changes sharpness and slight magnification, not a stretched tooth, and a steeper angle gives the opposite error. Correcting the beam to be perpendicular to the bisecting line restores true length.

It is inflated in small steps until the leak stops at 20 cm H2O, using the lowest pressure that seals, so the mucosa is not damaged.   134. The correct answer is C (Excess bilirubin in the plasma stains it yellow, whereas hemolysis would look pink to red): Bilirubin gives plasma a yellow to orange colour that stays clear, so icterus is the most likely cause when the plasma is yellow and not pink. Hemolysis tints plasma pink to red, lipemia makes it cloudy and milky, and carotenoid colour is a normal finding in some large animals but not in dogs. A technician should record the colour because icterus can point to hemolytic, hepatic, or post-hepatic disease.

Teach-back, where the client repeats the instructions in her own words, plus a written schedule such as morning and evening, corrects it and prevents recurrence. Reprinting a label without a conversation leaves the misunderstanding in place, blaming the client does not fix it, and a dose change is a veterinarian decision that does not address the cause.
  136. The correct answer is D (Wet the coat with cool water and run a fan, then stop at about 39.4 C): Cool water on the coat with a fan promotes evaporation and heat loss while peripheral vessels stay open. Ice water causes vasoconstriction that traps heat in the core and causes shivering. Cooling is stopped at about 39.4 C because the temperature keeps falling afterward, and chasing 37 C risks hypothermia. Alcohol on the paw pads covers too little surface to matter.

Bright red pulsing blood means the needle is in the carotid artery, and intra-arterial injection can cause seizures and collapse. A rapid bolus raises the risk of adverse reactions. Subcutaneous or intramuscular flunixin is not the recommended route and can cause tissue damage and infection.

The technician stops work, contains the sample so nobody is exposed, and informs the veterinarian immediately.   139. The correct answer is D (Naloxone, an opioid antagonist that reverses mu receptor effects): Naloxone competitively blocks opioid receptors and reverses respiratory depression from opioids. Doxapram stimulates breathing but does not reverse the opioid. Atipamezole reverses alpha-2 agonists and flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines, and neither acts at opioid receptors. Because naloxone may wear off before the opioid does, the patient must continue to be monitored.

Leaving the prescriber off the label leaves it incomplete. WHMIS applies to workplace chemicals rather than dispensed drugs, and a technician licence number is not a labelling element.   141. The correct answer is C (2 drops each second, from 480 mL/hr times 15 drops/mL divided by 3600 seconds): The set delivers 15 drops per mL, so 480 mL/hr x 15 = 7,200 drops per hour. Dividing by 3,600 seconds per hour gives 2 drops each second, which equals 120 drops per minute.   142. The correct answer is C (2 to 8 degrees C, checked with a min-max thermometer inside the unit): Vaccines and most biologics are kept at 2 to 8 degrees C, and a min-max thermometer shows whether the unit drifted out of range. Storage that reaches 0 degrees C risks freezing and damaging the vaccine. Ranges that go up to 10 degrees C allow drift that speeds loss of potency. Freezer temperatures destroy most inactivated and modified-live products.
