  145. The correct answer is B (Wrap in a towel and support the hindquarters, then view the cheek teeth with an otoscope): Rabbits have a long, narrow oral cavity and a fragile spine, and a kicking rabbit can fracture its back. A towel wrap with the hindquarters supported keeps it calm, and an otoscope cone or small oral speculum with light lets you see the cheek teeth and any sharp spurs. Looking only at the incisors misses the likely problem. Holding the head alone leaves the hindquarters free to kick, and feeling the cheeks cannot show spurs on the teeth.
  146. The correct answer is D (Rinse debris with sterile saline, then swab deep in the wound before antiseptic): Culture must reflect organisms from deep tissue, not skin flora. Surface debris is rinsed with sterile saline, and the swab is placed deep in the wound without touching skin, before antiseptics or antibiotics can inhibit growth. The swab then goes into transport medium. Swabbing skin edges or surface discharge gives contaminants, not pathogens.
  147. The correct answer is B (Fast briefly or not at all, since rabbits cannot vomit and long fasts risk stasis): Rabbits cannot vomit, so a long fast gives no safety benefit, and it harms the gut by slowing motility and causing stasis. Short or no fasting is used, and food is offered again soon after recovery.
  148. The correct answer is C (Anemia or poor perfusion such as blood loss, so check PCV and perfusion): Pale membranes mean too few red cells or constricted peripheral vessels, as in anemia, hemorrhage or shock, and prolonged capillary refill supports reduced perfusion. After a car accident with a fast heart rate, blood loss or shock must be assumed. Cyanosis gives blue-grey membranes, sepsis early on gives brick-red, and pain alone should not be assumed until bleeding is ruled out.
  149. The correct answer is C (Treat it as nonsterile, then rewrap and resterilize the contents before any later use): Once a sterile pack is opened, its barrier is broken and the contents are exposed to air and dust, so sterility can no longer be assured even if nothing was touched. The contents must be rewrapped and resterilized. A time limit, new tape, or a low-risk case does not restore a sterile barrier.
  150. The correct answer is B (Place a sterile non-adherent pad and a firm layered wrap, leaving the toes visible): Direct pressure with a pad and a firm layered bandage controls most limb bleeding. Leaving the toes exposed lets the technician spot swelling or coolness that signals a bandage that is too tight. A tourniquet risks ischemia and nerve injury and is a last resort for bleeding that pressure cannot control. Loose cotton does not compress the vessels.
  151. The correct answer is B (Atipamezole, an alpha-2 receptor antagonist that reverses sedation and analgesia): Dexmedetomidine is an alpha-2 agonist, so its effects are reversed by the alpha-2 antagonist atipamezole. Naloxone and flumazenil act at opioid and benzodiazepine receptors, and neostigmine reverses neuromuscular blockers by raising acetylcholine rather than acting at alpha-2 receptors.
  152. The correct answer is D (1.9 mL, from a 2.8 mg dose divided by 1.5 mg/mL): Dose = 28 kg x 0.1 mg/kg = 2.8 mg. Volume = 2.8 mg divided by 1.5 mg/mL = 1.87 mL, rounded to 1.9 mL. Using 1 mg/kg gives 28 mg and 18.7 mL, a tenfold overdose. Multiplying by the concentration gives 4.2 mL, and inverting the fraction gives about 0.5 mL, which would underdose the dog.
  153. The correct answer is A (Ethylene oxide gas works at low temperature and penetrates lumens, then needs full aeration): Ethylene oxide gas sterilizes at low temperature and reaches long lumens, which suits heat sensitive scopes, though items need a full aeration period afterward. Steam above 60 degrees C and dry heat would warp the plastic and rubber components. A short chemical soak is disinfection, not sterilization, and ultraviolet light cannot penetrate long narrow channels.
  154. The correct answer is D (Clean off debris with detergent, then apply potassium peroxymonosulfate for its full labelled contact time): Parvovirus is a non-enveloped virus that resists alcohol, so a product such as potassium peroxymonosulfate or diluted bleach is needed. Organic matter protects virus particles, so cleaning with detergent comes first, and the product must stay wet for the labelled contact time. Spraying over soil gives a false sense of safety.
  155. The correct answer is B (Discard the syringe and draw each drug into its own syringe unless compatibility is confirmed): Cloudiness or particles mean the drugs have precipitated, so the mixture is incompatible and may cause emboli or an unpredictable dose. Diazepam is well known for precipitating when mixed with other drugs. The syringe must be discarded and the drugs drawn separately unless a reliable source confirms compatibility. A flush, warming, or drawing off the clear liquid cannot restore the intended doses.
  156. The correct answer is A (Reduce the isoflurane and give manual breaths with oxygen, then recheck depth): Falling heart rate and respiratory rate together point to excessive depth. The correct response is to lower the vaporizer, support ventilation with oxygen, and check temperature, since small mammals cool quickly. Atropine does not correct depth, and a circuit change does nothing for the depth problem. Lowering the vaporizer without supporting breathing leaves a respiratory rate of 5 untreated while waiting ten minutes.
  157. The correct answer is D (48 mL/hr, from 320 mL deficit plus 200 mL maintenance plus 60 mL losses): Deficit is 8 kg x 0.04 x 1,000 = 320 mL. Maintenance for 12 hours is 8 kg x 50 mL/kg/day divided by 2 = 200 mL. Adding 60 mL of losses gives 580 mL, and 580 divided by 12 is about 48 mL/hr. Leaving out losses, doubling maintenance, or shrinking the deficit gives a wrong rate.
  158. The correct answer is D (Cats have low glucuronyl transferase activity, so conjugation and clearance are slow): Cats have limited glucuronyl transferase activity, so drugs cleared by glucuronidation, such as aspirin and acetaminophen, are eliminated slowly and can accumulate or cause toxicity. The longer interval reflects slow clearance. The other explanations misplace the species difference in absorption, excretion or binding, and one would predict a shorter interval.
  159. The correct answer is B (Fluid overload; reduce to the 8 mL/hr maintenance rate and alert the veterinarian): Maintenance is 4 kg x 50 mL/kg/day = 200 mL/day, or about 8 mL/hr, so 17 mL/hr is double. In a cat with cardiomyopathy, extra fluid raises the risk of pulmonary edema, shown by tachypnea and crackles. The rate should be reduced to maintenance and the veterinarian told. Stopping fluids and giving diuretics is the veterinarian's decision, 2 mL/hr leaves out the cat's weight, and 12 mL/hr uses a 16 hour day.
  160. The correct answer is C (The drying phase was cut short, so condensate stayed in the wrap): Steam condenses as it cools, and the drying phase lets that moisture evaporate before the load is removed. Pulling packs at gauge zero leaves them damp, and a damp wrap wicks bacteria through, so the pack is no longer considered sterile. A short exposure time affects sterility rather than dampness, high temperature does not create moisture, and wraps stored in humid air would not stay wet after a full drying phase.
  161. The correct answer is C (0.5 mL, from a 10 mg total dose divided by the 20 mg/mL concentration): The maximum dose is 5 kg x 2 mg/kg = 10 mg. A 2% solution holds 20 mg/mL, so the volume is 10 mg / 20 mg/mL = 0.5 mL, which is split among the nerve blocks. Treating 2% as 2 mg/mL gives 5 mL, a tenfold overdose in a cat. Using the canine 4 mg/kg limit doubles the dose, and cats are particularly sensitive to lidocaine toxicity.
  162. The correct answer is A (Follows the order under the veterinarian's authority, who remains responsible for drug and dose): Technicians work under the supervision and orders of a veterinarian, who is responsible for the diagnosis, drug choice and dose, and the exact tasks a technician may perform depend on the province. The technician follows a clear order and raises any concern. Choosing drugs or doses alone, taking on the prescriber's responsibility, or assuming scope is the same in all provinces goes beyond a technician role.
  163. The correct answer is B (Surgical mask and eye protection with gloves and a water-resistant gown or apron): Ultrasonic scaling creates an aerosol and splatter containing saliva, blood and bacteria, so the operator wears a mask, eye protection, gloves and a water-resistant gown or apron. Leaving out the eye protection or the mask exposes mucous membranes to the spray. A lead apron is for radiography and does nothing against droplets, and the scaler's ultrasonic frequency is above hearing range.
  164. The correct answer is C (Vagal reflex from traction on the globe, so ask the surgeon to release traction and report): Traction on the globe triggers the oculocardiac reflex, a vagal response that slows the heart, and the chart shows the fall starting at that moment. Asking the surgeon to release traction removes the cause and often restores the rate, and the veterinarian is told so further treatment can be ordered. A light plane would raise the rate rather than slow it, and cooling is slow and would not match the sudden drop. Giving atropine on the technician's own judgment while traction continues leaves the cause untouched.
  165. The correct answer is C (Two pairs of chemotherapy-rated gloves with a closed-front disposable gown, eye protection and a respirator): A cytotoxic spill can expose skin, eyes and airways, so the technician wears two pairs of chemotherapy-rated gloves, an impermeable closed-front gown, eye protection and a respirator. Single gloves give thinner skin protection than the double layer needed for a spill. A surgical mask does not filter fine aerosol or powder from a broken vial. Skipping eye and respiratory protection leaves the face exposed during cleanup.
  166. The correct answer is D (Alert the veterinarian at once, since a retained sponge likely needs surgery): A mottled density with a radiopaque marker in a spayed dog with abdominal pain and fever is highly suspicious for a retained surgical sponge, even when the count was recorded as correct, because counts can be wrong. The veterinarian needs to know now so surgery can be planned. Calling it normal, starting antibiotics alone or delaying for repeat imaging leaves the foreign body in place.
  167. The correct answer is D (Record date, patient and amount withdrawn and the withdrawing person and new balance immediately): The log is completed at the time of each withdrawal with the date, patient, amount, person withdrawing and the new balance, so the count can be reconciled at any moment. Working out the balance at day end lets errors build up between entries. Leaving the patient to the medical record breaks the trace inside the log itself. Waiting until the vial is finished loses every earlier withdrawal.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Has the appetite increased, stayed the same or dropped since weight loss began?): Weight loss with a normal or ravenous appetite points toward diabetes mellitus or hyperthyroidism, while a poor appetite points toward kidney disease or another illness, so appetite is the most discriminating next question. GI signs and urinary tract signs are worth recording but do not separate the leading metabolic causes. Toxin exposure is a possible cause of polyuria but is far less likely without clues.
  2. The correct answer is D (Report it as markedly prolonged and alert the veterinarian immediately; warming makes it valid): Warming the ACT tube to 37 degrees C is correct technique, so the 190 second result is valid and clearly prolonged, which fits loss of vitamin K dependent clotting factors. A bleeding patient needs the veterinarian informed at once so a PT, PTT and vitamin K1 therapy can proceed. A 300 second cutoff is not the reference limit and delaying reporting risks harm.
  3. The correct answer is A (It collects waste gas from the pop-off valve and vents it away from staff): The scavenging system carries waste gas released through the pop-off valve and the ventilator exhaust to a safe outlet, which protects staff from chronic exposure. Carbon dioxide is removed by the soda lime canister. The pop-off valve relieves pressure, but without scavenging the gas would enter the room. Fresh gas is not filtered of vapour, since the vapour is the anesthetic being delivered.
  4. The correct answer is C (Request sedation and analgesia from the veterinarian, then position with ties and sandbags): A painful fractured limb cannot be positioned by hand without stress, injury and exposure to staff, so sedation and analgesia authorized by the veterinarian, plus positioning aids, give a safe and straight view. Holding the limb puts hands near the beam and gloves are not rated for the primary beam. Forceful towel restraint hurts the animal and risks bites, and delaying leaves a fracture untreated.
  5. The correct answer is B (Tapeworm segments shed egg packets intermittently, so diagnosis relies on seeing proglottids): Dipylidium caninum proglottids crawl out of the anus and release egg packets, so eggs are shed unevenly and flotation is often negative. The segments themselves, which look like rice or cucumber seeds, provide the diagnosis. A negative float does not exclude tapeworm, and switching solution does not solve the problem.
  6. The correct answer is C (Report to the veterinarian and prepare a urinary catheter to decompress the bladder): A firm, painful bladder with straining and little urine suggests urethral obstruction or retention, which can progress to bladder rupture or postrenal azotemia. The veterinarian must be told promptly, and a catheter is prepared to decompress the bladder. Firm expression risks rupture, withholding fluids does not relieve the retention, and waiting for several hours leaves an obstructed patient untreated.
  7. The correct answer is B (Nociception under a stable plane, so tell the veterinarian and ask for a rescue opioid): A rise in heart rate and blood pressure at incision with stable depth and no movement is a sympathetic response to surgical pain, so the patient needs analgesia, such as a fentanyl bolus, given as ordered. Deepening the inhalant adds hypotension and may blunt the response without treating pain. Fluids address volume, not nociception, and too-deep anesthesia usually lowers heart rate and blood pressure.
  8. The correct answer is D (Stop using it and follow the SDS for protective equipment and disposal, then mix fresh): An expired sterilant cannot be trusted to disinfect or sterilize. The WHMIS label and SDS give the hazards, protective equipment, and disposal instructions, which are followed when discarding it before fresh solution is mixed. Strip testing or topping up an expired solution does not make it valid, and pouring it out without the SDS may expose staff or break disposal rules.
  9. The correct answer is B (Fearful and may bite if pushed, so give space and use low-stress handling): A stiff body, closed mouth, hard stare, low stiff tail and whale eye are fear signals, and a frozen dog may bite if it feels cornered. Giving space, approaching slowly and using low-stress handling is safest. No toy or object is described, so guarding does not fit. Dominance theory has been discredited, and firm restraint on a fearful dog raises the bite risk.
  10. The correct answer is C (Remove the blanket and run a fan, then tepid-wet the coat and stop at 39.5 C): Cooling is done with removal of heat sources, airflow and room temperature water, and stopped near 39.5 degrees C to avoid overshoot into hypothermia. Ice water causes vasoconstriction and shivering, wet towels under a blanket trap heat, and acepromazine impairs temperature control.
  11. The correct answer is C (It limits open-mouth panting, so the dog may overheat or become hypoxic): Brachycephalic dogs have narrowed airways and depend on open-mouth breathing to cool themselves and to move enough air when stressed. A muzzle that holds the mouth closed or restricts panting can cause overheating, hypoxia and respiratory distress. Muzzles are used with caution, with close observation and short use, or other restraint and sedation are chosen.
  12. The correct answer is A (Alert the veterinarian now and assess the puppy while collecting time, amount and the pill imprint): An unknown ingestion is time sensitive, so the veterinarian is told right away while the technician checks the puppy and gathers the time, the number of pills possibly missing and the imprint. Charcoal is given only on veterinary direction and may be unsuitable for some drugs. Reporting at later rounds wastes the treatment window. Identifying the pills first delays the call, since the imprint can be gathered at the same time.
  13. The correct answer is C (It supports Microsporum canis ringworm but culture confirms it, and people can catch it): Apple-green fluorescence on hair shafts supports Microsporum canis, but only some strains fluoresce, and crusts or ointments can glow falsely, so fungal culture or PCR confirms the diagnosis. Dermatophytes are zoonotic, so gloves and hand hygiene matter. Skipping culture, calling it bacterial, or calling the cat safe all misread the test.
  14. The correct answer is C (Tell the veterinarian in charge, recount all controlled drugs and start the required loss reporting): A missing controlled drug is a possible loss or theft and must be dealt with immediately and openly. The person in charge is told, every controlled drug is recounted to confirm the gap, and the clinic follows the reporting steps required by law and by the provincial regulator. Waiting, altering the record or running an informal inquiry first risks hiding diversion and puts the staff and clinic license at risk.
  15. The correct answer is A (Stop the transfusion and keep the catheter open with saline, then tell the veterinarian): Fever and tremors early in a transfusion signal a possible transfusion reaction, and the first step is to stop the transfusion, maintain IV access with saline, and notify the veterinarian. Continuing, even at a slower rate, gives more of a potentially incompatible product. Sampling for crossmatch can be done after the unit is stopped.
  16. The correct answer is A (Opioid-induced hyperthermia; notify the veterinarian, cool passively and recheck the temperature): Cats given pure mu agonists such as hydromorphone can develop a temperature rise that typically peaks within a few hours. This cat's score of 0 argues against pain, and a rise from 38.5 to 40.8 C after the drug points to a drug effect. The veterinarian should be told, passive cooling started and the temperature rechecked. More opioid would not help, panting at this rate is not respiratory depression, and a moved cage would not explain the fast rise.
  17. The correct answer is D (850 U/L, from multiplying the diluted reading by the dilution factor of 10): A 1:10 dilution is 1 part serum in 10 total parts, so the dilution factor is 10. The patient value is the displayed 85 U/L times 10, which is 850 U/L. Using 9 gives 765, using 11 gives 935, and dividing by 10 gives 8.5, which would make the dilution lower the patient's enzyme activity.
  18. The correct answer is C (Mix 4 mL of 50% dextrose and 4 mL of saline to make 25%): The dose is 4 kg x 2 mL/kg = 8 mL of 25%. Diluting 50% dextrose 1:1 halves it to 25%, so half the final volume is dextrose: 4 mL plus 4 mL saline. Undiluted 50% dextrose is hyperosmolar and damages veins, and the other mixtures give the wrong volume or the wrong concentration.
  19. The correct answer is D (Bandage too tight, so remove it and assess toes and reapply with lighter padding): Swollen, cool toes the morning after a bandage point to restricted blood flow from a bandage that is too tight, and delay risks tissue damage. The bandage is removed, the toes and skin assessed, and a new one applied with even pressure. Slipping would not cool the toes by itself, and repositioning leaves the compression in place. A wet outer layer does not cause cool, swollen toes, and adding layers to a tight bandage increases the pressure.
  20. The correct answer is C (Transduction then transmission then modulation and finally perception): Transduction converts the tissue injury into an electrical signal at the nociceptor, transmission carries it along the nerve to the spinal cord and brain, modulation changes the signal in the spinal cord and brain, and perception is the conscious experience of pain. Analgesic drugs can act at each step. The other orders reverse steps that depend on one another, for example perception cannot occur before the signal has been transduced.
  21. The correct answer is C (Keep her standing and cover exposed tissue with moist clean cloths, then call the veterinarian): Rumen visible through the incision is an eventration, which is an emergency needing veterinary repair. Keeping the cow standing limits further tissue from pushing out, and a moist clean covering protects what is exposed. Replacing contaminated rumen by hand invites peritonitis. Lying her down raises abdominal pressure, and scrub solution irritates exposed tissue.
  22. The correct answer is D (30 mL, because 3 mL of tissue multiplied by 10 gives the fixative volume): A 10 to 1 ratio of fixative to tissue means 10 mL of formalin for every 1 mL of tissue, so 3 mL x 10 = 30 mL. Dividing by ten or simply adding ten does not give the required excess of fixative. Too little formalin leaves the center of the specimen poorly fixed and unreadable for the pathologist.
  23. The correct answer is D (Clip a wide flank area and scrub aseptically, then infiltrate a line block before draping): A standing flank laparotomy uses sedation, a wide clip, a surgical scrub and an infiltrated line block so the mare stays on her feet. An epidural numbs the rear of the body and does not cover a flank incision. Casting the mare down belongs to a general anesthesia plan. A narrow clip leaves hair close enough to contaminate the field once the incision is made.
  24. The correct answer is A (Allodynia is pain from a harmless stimulus, and hyperalgesia is increased pain from a painful stimulus): Allodynia is pain from a stimulus that is normally harmless, such as light touch, and hyperalgesia is a stronger pain response to a stimulus that is already painful. The patient described shows both, flinching at a light stroke and crying at a firm pinch. Pain that outlasts healing describes chronic pain, which is a different idea. Reduced response is not allodynia, and swapping the two terms reverses their meaning.
  25. The correct answer is A (About 141 mg per day, so give one 75 mg tablet twice daily): Each dose is 32 kg x 2.2 mg/kg = 70.4 mg, and twice daily makes 140.8 mg per day. One 75 mg tablet per dose is 2.3 mg/kg, which is very close. Giving one tablet once daily halves the intended total. Giving two tablets per dose is 150 mg, nearly double the dose. Multiplying 70 lb by 2.2 treats pounds as kilograms and gives an overdose.
  26. The correct answer is D (Worsening hypoxemia; alert the veterinarian and prepare to escalate oxygen therapy): The readings fall from 96% to 88% while the respiratory rate climbs from 40 to 56, which shows real, progressive hypoxemia that flow-by oxygen is not controlling. The veterinarian must be told now so oxygen can be escalated, for example with an oxygen cage or intubation. Calling the trend stable ignores the fall. Raising the flow and waiting an hour delays escalation, and wrapping a dyspneic cat adds stress without treating the cause.
  27. The correct answer is A (The 2 L bag, since 1.5 L is needed and 2 L is next up): The bag volume is 25 kg x 60 mL/kg = 1,500 mL, or 1.5 L. The nearest available size at or above that value is 2 L, which allows a full sigh breath without the bag being emptied. A 1 L bag is too small and a 0.5 L bag matches a tidal volume rather than a reservoir. A 3 L bag is oversized, so the bag excursions become hard to see.
  28. The correct answer is C (Report the murmur to the veterinarian and watch ECG, blood pressure and oxygenation closely): A new loud murmur may reflect valve disease or a change in cardiac function, so the veterinarian is told right away and monitoring of ECG, blood pressure and oxygenation is increased even if vitals are stable. Recording it only leaves the veterinarian unaware during the procedure. Reporting after the dental removes the chance to adjust the anesthetic plan. Furosemide is a veterinary decision and may harm a patient that is under anesthesia.
  29. The correct answer is A (0.5 mL, because a 10 kg dog needs 1 mg at 2 mg/mL): Convert first: 22 lb divided by 2.2 equals 10 kg. The dose is 10 kg x 0.1 mg/kg = 1 mg, and 1 mg divided by 2 mg/mL gives 0.5 mL. Using 22 as kilograms gives 2.2 mg and 1.1 mL, a classic overdose error. Dividing by 2.2 twice gives a 4.5 kg patient and an underdose of 0.23 mL, and dividing 2 by 1 simply inverts the ratio and gives 2.0 mL.
  30. The correct answer is B (Overgrown cheek teeth with sharp points, caused by too little abrasive hay wearing the teeth): Guinea pig cheek teeth grow continuously and are worn down by chewing hay, so low hay intake lets them overgrow and form sharp spurs. These cut the cheek and tongue and cause drooling, a wet chin and slow chewing. The diet supplies vitamin C daily, which makes scurvy less likely. Gastric stasis does not explain the drooling, and no facial swelling is described.
  31. The correct answer is A (Swollen, cool or discoloured toes with the dog chewing at the bandage or in pain): A bandage that is too tight impairs circulation, so the toes become swollen, cool or discoloured and the dog chews at the bandage or shows pain. A damp, foul-smelling bandage points to contamination or infection, which needs a visit but is a different problem. Slipping shows looseness. Pink, warm toes with a calm dog are normal, and the owner is told to bring the dog in when the tight signs appear.
  32. The correct answer is D (The circulating technician, who is nonsterile and moves around the room supporting the team): The circulator stays nonsterile, so the circulator can open packs, adjust the lights, document counts, and fetch supplies without risking the field. A scrub technician is gowned and gloved and handles the instruments. The anesthetist focuses on the patient, and a surgical assistant works inside the sterile field, so neither matches the duties described.
  33. The correct answer is C (About 30 hours, because five half-lives clear nearly all of the drug): Each half-life removes half of what remains, so after one half-life 50% is left, then 25%, 12.5%, 6.25% and 3.1%. After about five half-lives (5 x 6 = 30 hours) over 95% is gone. Two half-lives leaves 25% and one leaves half. The same five half-lives estimate applies to the time needed to reach steady state with repeated dosing.
  34. The correct answer is B (Peel the flaps apart and hold edges back, then let the inner packet drop): The circulator is nonsterile, so only the outside of the pouch may be touched. Peeling the flaps outward and holding the edges away lets the inner packet fall or be lifted without any contact with the wrapper edge. Cutting and reaching in contaminates the packet, a scrubbed person must never touch the wrapper, and laying the pouch on the field puts a nonsterile surface on the sterile area.
  35. The correct answer is B (Change the exhausted absorbent and flush the circuit with fresh oxygen): A baseline that does not return to zero with inspired CO2 shows the animal is rebreathing CO2. A cool canister with violet granules after many hours of use means the absorbent is exhausted, and a working canister would be warm. The fix is to change the absorbent and flush the circuit. More flow only masks the problem, a lower vaporizer setting does not address it, and doxapram would not remove inspired CO2.
  36. The correct answer is D (Stop the fluid bolus and provide oxygen, then alert the veterinarian at once): The new tachypnea, crackles and cough after a bolus in an HCM cat indicate volume overload and pulmonary edema. The fluids should stop, oxygen should be given without stressing the cat, and the veterinarian called. Slowing the fluid still adds volume, a radiograph in a distressed cat is risky until it is stabilized, and diuretics need a veterinary order.
  37. The correct answer is A (Centrifuge with the coverslip in place, then lift it straight up onto a slide): In centrifugal flotation the coverslip rides on the meniscus during the spin so that ova and cysts that float come to the glass. After centrifugation the coverslip is lifted straight up and placed on a slide for examination. Removing the coverslip first, dragging it sideways, or skipping the slide each lose the material that floated to the glass.
  38. The correct answer is B (Keep it in its original amber vial inside the closed cabinet at room temperature): The label has two requirements, protection from light and storage between 15 and 25 degrees C, and the original amber vial in the closed cabinet meets both. Refrigeration falls below the labelled range. Foil beside the autoclave protects from light but exposes the vial to heat. A clear bottle on the window shelf exposes it to light and sun warmth.
  39. The correct answer is B (It is compared with breakpoints to classify the isolate as susceptible, resistant or intermediate): The zone diameter is read in millimetres and compared with published breakpoints for that drug and organism to give a susceptible, intermediate or resistant category. Zones are not comparable between different drugs because diffusion rates differ, and the zone does not count killed bacteria. A clear zone suggests inhibition, which is susceptibility.
  40. The correct answer is D (Open the pop-off valve to release the trapped gas, since pressure can injure the lungs): A closed pop-off valve with gas still flowing traps gas, so circuit pressure climbs and can cause barotrauma and reduce cardiac output. Opening the valve releases the excess gas to the scavenger straight away. Squeezing the bag adds pressure. Lowering the flow leaves the pressure high for minutes, and changing the vaporizer does nothing about a closed valve.
  41. The correct answer is D (Regular insulin is short acting, NPH is intermediate acting and glargine is long acting): Regular insulin has a rapid onset and short duration, about 3 to 6 hours, and is the one used intravenously. NPH is intermediate acting, and glargine is a long-acting analogue with a slow, flat effect. Each distractor swaps two categories, which could cause serious hypoglycemia or poor control if used when planning dosing intervals.
  42. The correct answer is A (1.2 L/min, because 6 kg x 200 mL/kg/min is 1200 mL/min): Flow equals 6 kg x 200 mL/kg/min = 1200 mL/min, which is 1.2 L/min. A decimal slip in either direction gives a flow that is ten times too low or ten times too high. Pounds are not used in this formula, which is built around kilograms.
  43. The correct answer is C (The sensor sat too far toward the crown, so the root apex fell beyond its edge): The exposure was acceptable, and each image shows the crown and coronal root but not the apex. That means the sensor edge lay near the gum line, so the apex fell off the sensor. Move the sensor deeper toward the palate and centre the tooth with the apex well inside the edge. A steep angle would shorten the whole tooth, wrong horizontal angle would overlap teeth, and a reversed sensor would degrade the whole image.
  44. The correct answer is A (The cylinder pressure gauge, which reads tank pressure before the regulator): The cylinder pressure gauge shows the pressure in the oxygen tank before the regulator, and the remaining volume is estimated from it. The flowmeter shows the flow rate delivered, not what is left. The pipeline gauge reads the wall supply, which is not the cylinder. The manometer reads pressure inside the breathing circuit.
  45. The correct answer is C (Lower the head below the body and suction the pharynx, then keep the cuff inflated): With a cuffed tube in place, the airway is protected only while the cuff stays inflated, and lowering the head lets fluid drain out of the mouth and away from the trachea while suction clears the pharynx. Deflating the cuff allows aspiration, and raising the head encourages reflux to travel toward the airway. Extubating during regurgitation removes the only protection the airway has.
  46. The correct answer is C (Onychectomy, which amputates the third phalanx together with the claw): Onychectomy is the amputation of the third phalanx with the attached claw, so it removes the nail growth tissue permanently and is a major surgery requiring pain control. Tendonectomy leaves the bone and claw in place. Arthrodesis fuses a joint, and trimming only shortens the visible nail.
  47. The correct answer is A (A supravital stain such as new methylene blue, which highlights nuclei in a wet mount): A supravital stain such as new methylene blue is added to a drop of wet sediment to accent nuclei and cell structure. Wright stain needs an air-dried and fixed smear, so adding it to wet fluid fails. Gram stain is for bacteria, and Sudan III stains lipid, not nuclei.
  48. The correct answer is A (1.6 mL, because the dose is 79.2 mg and the vial holds 50 mg per millilitre): Dose = 4.4 mg/kg x 18 kg = 79.2 mg. Volume = 79.2 mg / 50 mg/mL = 1.58, rounded to 1.6 mL. Using 2.2 mg/kg gives 0.8 mL, dividing 18 by 4.4 gives 4.1, and a decimal slip would give 16 mL, a tenfold overdose.
  49. The correct answer is C (How soon after dosing it began, and whether the puppy ate and acts normally): The reaction itself comes first: how soon after dosing the vomiting began, how often it happened and whether the puppy is eating and acting normally. This tells the veterinarian whether it is a mild reaction or needs a recheck. The dose and weight are already recorded at the clinic. Other medications may matter later, and vaccine history does not bear on this event.
  50. The correct answer is D (About 5 to 7 days, because the combination raises the risk of gastric ulceration): Corticosteroids and NSAIDs both impair gastric mucosal protection, and together they markedly increase ulcer and perforation risk. Standard teaching is a washout of roughly 5 to 7 days between the two classes. Giving them together or waiting only a day exposes the dog to that risk, while four weeks is the time for adrenal recovery, which is not the concern here.
  51. The correct answer is D (It treats chronic neuropathic or osteoarthritic pain, and sedation is the common side effect): Gabapentin acts on calcium channels in the spinal cord and is used for chronic and neuropathic pain, including osteoarthritis in cats. Sedation and a wobbly gait are the usual early effects and often ease after a few days. It does not block cyclooxygenase, so it is not an NSAID. It does not bind opioid receptors, and kidney injury at usual doses is not its common effect.
  52. The correct answer is A (Date, patient and drug with amount used and balance and the giver's signature): Each use is logged with the date, patient, drug, amount used, balance remaining and the signature of the person who gave it, so every use can be traced and stock reconciled. A balance recorded only monthly leaves long gaps where losses cannot be found. A log without the patient cannot link a use to an animal. A manager signature at month end does not identify who handled the drug.
  53. The correct answer is A (Leakage from a perforated bowel into the abdomen can cause severe peritonitis and adhesions): Free gas means the bowel wall has probably perforated. Barium that spills into the peritoneal cavity is very irritating and can cause peritonitis and adhesions, so it is avoided; a water-soluble iodinated medium is used if contrast is needed. Hyperosmolarity is a concern for iodinated agents, not barium. Barium is not absorbed, and it is a positive medium.
  54. The correct answer is D (Mostly prerenal azotemia that improved with rehydration; BUN is still slightly high): Concentrated urine on admission and a fall in creatinine into the reference range with fluids point to a mostly prerenal cause, since rehydration restored kidney perfusion. The BUN is still a little high, so a recheck is advised. An irreversible renal cause would not improve this much, obstruction needs urine production findings, and diet alone does not move creatinine.
  55. The correct answer is B (Alert the surgeon and have a hemostat and ligature material ready for the vessel): Bleeding that keeps welling after firm pressure usually comes from a vessel that needs clamping or ligation, so the surgeon is told at once and a hemostat and suture are ready. More pressure for several minutes has already shown it is not working. Closing skin over an uncontrolled bleed hides it and can form a hematoma. Flushing does not control a vessel, and the surgeon still needs to know.
  56. The correct answer is D (Check the incision twice daily and restrict activity to leash walks for 10 to 14 days): Skin and body wall incisions need about 10 to 14 days to seal, so activity stays limited to short leash walks and the incision is inspected twice daily for redness, swelling or discharge. Feeling better is largely due to analgesia and does not mean tissues have healed. Ointments, shampoo and wet incisions encourage licking, irritation and infection unless the veterinarian specifically directs them.
  57. The correct answer is C (Complicated crown fracture with pulp exposed; book radiographs soon for root canal or extraction): A pink or red spot at the centre of a fractured tooth is exposed pulp, so this is a complicated crown fracture. Exposed pulp is painful and lets bacteria reach the root canal, leading to pulp death and abscess. The tooth needs radiographs and treatment by root canal or extraction soon. A sealant over open pulp does not protect it, and dentin or enamel injuries do not show a pink spot.

The oozing laceration with normal pulse is stable. The cat with a mild nasal discharge breathes quietly and is eating. The lame dog has normal breathing and pink gums and can wait.

Stopping suddenly can trigger severe seizures. Waiting six months leaves a possible adverse effect unreviewed. Over 14 days that is 3 x 14 = 42 tablets. Counting once daily gives 21, ignoring the half tablet gives 28, and counting three doses a day gives 63. Each of those would leave the cat short or send home surplus tablets.
  61. The correct answer is A (Remove the bandage and notify the veterinarian because the toes suggest poor circulation): Swollen toes and a cold paw below a bandage are classic signs that it is too tight and is restricting venous and arterial flow. Prompt removal and reapplication prevent tissue necrosis, and the veterinarian must be told. Elevation, extra padding and heat over the bandage do not relieve the compression and may worsen it.
  62. The correct answer is C (50 g, found by dividing 175 kcal by 3.5 kcal per gram): The daily kcal divided by the food's energy density in kcal per gram gives grams, so 175 divided by 3.5 equals 50 g, and 50 g times 3.5 returns 175 kcal. Using the canned food row gives about 194 g, but the exhibit says canned food is not used. Multiplying gives 612 g, an impossible amount for a cat. Dividing by body weight gives 25 g and ignores the food's energy density.
  63. The correct answer is C (Divide red cell height by total column height without the buffy coat: 40 percent): PCV is the red cell column divided by the whole column from the base of the red cells to the top of the plasma, with the buffy coat excluded. Here 12 divided by 30 is 0.40, or 40 percent. Including the buffy coat gives 13 over 30, or 43 percent, which overstates the red cells. Dividing by plasma alone or reading the plasma fraction uses the wrong portion of the tube.
  64. The correct answer is B (1.2 mL, found by calculating 1 percent of 120 g body weight): One percent of 120 g is 0.01 x 120 = 1.2 g, which is about 1.2 mL of blood. Using 10 percent gives 12 mL, which is far more than a budgerigar can safely lose. A decimal slip gives 0.12 mL and halving gives 0.6 mL, neither of which matches the stated 1 percent limit.
  65. The correct answer is C (Record patient and dose plus date and balance in a bound log, drug locked): Opioids and ketamine are controlled under the Controlled Drugs and Substances Act, so each use must be traceable from the vial to the patient. The log is completed at the time of use with the patient, dose, date, remaining balance and signatures, and the drugs are stored locked. Vial-level records, leaving out ketamine, or weekly reconstruction would all leave gaps that cannot be reconciled with counts.
  66. The correct answer is D (A phenothiazine that blocks dopamine and alpha-1 receptors, sedating without pain relief): Acepromazine is a phenothiazine tranquilizer. It blocks dopamine and alpha-1 adrenergic receptors, giving calm sedation with vasodilation but no analgesia. Benzodiazepines act through GABA, alpha-2 agonists give sedation and analgesia with bradycardia, and opioids such as butorphanol provide pain relief, so none of those describes acepromazine.
  67. The correct answer is A (Step behind a lead barrier or leave the room, well away from the tube): Radiation protection rests on distance, time and shielding, and intensity falls with the square of distance, so leaving the room or using a barrier gives the largest cut. A second apron adds little and keeps staff in scatter. Lead gloves are rated for scatter, not the primary beam. Doubling mA while halving time keeps mAs the same, so scatter produced is the same.
  68. The correct answer is C (0.2 mL, because 2% is 20 mg/mL and the dose is 4 mg): A 1% solution holds 10 mg/mL, so 2% holds 20 mg/mL. The dose is 4 kg x 1 mg/kg = 4 mg, and 4 mg divided by 20 mg/mL equals 0.2 mL. Reading 2% as 2 mg/mL gives 2 mL, a tenfold overdose in a species sensitive to lidocaine. Using 200 mg/mL gives 0.02 mL, and using 2 mg/kg gives 0.4 mL, which does not match the order.

With ventilation unchanged and pressure falling at the same time, a sudden drop in cardiac output or a pulmonary embolus is the explanation. Hyperventilation would require a change in settings.   70. The correct answer is D (42 teeth with the formula 2 x (I 3/3 C 1/1 P 4/4 M 2/3)): The adult dog has 42 permanent teeth: per side, three incisors, one canine, four premolars on top and bottom, and two upper and three lower molars. The adult cat has 30, and the puppy's deciduous set has 28 with no molars. A formula with three molars in each arch is incorrect for the dog.

The veterinarian must know immediately so the cat can be stabilized and the obstruction relieved.   72. The correct answer is D (A seroma, so restrict activity and keep the collar on and report heat or pain): A soft, fluctuant, cool, painless swelling in a dog that is bright, eating, and afebrile suggests a seroma, which is fluid that collects under the skin with movement. It is managed with activity restriction and monitoring. Heat, pain, discharge, or fever would point to infection, a firm opening would suggest dehiscence, and a suture reaction usually shows local redness with a draining tract.

Entering 10 ignores the concentration, and using the 100 mg/mL stock value for a diluted syringe gives 0.1 mL/hr. Dividing by 0.5 mixes the per kg rate with the total amount.
  76. Intraoral radiographs show a widened or narrowed pulp, periapical lucency, or root changes that decide between root canal and extraction.   78. The correct answer is C (About 48 mL/hr, from a 320 mL deficit plus 200 mL maintenance plus 60 mL losses): Deficit = 0.04 x 8 kg x 1000 = 320 mL. Maintenance at 50 mL/kg/day is 400 mL/day, so 200 mL over 12 hours. Add 60 mL of losses: 320 + 200 + 60 = 580 mL, and 580 / 12 is about 48 mL/hr. Semirestricted areas call for clean scrub attire and hair covering, while masks are required in the restricted zone or when sterile supplies are open. The blade should be changed and the veterinarian told, because damaged skin raises the infection risk. A longer time records more movement, not less. A grid and extra mAs lengthen the exposure effect, and a wider field adds scatter without helping sharpness.

Prophy cups are single use or must be sterilized. Alcohol wipes and chlorhexidine soaks do not sterilize, and the handpiece does touch the mouth when the angle is attached.

Reading it and reporting the concern to the supervisor is the correct step. A package insert is written for prescribers, a scavenging log records equipment checks, and practice standards do not replace the sheet.
  84. Blood agar is a general medium that shows hemolysis, mannitol salt agar is selective for staphylococci, and chocolate agar is enriched for fastidious organisms.

  86.   87. Pushing contaminated bowel back in carries bacteria into the abdomen and may kink vessels. Dry gauze sticks to the serosa and dries it out. Surgical scrub solutions are toxic to exposed tissue, so only sterile isotonic saline is used.
  88. The drop began with the tube retie, and pink membranes with a normal SpO2 argue against a perfusion problem. Low pressure would normally raise the rate through the baroreceptor reflex, and pain does the same. The technician should release the tension, check anesthetic depth and tell the veterinarian.
  89. The container is held in the stream without touching the dog. The first portion overstates contaminants, the final portion is not representative, and urine from any floor picks up debris and bacteria.
  90. The correct answer is D (Pasteurella multocida upper respiratory infection, commonly called snuffles): Sneezing, thick white nasal discharge, wet inner forepaws from wiping the nose and noisy breathing in a rabbit from a crowded pet store are the classic findings of Pasteurella infection. Allergic rhinitis gives a clear watery discharge, and myxomatosis causes swollen eyelids and genital swelling. Dental malocclusion mainly causes drooling and weight loss rather than sneezing with thick discharge.
  91. The correct answer is B (Early hyperdynamic (septic) shock needing urgent veterinary assessment and fluids): Red injected membranes, a fast capillary refill, bounding pulses, tachycardia and fever in a dog with a known infection are the hyperdynamic phase of septic shock, which needs urgent veterinary assessment and fluids. A dog in late decompensated shock would have pale membranes and weak pulses. Heat stroke does not explain a pyometra, and oral rehydration is far too slow for a dog in shock.
  92. The correct answer is D (Wash the puncture with soap and water, then tell the supervisor and seek medical care): Cat bite punctures are deep and carry Pasteurella and other bacteria, so thorough washing with soap and water comes first. The injury is then reported to the supervisor under the clinic's workplace incident procedure, and medical care is sought because these wounds often become infected. Continuing the procedure delays treatment, and alcohol alone does not clean a deep puncture.
  93. Stress can raise temperature, but not with these signs, NSAIDs mask a fever, and lancing an abscess is a veterinarian's decision.
  94.   95. Products with VOHC acceptance have shown plaque or calculus reduction. Weekly brushing is too infrequent, hard bones and antlers fracture teeth, and kibble alone does not replace brushing.

Subtracting from the full 10 mL ignores earlier withdrawals and hides a possible discrepancy. Reading 0.3 mL as 3 mL gives a tenfold error, and leaving the balance unchanged breaks the requirement to update it after every use.

  100. The correct answer is B (Limb fractures from uncoordinated standing, reduced by padding and assisted recovery): Horses recover by trying to stand while still ataxic, and the main risk is injury, such as limb fractures or myopathy, made worse by slipping. A padded stall, non-slip floor, head and tail ropes and sedation in recovery reduce that risk. Colic can occur later but is not the principal risk at standing.   101. The correct answer is B (Apply a workplace label naming the product and hazards, with the SDS kept in the lab): When a hazardous product is moved to another container, a workplace label with the product identifier, safe handling information and a reference to the SDS is required. The SDS must be readily available to workers where they use the product, not stored off site or requested only when an exposure happens.
  102. The correct answer is D (A 3.5 mm ID tube premeasured from the incisors to the thoracic inlet): Most adult cats of 3 to 5 kg accept a 3.5 to 4.0 mm ID tube. Measuring from the incisors to the thoracic inlet places the tip in the mid trachea, where ventilation reaches both lungs. A tube measured to the carina tends to enter one bronchus, and a tube too small or too short leaks and cannot protect the airway. A 5.5 mm tube is too large and can injure the feline larynx.

  110. A seroma is a soft, cool, painless pocket without discharge, and normal inflammation is mild and not purulent. A suture reaction usually appears later and rarely causes fever. The veterinarian should examine the wound and an Elizabethan collar should be used.
  111. The correct answer is D (Wash the wound well with soap and water, then contact public health or a physician): Prompt, thorough washing with soap and water lowers the viral load at the site, and it is the first step in a suspected rabies exposure. A bat scratch can be tiny and the bat cannot be tested, so public health or a physician decides about post-exposure prophylaxis. Waiting for the bat delays care, veterinary vaccine is not used on people, and the ten day observation period applies to dogs, cats and ferrets.
  112. The correct answer is D (Hiding away and a hunched posture, and stopping eating or grooming): Cats mask pain, so withdrawal, a tucked or hunched posture, and loss of appetite or grooming are the signs owners should report. Cats also purr when stressed or hurting, so purring does not rule out pain. Long periods of sleeping may be a cat in pain avoiding movement, and head shaking after oral surgery can mean oral discomfort rather than an anesthetic effect.
  113. The correct answer is A (0.4 mL in the oral syringe, given once each day for this cat): Dose volume equals the dose rate times body weight, so 0.1 mL/kg multiplied by 4 kg gives 0.4 mL, which a 1 mL syringe measures easily. Using the rate alone forgets the weight, and doubling it treats a once daily drug as a twice daily one. Dividing the syringe volume by weight uses an operation that does not apply to a per kilogram rate.
  114. The correct answer is A (Abdominal viscera press on the diaphragm and caudal vena cava, cutting venous return and ventilation): In a large horse on its back, the weight of the gut pushes the diaphragm forward and compresses the caudal vena cava, which lowers cardiac output and creates ventilation perfusion mismatch. Muscle compression causes myopathy that shows up later, and excess isoflurane lowers pressure without explaining a falling PaO2.   115. The correct answer is C (Compressions are producing low cardiac output, so change compressors and check depth and rate): During arrest, ETCO2 reflects cardiac output because CO2 reaches the lungs only if blood moves. Values under about 15 mmHg suggest poor compressions, so rotate compressors and check depth and rate. A tube in the esophagus gives near zero, not 12, and a return of spontaneous circulation produces a sudden rise, often above 40 mmHg.
  116. The correct answer is C (Brush the teeth daily with a soft brush and veterinary pet toothpaste): Daily mechanical removal of plaque by brushing is the best supported home measure, and pet toothpaste is safe to swallow. Human toothpaste contains fluoride and detergents that dogs swallow and can upset the stomach. Hard bones and antlers commonly fracture teeth, and water additives may help slightly but do not remove plaque the way a brush does.
  117. The correct answer is D (Supragingival ultrasonic scaling and subgingival curettage, polishing then sulcus irrigation): Gross calculus is removed first with the ultrasonic scaler, then subgingival deposits are cleared with curettes, the crowns are polished to smooth the scratches, and the sulcus is irrigated last to flush out paste and debris. Polishing first leaves calculus under the paste, curettage before gross removal pushes deposits deeper, and irrigating early leaves polishing residue in the sulcus.
