  119. The correct answer is C (Temperature falls steadily and often 1 to 2 degrees C in the first hour): Anesthesia lowers metabolic heat production and blunts thermoregulation, and clipping, cold fluids and open body cavities add to the loss. Small patients such as cats lose 1 to 2 degrees C in the first hour. The normal feline range is 38.0 to 39.2 degrees C, not 37.0. Active warming is therefore planned from the start, and the temperature is recorded regularly.
  120. The correct answer is C (Prepare 10% calcium gluconate for slow IV use under continuous ECG monitoring): A slow rhythm with absent P waves in an obstructed cat points to hyperkalemia, which paralyzes atrial muscle. Calcium gluconate given slowly under ECG monitoring protects the heart within minutes. Atropine often fails when potassium is high, lidocaine is for ventricular tachycardia, and defibrillation is only for ventricular fibrillation or pulseless ventricular tachycardia.
  121. The correct answer is B (Adjust the calibration screw until distilled water reads exactly 1.000, then test the urine): Distilled water must read 1.000 on a urine refractometer, so a reading of 1.004 means the instrument is out of calibration. The calibration screw is turned until the water reads 1.000. Hand-correcting every result or accepting the error risks misjudging concentrating ability, and saline does not read 1.000.
  122. The correct answer is A (Strong positive in the left rear quarter, so collect an aseptic milk sample for culture): The CMT reagent lyses somatic cells and releases DNA, which forms a gel in proportion to the cell count. A thick adherent gel in one quarter is a strong positive, and the three free-flowing quarters are negative. An aseptic sample from the affected quarter alone goes for culture, because pooling dilutes it. A trace would not form a thick gel, and the high bulk-tank count supports a true finding rather than a paddle error.
  123. The correct answer is A (6 mL of the 2% stock solution plus 94 mL of sterile water): Use C1 x V1 = C2 x V2. Then 2% x V1 = 0.12% x 100 mL, so V1 = 12 / 2 = 6 mL of stock, and 100 - 6 = 94 mL of water. Check: 6 mL of 2% stock holds 0.12 g, and 0.12 g in 100 mL is 0.12%. Twelve mL would give 0.24%, 0.6 mL gives only 0.012%, and 16.7 mL comes from dividing the strengths the wrong way around.
  124. The correct answer is B (IV is into a vein and IM into muscle, SQ under skin and PO by mouth): IV means intravenous (into a vein), IM intramuscular (into muscle), SQ subcutaneous (under the skin) and PO per os (by mouth). Each wrong pairing contains one error, such as the rectal route for oral drugs or the abdomen for a vein. A misread route changes onset, absorption and safety, for example a drug meant for muscle given into a vein.
  125. The correct answer is B (It inhibits cyclooxygenase enzymes, which lowers prostaglandins produced at the inflamed joint): NSAIDs inhibit the cyclooxygenase enzymes that convert arachidonic acid into prostaglandins, so less of the inflammatory and pain-sensitizing mediator is made. Antihistamines work at histamine receptors, glucocorticoids act through intracellular receptors and immune suppression, and local anesthetics block sodium channels in neurons.
  126. The correct answer is A (Report to the veterinarian and note the intact tablet so dosing can be repeated): A whole tablet in the vomitus means the dose was probably not absorbed, so the dog may be underdosed, but redosing is a decision for the veterinarian, who also considers why the dog vomited. Skipping the dose without telling anyone risks treatment failure, giving a dose without authority is outside the technician role, and a half dose has no basis when the tablet is intact.
  127. The correct answer is D (Stain is retained where epithelium is missing, so the cornea has an ulcer): Fluorescein is water soluble and binds the exposed hydrophilic corneal stroma, but it does not adhere to intact lipophilic epithelium. A green patch that stays after flushing means the epithelium is absent, which defines an ulcer. Stain draining to the nostril shows duct patency but is a different observation. Dry eye is confirmed with a Schirmer tear test, not fluorescein uptake.
  128. The correct answer is B (Dip the navel in 7 percent iodine and feed 4 L of colostrum within hours): The navel is an entry point for bacteria, so it is dipped in 7 percent iodine soon after birth. The calf gets about 4 L (roughly 10 percent of body weight) of good quality colostrum in the first few hours, because antibody absorption across the gut falls quickly and ends by about 24 hours. Delaying colostrum leads to failure of passive transfer and higher risk of sepsis and scours.
  129. The correct answer is C (20.0 mmol/L, from multiplying 56 by the urea nitrogen factor of 0.357): Urea nitrogen in mg/dL converts to urea in mmol/L by multiplying by 0.357. For 56 mg/dL the arithmetic is 56 x 0.357, which is 19.99, or 20.0 mmol/L. Dividing by the factor gives 156.9. The 0.0884 factor belongs to creatinine and 0.0555 belongs to glucose, so each gives an analyte-specific wrong answer.
  130. The correct answer is D (Withhold food for 8 to 12 hours, and allow water until premedication is given): Healthy adult dogs and cats are typically fasted for roughly 8 to 12 hours, with water until premedication, so the stomach is mostly empty and the risk of regurgitation is lower. A fast that is far longer adds stress and may increase reflux acidity. A very short fast leaves food in the stomach. Pediatric and diabetic patients are fasted for shorter times.
  131. The correct answer is D (Vomiting and poor appetite or black stool, which can mean stomach or intestinal ulceration): NSAIDs reduce protective prostaglandins in the gastrointestinal tract, so ulceration with vomiting, anorexia or melena is the most common adverse effect clients must be told to report. Polydipsia and panting are classic of glucocorticoids, and the other effects are not typical of NSAIDs.
  132. The correct answer is C (Loosen the grip at once so the keel can move, then allow rest and give oxygen): Birds have no diaphragm and breathe by moving the keel and ribs, so compressing the chest in restraint stops them from breathing. Releasing the pressure at once, allowing rest and giving oxygen is correct. A slight loosening is not enough for a gasping bird, neck restraint risks injury, and water by beak can be aspirated by a bird in respiratory distress.
  133. The correct answer is C (Stop the infusion and give oxygen, and alert the veterinarian for diuretic orders): New crackles, a cough and fast breathing after a bolus in a dog with heart disease point to pulmonary edema from volume overload. Stopping fluids prevents more load, oxygen supports breathing, and the veterinarian must order any furosemide. Slowing the rate leaves the overload running, and more fluid would worsen the edema.
  134. The correct answer is B (About 10 to 25 mmHg, with both eyes within a few mmHg of each other): Normal intraocular pressure in dogs is about 10 to 25 mmHg, and the two eyes should be within a few mmHg of each other. A range reaching 35 includes values that suggest glaucoma, a range topping out at 15 includes low values that suggest uveitis, and a 10 mmHg difference between the eyes suggests disease in one of them. The reading is taken after topical anesthetic.
  135. The correct answer is C (Hold the back edge with the cutting edge away, then press the handle into the palm): Sharp instruments are passed so the cutting edge points away from both people and the handle lands in the receiver's hand. Facing the blade at the surgeon or presenting it edge up creates the conditions for a cut, and leaving a blade on the drape risks injury and a contaminated field. A neutral zone basin is the preferred alternative when one is set up.
  136. The correct answer is A (Fear or defensive threat, so approach beside the shoulder and speak calmly): Pinned ears, wide eyes showing the whites and a swishing tail are warning signals of fear or a defensive threat, and a kick is possible. A calm approach at the shoulder, where the horse can see the handler and the hind legs cannot reach, lowers risk. Walking up behind the horse or reaching over its face invites injury, and tiredness does not produce these signals.
  137. The correct answer is A (Clip and scrub a wide area, with the line block placed before the final scrub): A bovine flank prep needs a generous clipped field, a surgical scrub that starts at the incision and moves outward, and the local line block placed before the final scrub because infiltration can introduce contamination. A narrow strip leaves hair near the incision. Painting over dry hair does not clean the skin, and scrubbing inward drags contamination toward the surgical site.
  138. The correct answer is D (Ask the veterinarian to confirm the dose, frequency and product strength first): An incomplete or unclear order must be clarified with the prescriber before it is filled. Here the dose, route, frequency and the vial strength are all uncertain, and 40 mg is a very large amount for a 6 kg dog. A colleague's guess, the client's memory and a calculated dose approved afterward all let a possible dosing error reach the patient, and none is within a technician's role.
  139. The correct answer is B (Treat the test as invalid and discard the device, and repeat on a new one): The control spot confirms the reagents and flow worked, so a missing control makes the whole test invalid whatever the sample spot shows. The device should be discarded and the test repeated on a new one, while checking expiry date, storage temperature and sample volume. Reporting a result, waiting longer, or adding sample to a used device cannot restore a failed control.
  140. The correct answer is C (Ask a second trained person to calculate independently, then compare results before drawing up): A real independent check means a second trained person works the numbers without seeing the earlier result, and the two results are compared. Repeating the sum alone repeats the same slip, showing the answer biases the checker, and pump libraries do not catch a wrong concentration or weight entered by the user. Any disagreement is resolved before the drug is drawn up.
  141. The correct answer is C (A P-glycoprotein defect lets ivermectin into the brain, so alert the veterinarian now): The MDR1 mutation common in collies and related breeds reduces P-glycoprotein, a pump that keeps ivermectin out of the brain. That explains drooling, ataxia, tremors and dilated pupils after a small ingestion, and it needs urgent veterinary care with supportive treatment. Home liver remedies tempt because ivermectin is metabolized there, but they do not address brain exposure. Inducing vomiting in a neurologic dog risks aspiration, and calling the signs stress delays treatment.
  142. The correct answer is A (Heat may have reduced potency, so replace the tablets and store future doses per the label): Many medications lose potency when exposed to high heat, and a car in summer can far exceed label limits. The affected tablets should be replaced and the client shown the label storage directions. Resistance is possible in general but does not fit a drop in effect after a heat exposure. A fatty meal cannot repair a degraded tablet, and refrigeration does not restore potency that heat has already removed.
  143. The correct answer is B (250 kcal per day from 30 x 6 = 180 plus 70): The linear formula is 30 x 6 = 180, plus 70 gives 250 kcal/day. Leaving out the constant gives 180, swapping the terms gives 420, and doubling 250 gives 500, which wrongly applies an illness factor to a figure that is already the resting requirement. Illness factors are no longer routinely applied in hospitalized patients.
  144. The correct answer is C (Notify the veterinarian promptly, since glucosuria with ketonuria suggests ketoacidosis): Large glucose with moderate to large ketones in a vomiting diabetic dog suggests diabetic ketoacidosis, which needs prompt blood gas, electrolyte and chemistry testing and IV fluids. A trace of protein is a minor finding and does not suggest infection. Repeating the strip after a delay or simply recording the values lets a time-sensitive condition go unaddressed.
  145. The correct answer is A (Oxygen support and N-acetylcysteine given as directed by the veterinarian): Cats lack the glucuronidation needed to clear acetaminophen, which causes methemoglobinemia (brown gums) and facial edema. Treatment is oxygen and N-acetylcysteine, which supplies glutathione. Vitamin K1 is for anticoagulant rodenticides and fomepizole is for ethylene glycol. Methylene blue can cause Heinz body anemia in cats and is generally avoided.
  146. The correct answer is D (The 18 gauge catheter is wider, since a lower gauge number means a larger diameter): Gauge numbers run opposite to diameter, so an 18 gauge catheter is wider than a 22 gauge catheter and allows faster fluid flow for a dog in shock. Flow rises steeply with the width of the bore, so width matters far more than a small change in length. A thinner bore does not raise flow, and friction falls as the bore widens, not rises. The wider catheter suits rapid resuscitation in a large patient.
  147. The correct answer is C (0.8 mL, because 2% is 20 mg/mL and 4 kg x 4 mg/kg is 16 mg): A 1% solution holds 10 mg/mL, so a 2% solution holds 20 mg/mL. The ceiling is 4 kg x 4 mg/kg = 16 mg, and 16 mg / 20 mg/mL = 0.8 mL. Reading 2% as 10 mg/mL or as 2 mg/mL gives volumes that are wrong by a factor of two or ten, and halving the dose is not part of the order.
  148. The correct answer is A (Start at the low end of the range, lengthen the interval and watch sedation and breathing): Opioids are metabolized by the liver and their metabolites cleared by the kidney, so reduced function prolongs and intensifies the effect in an older patient. Starting low, lengthening the interval and watching sedation and breathing is the safe approach. An NSAID tempts as a different pathway but is hard on already compromised kidneys. A shorter interval stacks doses, and diluting the same dose leaves the total drug exposure unchanged.
  149. The correct answer is C (Sterile gloves and the catheter kept in its inner package, tip untouched during advancement): Urinary catheterization is a sterile procedure, so the technician wears sterile gloves, uses sterile lubricant, and handles only the part of the catheter still inside its package so the tip stays untouched. Clean gloves tempt because bladder urine is not sterile, but they carry bacteria into the urinary tract. A multi use lubricant tube is contaminated, and an oversized catheter traumatizes the urethral lining instead of protecting it.
  150. The correct answer is B (Track food intake and fecal pellet output each shift, since falling intake signals pain): Rabbits hide pain and are prey animals, so quiet behaviour does not mean comfort. Appetite and fecal output are the practical indicators, because pain reduces eating, which slows the gut and can lead to stasis. Heart rate varies with stress and handling, so it is a poor stand-alone pain measure. Body temperature does not track pain, and expecting thrashing misses the stillness a hurting rabbit shows.
  151. The correct answer is C (Place the bird in a warm dark quiet oxygen-supplemented enclosure and postpone handling): Birds with respiratory distress can die within seconds of restraint because they lack a diaphragm and must move the keel and chest to breathe. A warm, dark, quiet enclosure with oxygen reduces stress while the veterinarian is told. Weighing, blood draws and breath counting all require restraint, so they wait until the bird is stable. A bright, busy area keeps the bird stressed even though it allows watching.
  152. The correct answer is A (Find and repair the leak first and check hoses and bag and canister seals and connections): A fall from 30 to 10 cmH2O in ten seconds means the system is not gas tight, which risks inadequate anesthetic delivery and waste gas exposure to staff. The leak is found and fixed before the patient is induced. Raising the test pressure does not cure a leak, and a mask induction leaks even more gas into the room. The scavenging system is not a cause of a loss in circuit pressure.
  153. The correct answer is D (Cover the paw with a sterile wrap, then scrub outward from the incision): In a hanging leg prep the clipped limb is suspended, the paw is covered so it cannot recontaminate the field, and the final scrub starts at the planned incision and moves outward in widening circles, so the cleanest area stays at the centre. Starting at the toes drags contamination toward the incision. Alcohol alone does not remove debris, and returning to the paw after scrubbing the site brings dirt back to a clean area.
  154. The correct answer is B (A soft, movable fluid pocket under the skin with no leakage from the puncture site): A properly placed subcutaneous injection forms a soft, movable bleb as fluid spreads under the skin, and nothing leaks out when the needle is withdrawn. A warm, tender pocket points to irritation rather than correct placement. A flat site with a bead of blood suggests the fluid did not enter the space, and spreading dark bruising indicates a damaged vessel.
  155. The correct answer is B (0.4 mL, from 40 mg divided by 100 mg/mL): The dose is 5 mg/kg x 8 kg = 40 mg. At 100 mg/mL that is 0.4 mL. The 4 mL syringe is exactly ten times too large and shows a decimal error that would be a serious overdose. Moving the decimal the other way gives a tenfold underdose, and 2 mL assumes a concentration that is not on the vial.
  156. The correct answer is D (Client and patient names and drug name and strength and directions, quantity and date): A dispensed medication label identifies the client and patient, names the drug and strength, gives clear directions for use, states the quantity and the date dispensed, and shows the clinic contact. Each incomplete set tempts because it lists some real label items, but each leaves out something the owner needs to give the right dose safely. Personal phone numbers, bottle expiry details and storage temperatures cannot replace strength, quantity and the dispensing date.
  157. The correct answer is B (Re-inflate the cuff and lower the head, and suction the pharynx): The priority is to stop material from entering the lungs. Re-inflating the cuff seals the trachea, lowering the head lets fluid drain out the mouth, and suction clears the pharynx, and only then is the tube removed. Pulling the tube at once lets fluid fall into the open larynx. Elevating the head sends fluid toward the airway. Esophageal lavage may help later but does not come first.
  158. The correct answer is D (Dogs about 0.37 to 0.55 L/L and cats about 0.30 to 0.45 L/L): Typical reference ranges are 0.37 to 0.55 L/L in dogs and 0.30 to 0.45 L/L in cats, which are the same as 37 to 55 percent and 30 to 45 percent. Cats run lower than dogs, and values near 0.20 to 0.30 would indicate anemia in either species.
  159. The correct answer is A (Draw up with the matching syringe and inject beneath a tent of loose skin, rotating sites): Insulin is given subcutaneously, so the skin is tented and the needle is placed at the base of the tent, and sites are rotated to avoid scarring and uneven absorption. The syringe must match the insulin concentration, because a U-100 syringe used with U-40 insulin delivers a very different dose. Intramuscular injection changes absorption, and vigorous rubbing or repeating one spot affects consistency.
  160. The correct answer is D (Estimate the blood loss from sponges and suction, then report it): Rising heart rate, falling pulse pressure and a weak peripheral pulse suggest hypovolemia, and 400 mL is about 14 mL/kg, a significant amount. The technician should estimate the loss, tell the veterinarian, and prepare isotonic crystalloid or blood products. Deepening or lightening anesthesia does not fix volume loss. Atropine would raise heart rate further and worsen the problem.
  161. The correct answer is A (Mayo scissors for heavy fascia and sutures, Metzenbaum scissors for delicate tissue dissection): Mayo scissors have short, heavy blades suited to fascia and suture, while Metzenbaum scissors have longer, thinner blades for delicate dissection of soft tissue. Cutting suture or tough fascia with Metzenbaums dulls and damages them. Using one pair for both tasks wastes the purpose of each.
  162. The correct answer is A (It confirms the cycle kills resistant spores, proving sterilization conditions were met): Geobacillus stearothermophilus spores are among the most resistant to steam, so if they are killed the cycle has delivered enough heat, pressure and time to sterilize. A chemical indicator only shows that a pack was exposed to conditions, and a gauge check or reservoir cleaning are separate maintenance tasks.
  163. The correct answer is B (30 kg, found by dividing 66 lb by 2.2 lb per kilogram): A kilogram is about 2.2 lb, so pounds are divided by 2.2: 66 divided by 2.2 equals 30 kg. Checking by multiplying, 30 kg times 2.2 gives 66 lb. Halving gives 33 kg because it treats 2 as the factor, using 2.3 gives 28.7 kg, and multiplying by 0.4 gives 26.4 kg. Each of those uses a number other than the 2.2 given in the stem.
  164. The correct answer is D (Measure 25 mL of 2% concentrate, then add sterile water to a 1000 mL total): Use C1 x V1 = C2 x V2: 2% x V1 = 0.05% x 1000 mL, so V1 = 50 / 2 = 25 mL of concentrate, brought up to 1000 mL with sterile water. Checking: 25 mL x 2% = 0.5 and 1000 mL x 0.05% = 0.5. The 40 mL option treats the ratio 2 / 0.05 as a volume, 250 mL is a tenfold slip, and 50 mL would give a 0.1% flush that is twice as strong as ordered.
  165. The correct answer is A (Tape the wings and legs to a radiolucent board, leaving the keel and chest free): Birds breathe by moving the keel and body wall to ventilate air sacs, so anything pressing on the chest can cause hypoventilation, especially under anesthesia. Taping the wings and legs to a radiolucent board gives a symmetrical position and keeps hands out of the beam. A tight towel and weights compress the chest, and hand holding adds exposure.
  166. The correct answer is D (A Bain circuit at about 200 mL/kg/min fresh gas flow, because it adds little resistance): Very small patients need a non-rebreathing circuit with little dead space and resistance, supplied with a high fresh gas flow, about 150 to 300 mL/kg/min, to wash out CO2. A low flow on a Bain circuit leads to rebreathing of CO2. A circle system adds resistance and dead space even with a small bag. A Magill circuit works well in larger patients but its valve and dead space are too much for a 1.5 kg kitten.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Report the count as correct aloud and record it, and initial the sheet before closure): A closing count is done by two people, announced to the surgeon as correct or incorrect, and written on the sheet with initials before the body cavity closes. Here 20 starting squares plus 10 added gives 30, so the sheet is correct and needs no recount. Gauze is easily retained and must be counted along with the sponges. Completing the sheet after closure removes the check at the point it can still prevent a retained item.
  2. The correct answer is A (Keep them labelled and place them in the clinic's pharmaceutical waste bin for authorized disposal): Returned medications should be kept contained and labelled, then sent through an authorized pharmaceutical waste or take-back program. A sharps container is sealed but belongs to a different waste stream. Pouring dissolved tablets down a drain puts drug into waterways just as flushing does. Returned drugs cannot be put back into stock because storage conditions after leaving the clinic are unknown. The technician should also record the return if clinic policy requires it.
  3. The correct answer is C (Wear gloves and face protection, isolate the dog and tell the veterinarian now): Rabies is a reportable disease in Canada, so the veterinarian must be told immediately and will notify the Canadian Food Inspection Agency. Staff should avoid contact with saliva, wear gloves and face protection, and isolate the animal. Ward observation without isolation and a late report both expose staff, and handling the dog to collect saliva adds bite risk. Sending blood for testing does not work because rabies is confirmed on brain tissue after death.
  4. The correct answer is C (58 kVp and 4 mAs, the row covering 9 to 10 cm): The measurement of 9.4 cm falls in the 9 to 10 cm row, which lists 58 kVp and 4 mAs. The 7 to 8 cm row would underexpose a thicker cat, and the 11 to 12 cm row would overexpose it. The chart is for non-grid work, so doubling the mAs is a grid correction that does not belong.
  5. The correct answer is B (Report the finding to the veterinarian right away, since the cast needs removal): Swollen, cool, spread toes mean the cast is compressing the limb and impairing circulation, which can lead to tissue necrosis within hours. Licking at the cast edge is a sign of discomfort. The veterinarian must be told immediately even during surgery. A technician cutting a cast window can cause swelling through the opening, pain medication hides the signal, and elevation and waiting do not relieve the compression.
  6. The correct answer is D (Naloxone, a pure mu antagonist that reverses the overdose): Naloxone is a pure competitive antagonist at opioid receptors and fully reverses a mu agonist overdose, including respiratory depression. Flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines and atipamezole reverses alpha-2 agonists. Butorphanol can partly reverse a mu agonist while keeping some analgesia, but it does not give the complete reversal that this emergency needs.
  7. The correct answer is A (A v-gel supraglottic airway sized for the rabbit's weight, placed without a laryngoscope): A supraglottic device is shaped to sit over the rabbit's larynx and is placed blindly without visualization, which avoids laryngeal trauma and spasm. A cuffed tube passed blindly risks tracheal rupture because the rabbit trachea is fragile. A facemask leaks gas and obstructs the surgical field, and an improvised catheter provides no seal. The device is sized by weight and connected to a nonrebreathing circuit.
  8. The correct answer is A (Residual gas is expected and absorbs within hours, so monitor comfort and breathing): Carbon dioxide used for insufflation is absorbed across the peritoneum, so a drum-like abdomen is normal early on in a comfortable patient with a steady respiratory rate. Pain, pale membranes, rising respiratory effort or a dull fluid wave would suggest a complication, and the technician reports any change. Peritonitis and a bowel leak would be painful, and a tight bandage would only restrict breathing in a dog with expected residual gas.
  9. The correct answer is C (Use is extra-label, so the veterinarian prescribes it and sets an extended withdrawal period to record): Using a drug in a species or way not on its label is extra-label use, which in food-producing animals must be directed by a veterinarian who sets a suitable withdrawal period for both milk and meat. Cattle label times do not apply automatically to goats, and no herd size is exempt. The withdrawal time should be recorded and communicated to the producer.
  10. The correct answer is A (12 mL/hr from 120 mg/hr divided by 10 mg/mL): Dose is 10 kg x 0.2 mg/kg/min = 2 mg/min, or 120 mg/hr. At 10 mg/mL this is 12 mL/hr. 0.2 mL is correct per minute but the pump is set per hour, and 120 forgets the concentration.
  11. The correct answer is C (An exaggerated immune response to plaque, so radiographs and extractions are usually planned): Feline caudal stomatitis is an inflammatory response to plaque on the teeth, and the usual plan includes dental radiographs and extraction of affected teeth, with medical support. Calicivirus may play a role but antibiotics alone do not cure it, and scaling alone does not resolve the disease. Resorption and fluoride do not fit this picture.
  12. The correct answer is D (At the clear tip, leaving about 2 mm of nail beyond the pink quick): In light claws the pink quick is visible, and the cut is made in the clear tip about 2 mm beyond it. Cutting at the curve opens the quick, which is painful and bleeds. Cutting right at the quick edge leaves no margin for a slightly off clip. A second clip into the pink also cuts live tissue, so the first clip beyond the quick is the safe place to stop.
  13. The correct answer is D (Beside the neck near the shoulder, with a handler holding the haltered horse at its head): Standing beside the neck near the shoulder keeps the technician out of reach of both front and hind feet, and a handler at the head controls movement. A horse tied short can pull back or panic with no one to calm it. A handler at the rump leaves the head uncontrolled. Holding the lead while drawing leaves one hand for the needle and no second person to steady the horse if it spooks.
  14. The correct answer is B (The initials of the technician who made the exposures): Checking the exhibit against the audit list: the date, patient, views, kVp and mAs, and the retake with motion blur as its reason are all present. No line records who made the exposures, and that is needed for traceability when a retake pattern or technique problem is reviewed. Client contact details, sedative doses and the radiologist's interpretation may be useful elsewhere but are not on the audit list.
  15. The correct answer is D (520 mL, made up of a 320 mL deficit and 200 mL of twelve-hour maintenance): Deficit = 8 kg x 0.04 = 0.32 L = 320 mL. Maintenance is 50 mL/kg/day x 8 kg = 400 mL/day, so 12 hours needs 200 mL. Total is 320 + 200 = 520 mL. Using only the deficit or only maintenance gives 320 mL or 200 mL, and adding a full day of maintenance gives 720 mL, which overloads a small cat in half a day.
  16. The correct answer is D (Treat it as non sterile and hold it back, then review cycle records and reprocess it): A tape that has not changed color means that pack did not receive the sterilizing conditions, so it must be treated as non sterile and sent through the cycle again after the cause is found, such as a loading or cycle fault. The behaviour of other packs does not prove this one was reached by steam. A new strip tests nothing about the earlier cycle, and clean looking instruments can still carry organisms.
  17. The correct answer is C (No vomiting, but prepare to rinse the mouth and give milk and pain relief): Vomiting would expose the esophagus and mouth to the caustic a second time and risks aspiration. Gentle rinsing, a small volume of water or milk for dilution, and pain control are the first steps. Charcoal does not bind caustic agents and hides the lining for endoscopy, and neutralizing with an acid causes a heat-producing reaction that adds burn damage.
  18. The correct answer is C (A glucocorticoid effect inducing ALP and mildly raising ALT, not liver failure): Glucocorticoids induce a steroid isoenzyme of ALP, so marked ALP with a mild ALT and a raised cholesterol fits steroid hepatopathy. Normal bilirubin and albumin argue against liver failure or obstruction, and the dog is clinically well. Red cells hold little ALP, so hemolysis does not explain the rise.
  19. The correct answer is B (Lower the mAs by about half and keep the kVp, since contrast is already adequate): An overexposed image has too much radiation reaching the film, and since contrast is acceptable the correction is to change density with mAs alone, about half. Changing kVp would also change contrast, and a grid is for parts thicker than about 10 cm and would add exposure. Shorter development might lighten the film but leaves the exposure error uncorrected.
  20. The correct answer is C (Lower the isoflurane and tell the veterinarian, and raise fluids as directed): Propofol given quickly and inhalant together lower blood pressure, and geriatric patients are sensitive to both. The best response is to lower the inhalant, tell the veterinarian, and increase fluid as ordered. Raising isoflurane would deepen hypotension. Propofol also lowers pressure. Waiting 15 minutes leaves an old dog with a low MAP, which risks kidney and brain injury.
  21. The correct answer is B (Gingivitis, where inflammation stays limited to the gum margin without attachment loss): Gingivitis is inflammation of the gum without loss of attachment, so it is reversible once plaque is removed and controlled. Once periodontitis begins, there is bone and attachment loss that does not return, and treatment aims to halt further loss rather than reverse it. Gum recession already means attachment has moved, and a stable, controlled periodontitis is still one with permanent loss. Early periodontitis tempts because little support is gone, but that loss does not return.
  22. The correct answer is A (Methadone premedication and an NSAID once normotensive, and a bupivacaine regional block): Multimodal analgesia combines classes acting at different sites: a mu opioid for central effect, an NSAID for peripheral inflammation once perfusion is adequate, and a local or regional block to stop transmission. Two mu opioids add the same mechanism and more respiratory depression. Two NSAIDs increase gastric and renal risk without added benefit, and butorphanol alone has short, weak analgesia for orthopedic pain.
  23. The correct answer is C (Hypoventilation from excess depth, so lower the vaporizer and assist breaths): The chart shows ETCO2 rising from 38 to 65 mmHg over an hour while the breaths are slow and shallow, which indicates hypoventilation. High isoflurane and an opioid infusion depress the respiratory drive. The first action is to lower the vaporizer and assist ventilation. A fresh canister excludes rebreathing, and an embolism or small leak does not explain a slow, shallow pattern.
  24. The correct answer is D (Compress at 100 to 120 per minute with 10 breaths per minute, rotating every 2 minutes): With a secured airway, compressions continue at 100 to 120 per minute while the ventilator or bagger delivers about 10 breaths per minute without pausing. Compressors rotate every 2 minutes because fatigue lowers compression quality quickly. The 30 to 2 pause pattern is used when the patient is not intubated, 60 per minute is too slow to generate flow, and rotating at 5 minutes allows quality to decay.
  25. The correct answer is A (Fear response, so use a towel and a quiet room and minimal restraint for handling): Salivation, dilated pupils, flattened ears and hiding together suggest a fear response, which is handled with quiet surroundings, minimal restraint and a towel, not force. Firm abdominal palpation would add to the fear and no abdominal signs were reported. Heat stress tempts because of drooling, but nothing suggests overheating and ice cooling is harmful. Firm restraint to teach the cat escalates fear and risks injury to the cat and handler.
  26. The correct answer is B (Pause briefly and let a caregiver take the child out, resuming when ready): A frightened child needs to leave the space with a supportive adult, and a short pause costs the sedated pet little. Telling the child to look away leaves the distress unresolved. Sending a sedated pet home leaves it in an unsafe, uncomfortable state. Reassuring the child to stay quietly keeps a crying child in a stressful room, although a calm child who wants to be present can be gently included.
  27. The correct answer is B (Measure from nose to the last rib and mark the tube, and place a mouth gag): The tube length is measured from the tip of the nose to the last rib so the tip reaches the stomach. A mouth gag stops the dog chewing the tube, and the tube must be lubricated with a water-soluble product. Too short a tube stops in the esophagus and too long a tube risks injury. The veterinarian passes the tube and may stop if resistance is met at the cardia.
  28. The correct answer is C (10 mAs, found by multiplying 200 mA by 0.05 seconds of exposure): mAs is tube current multiplied by exposure time, so 200 mA x 0.05 s = 10 mAs. Dividing current by time gives 4000, which is not a unit of exposure. Reading 0.05 as 0.5 gives 100, a decimal slip, and halving the current first gives 5, an invented step. Checking the product twice gives 10 mAs both times.
  29. The correct answer is B (Remove it from use, discard it per clinic policy and tell the person managing stock): A bag with a leaking port or an altered appearance may be contaminated and must not be given by any route. It should be removed, discarded per policy and reported so other bags can be checked. Alcohol does not sterilize the fluid inside, and a new administration set or cap leaves the contaminated fluid in place. A filter does not remove bacterial toxins that are already in the bag.
  30. The correct answer is A (Hold the NSAID and report the dehydration, and wait until renal status is confirmed): NSAIDs reduce renal blood flow, which is dangerous in a dehydrated, very young patient with no renal values. The correct move is to hold the dose and tell the veterinarian so hydration can be restored and kidney function checked. Half doses still reduce renal perfusion, food helps the stomach and not the kidney, and fluids given after the drug do not undo the exposure.
  31. The correct answer is B (Alert the veterinarian immediately and prepare the antidote, since cats need it within about 3 hours): Antifreeze contains ethylene glycol, which is absorbed rapidly and metabolized to toxins that injure the kidneys. In cats the antidote, such as fomepizole, must start within about 3 hours of ingestion, much sooner than in dogs, so the window is nearly gone. Waiting for kidney values or relying on emesis and charcoal loses the time.
  32. The correct answer is B (Digoxin toxicity, so hold the next dose and call the veterinarian): Vomiting, anorexia and arrhythmias soon after a digoxin increase point to digoxin toxicity, which has a narrow therapeutic index. The technician holds the next dose, tells the veterinarian and prepares for an ECG and serum digoxin level. Giving more digoxin would worsen toxicity, blaming pimobendan ignores the timing, and treating only dehydration continues the likely cause.
  33. The correct answer is D (100 units/mL and ten times the order, since 1 mL went into 9 mL saline): Stock heparin of 1,000 units/mL diluted with 1 mL in 9 mL of saline gives 10 mL at 100 units/mL, which is ten times the ordered 10 units/mL. The correct preparation needs 0.1 mL of stock in 10 mL of saline. A flush that strong risks bleeding in a patient. The other values come from ignoring dilution or applying it twice.
  34. It is unwound turn by turn, since pulling it off drags hair and can injure skin. Adhesive tape directly on skin is not used.

Adjusting the balance without a report hides a possible loss. Waiting for the next count delays action on a controlled drug. The logged waste was 0.2 mL, not a whole vial, so blaming waste does not explain the missing vial.

  37. A width to chest ratio is the cardiothoracic ratio on a ventrodorsal or dorsoventral view. Neither a sternum comparison nor a rib count defines the score.
  38. The correct answer is D (Hyperdynamic septic shock; alert the veterinarian and prepare IV access and fluids): Bright red gums, bounding pulses, a short capillary refill, tachycardia and fever with an infected incision are early hyperdynamic septic shock, driven by vasodilation. The patient needs a veterinarian immediately, IV access, fluids and cultures before antibiotics. Hemorrhagic compensation looks similar but has no source, heat stroke does not explain the incision, and anaphylaxis would follow a drug exposure with hives or swelling.
  39. Only then is the wound lavaged with sterile saline and assessed. Applying a new layer first traps debris, scrubbing with strong antiseptic harms healthy tissue, and ointment before assessment hides the wound's progress.
  40. The correct answer is A (Loud tooth grinding with a hunched posture and less appetite): In rabbits, loud harsh bruxism with a hunched, still posture, reduced appetite and fewer fecal pellets are recognized signs of pain after surgery. Soft chattering while being stroked tempts because it is also tooth noise, but it signals contentment. Foot thumping is an alarm response, and sitting quietly while eating hay and passing normal droppings does not show the intake change that points to pain.
  41. The correct answer is C (Collect with a quick clean venipuncture and gently invert the EDTA tube, then check a smear): Cat platelets clump easily after slow collection or poor mixing, producing falsely low analyzer counts. A quick clean draw, gentle inversion and a smear examined for clumps are the standard prevention and check. Heparin tempts but does not stop clumping, and reporting a low count on a bright cat skips the smear check. A repeat slow draw recreates the cause.
  42. The correct answer is B (Lower the vaporizer and raise fluids as directed, then tell the veterinarian): Low urine output here reflects reduced renal perfusion, usually from hypotension. Lowering the inhalant and increasing fluids as directed support blood pressure and kidney perfusion, and the veterinarian must be informed. Furosemide can worsen volume depletion. Cutting fluids deepens hypotension. Waiting delays treatment of a problem that can cause kidney injury.
  43. The correct answer is D (Heart rate falls and blood pressure rises at first from peripheral vasoconstriction): Dexmedetomidine stimulates alpha-2 receptors, causing peripheral vasoconstriction with an initial rise in blood pressure and a reflex bradycardia, and it also reduces sympathetic outflow and cardiac output. Phenothiazines like acepromazine cause vasodilation and hypotension. Ketamine tends to raise heart rate and blood pressure through sympathetic stimulation.
  44. The correct answer is B (Normal healing with apposed edges and mild pinkness, and slight swelling without discharge): A healing incision at three days commonly has closely apposed edges with mild pinkness and slight swelling, and no discharge or heat. Dehiscence shows gaping edges, a seroma is a fluctuant pocket, and infection brings pus, marked redness, heat, and pain. Draining or opening a normal incision would harm it.

Using the pound figure as kilograms gives 17.6 mL, and multiplying by 2.2 gives 38.7 mL, both dangerous overdoses. The calculated volume is only a ceiling, since propofol is given slowly to effect.

Converting to pounds gives 30.8 lb and 154 mL/hr, which is more than double the order. Using 10 mL/kg/hr gives 140 mL/hr, a rate meant for a different order.   48. The correct answer is B (Warm the cat and tell the veterinarian, who may order a crystalloid fluid bolus): Acepromazine blocks alpha-1 receptors, causing vasodilation, hypotension and heat loss, and it has no reversal agent. Support is warming and a fluid bolus ordered by the veterinarian. Atipamezole reverses alpha-2 agonists only. Epinephrine acts on beta-2 receptors that cause more vasodilation, a classic mistake, and atropine addresses rate while the problem here is low vascular tone.

A stricture is a chronic result of repeated obstruction, and an atonic bladder lets a catheter pass freely, so the catheter itself should not meet resistance.

  57.   58. The correct answer is C (Approximately 37.8 to 39.2 degrees C in a healthy resting ferret): A healthy ferret has a rectal temperature of about 37.8 to 39.2 degrees C, or 100 to 102.5 degrees F. A range starting at 36.5 sits below the lower limit and suggests hypothermia. A range of 39.5 to 40.5 begins above the upper limit and would be a fever. Rabbits run a little higher than ferrets, so their range tempts but does not fit.
  59. Prompt veterinary examination, warmth, quiet and hay are the priority. Mineral oil risks aspiration, and home simethicone with massage delays needed care.   60. The correct answer is C (0.02 mL, because 6 mcg divided by 300 mcg per mL gives this volume): Weight is 120 g = 0.12 kg. Dose is 50 mcg/kg x 0.12 kg = 6 mcg. Volume is 6 mcg / 300 mcg per mL = 0.02 mL. Reading the weight as 1.2 kg or 12 kg gives 0.2 mL or 2 mL, a ten or one hundred fold overdose in a tiny animal. Misreading the concentration gives 0.002 mL, which is too little to measure.

The ovarian bursa and ligament are handled with the ovaries in both procedures, so they do not separate the two.

  69. The correct answer is A (500 mg for the tablet and 0.75 mg for the capsule): One gram is 1000 mg, so 0.5 g is 500 mg. One milligram is 1000 mcg, so 750 mcg is 0.75 mg. Moving the decimal by the wrong number of places gives 50 and 7.5, or 5000 and 0.075, and keeping 750 unchanged treats micrograms as milligrams, which would be a 1000-fold error in a dose.

Gloves, isolation and veterinary notification are the right response, and the veterinarian decides whether the signs fit a notifiable disease. Dividing by two goes the wrong way, reporting 85 ignores the dilution, and using three parts assumes the saline volume was twice the serum volume.

  77. The hematocrit and MCV can read falsely low. Clotting is less likely because there is more EDTA per millilitre, and gentle mixing and prompt testing do not correct the ratio problem.

  82. The correct answer is D (Use cat-labelled products on cats and keep the treated dog from them until dry): Cats cannot safely tolerate permethrin products made for dogs because they metabolize it poorly, so cats are treated only with cat-labelled products. Residue on a recently treated dog can poison a cat that grooms, rubs or sleeps on it, so contact is prevented until the product is dry. Gloves do not replace it, because hands are contaminated during glove removal. Wiping used gloves with alcohol is not an accepted substitute. Blood volume is 90 x 20 = 1800 mL, the rise is 25 - 15 = 10, so 1800 x 10 / 40 = 450 mL. A feline volume gives 300 mL, dividing by 100 gives 180 mL, and using 25 instead of 10 gives 1125 mL.

  86.   87. The correct answer is D (They block voltage-gated sodium channels in nerve fibres, stopping action potentials from travelling): Local anesthetics enter the nerve membrane and block sodium channels from the inside, so the action potential cannot start or propagate and the signal never reaches the spinal cord. Their effect is local and reversible. Calcium channel blockade describes gabapentin, mu activation describes opioids, and cyclooxygenase inhibition describes NSAIDs, each of which works by a different mechanism.

The crowded contact surfaces cannot be cleaned by chewing, tongue motion or brushing, and plaque and calculus build up. Bulldogs do not have extra teeth, and saliva output is not reduced in the breed. Body fat may add to inflammation but does not explain plaque returning within weeks around crowded teeth.

The veterinarian needs to know right away so platelets and coagulation can be assessed. Local pressure or a hemostatic gel treats only the site and lets scaling continue. Giving vitamin K before a diagnosis is not within technician scope and may not suit the cause.

A Doppler probe or ECG is used instead. Rates of 120 to 200 are much too low and would be misread, and a pulse over the wing vein cannot be counted accurately at several hundred beats per minute.
  92. Struvite forms coffin-lid prisms in alkaline urine, ammonium biurate forms brown thorn apples, and cystine forms six-sided plates. Matching shape and urine pH gives the best identification.

  96. Intraperitoneal fluids absorb too slowly in poor perfusion. The umbilical vein is closed in a kitten of this size, and the airway route cannot replace vascular access for fluids.
  97. The correct answer is D (Scrub center outward with soap and rinse with alcohol, repeat three times then apply antiseptic): Skin preparation moves from the center outward so that cleaner skin is never re-contaminated by the dirty periphery. Alternating antiseptic soap with alcohol removes debris and lowers bacterial load, and the final antiseptic is left on to dry. A single alcohol wipe has too little contact, and ending with alcohol only omits the final antiseptic. Palpating the vein after preparing it undoes the aseptic work.
  98. The correct answer is C (Keep the fast short and check blood glucose, and have dextrose containing fluids ready): Ferrets have a high metabolic rate and an insulinoma already pulls glucose down, so fasting is kept to a few hours, glucose is measured, and dextrose is available if levels fall. A long overnight fast risks severe hypoglycemia. A large meal just before anesthesia raises aspiration risk and also triggers more insulin release.
  99. The correct answer is B (The collar is short and loose; refit one that extends past the nose and fits snugly): A collar that is short or loose lets the dog reach the incision. It should extend just past the nose and fit snugly, with room for two fingers at the neck. A soft collar bends and allows reaching, and sedation does not fix a collar that is the wrong size. A bitter spray on a short loose collar still leaves the dog able to reach the sutures.
  100. The correct answer is B (Announce the break and step back, and change to a fresh pair of sterile gloves): The mask is not sterile, so the glove is now contaminated even though the gown is intact. The correct response is to announce it, step away, and reglove using sterile technique. Wiping or spraying a contaminated glove does not make it sterile, and continuing to handle instruments just transfers contamination to the sterile field.
  101. The correct answer is D (Giving an NSAID with a corticosteroid greatly raises the risk of gastrointestinal ulceration): Meloxicam at 0.1 mg/kg and prednisone at 0.5 mg/kg twice daily are both normal doses for a 12 kg dog on day two. The real danger is combining an NSAID with a corticosteroid, which together injure the gastric lining and can cause ulceration or perforation. The veterinarian should be told before the next dose. A high prednisone dose, a day one meloxicam dose or a general spinal contraindication each tempt, but none matches the sheet.
  102. The correct answer is A (Emergence reaction from ketamine, so use a quiet dim kennel and ask about a sedative): Ketamine used without a sedative can cause rough recoveries with vocalization and paddling because of dissociation. The vital signs are normal, so hypoxemia and pain are unlikely. The cat is moved to a quiet, dim place with little handling, and the veterinarian is asked about a low dose of a sedative. Naloxone does not reverse ketamine, and extra ketamine would prolong the reaction.
  103. The correct answer is C (Between 10 and 14 days after surgery, once the edges are sealed and not inflamed): Skin on the trunk usually reaches enough tensile strength by 10 to 14 days in a healthy dog with no healing delay. Removing sutures at 3 to 5 days risks dehiscence because the wound is still in the early repair phase. Leaving them for 3 or more weeks invites skin reaction and sinus tracts. The 5 to 7 day interval suits facial or highly vascular sites, not a midline abdominal incision.
  104. The correct answer is A (Sternal recumbency and pelvis at the table end, with the tail tied forward over the back): A perineal urethrostomy is done from behind, so the cat is placed sternal with the hindquarters hanging past the table end, limbs padded, and the tail tied forward over the back to clear the field. The perineum, scrotal area, and ventral tail base are clipped widely for a sterile prep. The dorsal position suits abdominal surgery, a free tail contaminates the field, and a small clip leaves hair near the incision.
  105. The correct answer is D (Tip the ewe onto her rump, seated against the handler's legs and supported): Sitting a sheep on her rump with her back against the handler's legs relaxes her, exposes the belly and udder, and is the standard posture for shearing and foot care. Pulling wool bruises the skin and damages the fleece. Casting and kneeling on the neck can compress the trachea or the jugular veins. Tying short keeps her standing and leaves the belly out of reach.
  106. The correct answer is D (Blocks vitamin K recycling, so clotting factors already circulating must first be used up): Anticoagulant rodenticides inhibit vitamin K epoxide reductase, so the liver cannot recycle vitamin K to make factors II, VII, IX and X. Factors already circulating keep working until they are used up, which delays bleeding for days. Vitamin K1 supplies the vitamin so new factors can be made, rather than replacing factors directly. Slow absorption and calcium binding sound reasonable but do not explain the delay.
  107. The correct answer is A (The absorbent is exhausted and should be replaced before the next patient): Soda lime indicator dye changes to violet as the granules lose their ability to absorb CO2. When most of the canister has changed, the absorbent is exhausted and the patient would rebreathe CO2, so it is replaced before the next patient. Drying granules, reading white as spent or airing out the canister does not restore absorbing capacity.
  108. The correct answer is A (Only the owner and the treating team and anyone the owner authorizes): Patient information belongs to the owner and is shared with the team caring for the patient and with others only when the owner authorizes it, or when the law requires release. Knowing the pet's name does not make a caller an owner, a breeder no longer holds ownership, and another clinic still needs the owner's authorization before a record is released.
  109. The correct answer is B (Dog 60 to 140 and 10 to 30; cat 140 to 220 and 20 to 30): A calm adult dog normally has a heart rate of about 60 to 140 and a respiratory rate of 10 to 30. A cat runs faster, with a heart rate of about 140 to 220 and a respiratory rate of 20 to 30. Swapping the species, blending them into one range or using stressed respiratory rates would make a normal patient look ill or an ill patient look normal.
  110. The correct answer is B (Press clear acetate tape on the scaly dorsum and examine it at low power): Cheyletiella is a surface mite that lives in keratin debris, so tape impressions, combings and hair pluck samples recover it. Deep scraping is for follicular mites such as Demodex. Fecal flotation and ear swabs are not the right site for a mite on the dorsum.
  111. The correct answer is D (22.0 kg, found by dividing 48.4 lb by 2.2 lb per kilogram): One kilogram is 2.2 lb, so pounds are divided by 2.2: 48.4 / 2.2 = 22.0 kg. Multiplying gives an impossible weight for this dog, halving understates how many pounds are in a kilogram, and 2.5 is not the conversion factor. Every later dose depends on this single number, so it is recorded in kilograms.
  112. The correct answer is A (Trim only the fully mature primaries and leave the dark growing feather uncut): A dark, full shaft is a blood feather with a living blood supply, and cutting it causes bleeding. The technician trims only the mature primaries and leaves the growing feather alone. Cutting it, even with styptic powder ready, creates the injury, and pulling a feather is reserved for one that is already bleeding.
  113. The correct answer is C (The forelimb or cranial body, so the drug reaches the general circulation first): Blood from the hind limbs and tail of many reptiles can pass through the kidneys before reaching the heart, so a drug injected there may be partly excreted or reach the kidney in high concentration. Injecting in the forelimb or cranial body avoids that. Muscle mass and ease of holding do not matter as much as this circulation pattern for a renally excreted drug.
  114. The correct answer is A (A 22 gauge catheter, which suits an adult cat's cephalic vein and gives adequate flow): An adult cat's cephalic vein usually accepts a 22 gauge catheter, which is a good balance of flow and vein size. An 18 or 14 gauge catheter is too wide for a cat's peripheral vein. A 26 gauge catheter is a neonatal or exotic size and restricts fluid flow in a dehydrated cat. Gauge numbers run opposite to diameter, so a higher number is a narrower catheter.
  115. The correct answer is C (Verify two identifiers of name and patient number, and add a surname alert): Two unique identifiers, such as the patient's given name and the patient number, are checked at every treatment, and alert labels warn the whole team about the shared surname. Using the surname or cage card colour alone can fail because dogs with similar names create confusion, and appearance such as breed and weight is not reliable between similar dogs. Confirming identity only at pickup misses medication errors during the stay.

A weak suckle, sunken eyes and a skin tent of about 4 seconds in a standing calf fit roughly 8 percent dehydration, so replacement is balanced electrolytes over 24 hours as the veterinarian directs. A percentage of 12 gives 4.8 L, 5 percent gives 2.0 L and 3 percent gives 1.2 L, each a deficit from the wrong estimate.

The settings are delivering too much minute ventilation, so lowering the rate or volume lets CO2 rise to the 35 to 45 range. Raising ventilation would lower it further.

Chemical indicators only show exposure to conditions, not kill. The remaining packs must be held and the veterinarian informed because patients may have been exposed.   119. The correct answer is B (The supplier's safety data sheet kept at the workplace lists hazards, first aid and spill steps): WHMIS 2015 requires suppliers to provide a safety data sheet for hazardous products, and employers must keep it accessible to workers. It covers hazards, safe handling, first aid, spill response and disposal. Label pictograms give only a quick hazard warning, a drug package insert describes patient use, and a practice standards document does not list product hazards.
  120. The correct answer is C (125 mL/hr from dividing the 1,000 mL volume by 8 hours of infusion): Rate in mL/hr equals volume divided by hours: 1,000 mL / 8 hr = 125 mL/hr. Dividing by 480 minutes gives about 2 mL per minute, which is not an hourly rate. Dividing by 10 hours would stretch the bag past the plan, and the multiply-by-8-over-60 route has no physical meaning.
  121. The correct answer is B (1 mg/kg, because 1.2 mL x 10 mg/mL is 12 mg for 12 kg): Volume x concentration gives milligrams: 1.2 mL x 10 mg/mL = 12 mg. Dose per kilogram is 12 mg / 12 kg = 1 mg/kg, which equals the labelled injectable dose, so the order is within the label. Dividing mL by kilograms gives 0.1, reading the vial as 100 mg/mL gives 10, and quoting the oral rate gives 2, none of which comes from the numbers given.
  122. The correct answer is D (Apply a forced warm air blanket with towel insulation and recheck temperature every 15 minutes): Shivering increases oxygen demand and metabolic rate, so a cat at 35.2 C needs active external warming. Forced warm air blankets give controlled, even heat without burn risk, and rechecking temperature every 15 minutes prevents overshooting. Passive insulation alone is too slow, and heating pads or hair dryers set high cause burns in a vasoconstricted, recovering patient.
  123. The correct answer is D (Discard the first high reading and average the four consistent readings, about 138 to 142): Oscillometric readings vary, and the first reading is often an outlier. Technicians take several measurements, discard the outlier and average the consistent ones, here about 138 to 142 mmHg. Averaging all five gives 144, which the outlier drags upward, and choosing the lowest value is just as arbitrary. Waiting for two identical readings is unrealistic with this method.
  124. The correct answer is C (Biological substance Category B and marked UN3373, and triple packaged for courier transport): A specimen suspected of containing an infectious agent that is not a high-consequence pathogen ships as Biological substance, Category B, marked UN3373 and triple packaged with a leakproof primary container, absorbent secondary packaging and a rigid outer box. Category A is for severe pathogens, a bare label does not meet the requirement, and a padded envelope is inadequate.
  125. The correct answer is D (2.4 mL, because 0.5% equals 5 mg/mL and the total dose is 12 mg): A percent solution is grams per 100 mL, so 0.5% is 0.5 g per 100 mL = 500 mg / 100 mL = 5 mg/mL. The dose is 1 mg/kg x 12 kg = 12 mg, and 12 mg / 5 mg/mL = 2.4 mL. The 24 mL answer treats the product as 0.5 mg/mL, the 0.24 mL answer treats it as 50 mg/mL, and 1.2 mL uses the 10 mg/mL strength of a 1% product.
  126. Corn syrup on the gums raises glucose quickly while IV access is arranged for dextrose. Prednisolone works too slowly for an emergency, a fluid bolus does not treat the cause, and searching for an oral foreign body delays correcting the low glucose.
  127. The correct answer is A (Schirmer test first, then fluorescein with a rinse and tonometry last): The Schirmer test must come before any drops, because anesthetic reduces tear production and stain with rinsing disturbs the tear film. Staining is next, and tonometry is performed last after topical anesthetic. The veterinarian decides whether tonometry is safe when a deep ulcer is suspected.
  128. The correct answer is B (The supervising veterinarian stays responsible, while technicians work within provincial regulations): Veterinarians supervise and delegate, and they stay accountable for the care of the patient. The tasks a technician may perform differ by province under the veterinary acts and association rules, so the technician also works within that scope. Delegation does not move responsibility to the technician, the practice owner holds the business rather than the medical act, and associations set rules rather than approving each task.
  129. The correct answer is D (Rebreathing from exhausted soda lime or a stuck valve, so replace the absorbent): A capnogram baseline that stays above zero means the dog is inhaling CO2, so the problem is in the circuit, usually exhausted soda lime or an incompetent expiratory valve. Hypoventilation or hyperthermia can raise the peak, but the baseline still returns to zero. A kinked tube would limit flow and breathing, and anesthetic depth and rate are unchanged here.
  130. The correct answer is A (Delayed opioid respiratory depression; notify the veterinarian, give oxygen and support ventilation): Morphine is hydrophilic, so after epidural use it can spread cranially and cause respiratory depression hours later. A rate of 6 with SpO2 at 90% is a risk to oxygenation and needs immediate action. The technician alerts the veterinarian, supplies oxygen and supports breathing, with reversal as directed. Treating it as natural sleep wastes time, and a fluid bolus or dextrose would not correct a failing respiratory rate.
  131. The correct answer is B (The tube lumen is blocked with mucus, so remove or clear the tube and ventilate): Visible mucus in the tube together with a motionless bag and strong chest effort shows an obstruction of the narrow tube. Small birds have complete tracheal rings and small tubes that clog easily, so the tube is cleared or replaced and breaths are given. A tube past the syrinx gives one sided ventilation without a visible plug, and deepening anesthesia or suspecting an air sac puncture does not clear the blockage.
  132. The correct answer is A (Stop the infusion and leave the catheter in, then aspirate residual drug before removing it): Swelling at the catheter site during infusion signals extravasation, where drug leaks into perivascular tissue. The infusion is stopped, and the catheter stays in so residual drug can be aspirated back out before removal, then the veterinarian directs further care. Flushing or continuing to infuse drives more irritant into the tissue, and removing the catheter first loses the chance to aspirate.
  133. The correct answer is A (Dogs in hospital with acute pain, such as after surgery or trauma): The Glasgow Composite Measure Pain Scale is a clinician scored tool validated for acute pain in hospitalized dogs, for example after surgery or trauma. Cats use separate feline scales. Chronic osteoarthritis at home is measured with owner completed questionnaires, so an owner scored acute scale is also a mismatch.

A weight-based dose is recalculated on the current weight and confirmed by the veterinarian before the refill. Repeating the old dose or recalculating from the old weight keeps the error, and skipping confirmation leaves a changed dose unchecked.
  135. Records are never erased or silently adjusted. The technician informs the supervising veterinarian, documents the discrepancy, and the clinic investigates and reports as required. A corrective entry or a crossed-out balance hides the problem, and waiting delays an investigation.
  136. The correct answer is B (Offer a small bland meal, then increase portions gradually over the next day): Once the dog is alert, swallowing normally and not vomiting, small portions of a bland, digestible food provide energy that supports healing without overloading the stomach. Portions then increase as the dog tolerates them. Withholding water or food for a long time delays recovery, and rich full portions risk vomiting and slow gastric emptying.
  137. The correct answer is B (An esophagostomy tube, placed through the lateral neck into the esophagus): An esophagostomy tube enters the cervical esophagus through a small incision in the lateral neck and is useful for weeks to months. A gastrostomy tube goes through the body wall into the stomach, and a nasogastric tube passes through a nostril for short term use. A pharyngostomy tube enters through the pharynx rather than the neck incision described.
  138. The correct answer is C (Aim perpendicular to an imaginary line bisecting the angle between the tooth axis and the sensor): When the sensor cannot be placed parallel to the tooth, as at the mandibular canine, the bisecting angle technique is used. The beam is aimed at right angles to an imaginary line that splits the angle between the tooth's long axis and the sensor plane, which gives an image close to true length. Aiming at the sensor foreshortens the tooth, and aiming at the tooth axis elongates it. The parallel technique is reserved for the mandibular cheek teeth.
  139. The correct answer is A (Fissure lines between lobes and retracted rounded lung borders with the heart outline obscured): Pleural fluid surrounds and retracts the lung lobes, so scalloped borders, interlobar fissure lines and a lost heart outline are typical. A mass appears as a focal opacity that displaces structures like the trachea. Hyperinflation with a flattened diaphragm suggests air trapping, and bronchial rings suggest bronchial disease. Both look different from fluid.
  140. It is not used, and it is reported and discarded by protocol because it may involve a controlled drug that must be accounted for. Relying on a colleague's memory or on the volume is unsafe, and discarding it alone without a witness leaves the drug unaccounted for.
  141. A single site cannot hold 3 mL, and 3 mL given into the abdomen would still exceed what a 30 g mouse can safely receive in one injection. Cold fluids add a chill risk to an already small animal.
  142. Confirmation with a urine protein to creatinine ratio or sulfosalicylic acid test is advised. Glomerular disease, infection, and concentration do not best explain 2+ protein with inactive sediment and a pH of 9.0.
  143. The correct answer is D (Degenerating renal tubular epithelial cells, supporting kidney tubular injury): Casts form only in renal tubules, and granular casts are made of degenerating tubular epithelial cells, so they point toward tubular injury, which matches grape toxicity with azotemia and isosthenuria. Casts do not come from the bladder or genital tract. A rare hyaline cast can be normal, but several granular casts are not.
  144. The correct answer is B (Centrifuge the tube and move the serum to a labelled plain tube, then ship cold): Serum left on the clot for hours exposes it to red cell metabolism, which lowers glucose and raises potassium and phosphorus. The sample should be centrifuged promptly and the serum transferred to a clearly labelled tube, then shipped cool with a requisition. Freezing clotted blood hemolyzes the cells, a clot does not protect the serum, and EDTA chelates calcium and magnesium and contaminates a chemistry sample.
  145. The correct answer is D (A bactericidal drug, because this dog has too few neutrophils to clear inhibited organisms): Bacteriostatic drugs stop bacterial growth and rely on the immune system to finish the job, which works in a healthy animal. A neutropenic septic dog lacks that defence, so a drug that kills bacteria directly is favoured. Spectrum matters, but it is not the only factor, and the endotoxin concern does not outweigh the need to clear infection.
  146. The correct answer is B (Fill the wound with sterile water-soluble gel, then clip nearby hair and scrub the skin): A sterile water-soluble gel traps hair and debris during clipping so it can be rinsed away with the gel. Clipping dry lets hair fall into the tissue, concentrated scrub damages exposed tissue, and ointment under a bandage seals contaminants in before the wound has been cleaned or assessed.
  147. The correct answer is A (The set's drop factor of 15 gtt/mL does not match the 60 gtt/mL programmed): A drop-counting pump counts drops, so it relies on the drop factor entered. A standard adult set delivers 15 gtt/mL, so each drop is four times the volume the pump expects, and 12 mL/hr becomes 48 mL/hr, which is 96 mL over 2 hours. A units slip would give a much larger error, double counting would give only 48 mL, and a weight entry does not change a set mL/hr rate.

The reserve cylinder is then connected and the circuit refilled. Turning the vaporizer up gives agent with no oxygen. The flush valve uses the same empty supply. Waiting lets hypoxemia develop.

  150. The correct answer is D (A 2.4 cm cuff at 40 percent of limb circumference over the palmar artery): The cuff bladder width should be about 30 to 40 percent of limb circumference, so 6 cm gives roughly 1.8 to 2.4 cm, and a 2.4 cm cuff fits. An oversized cuff such as 4 cm underestimates pressure, while a cuff that is too narrow overestimates it. A loose cuff also reads falsely high and the probe belongs distal to the cuff over the palmar artery, not at the elbow fold.
  151. The correct answer is D (Scaling leaves microscopic scratches in enamel, and polishing smooths them so plaque attaches more slowly afterward): Ultrasonic and hand scaling roughen enamel at a microscopic level, and a rough surface collects plaque faster. Polishing with a fine paste and light pressure smooths those scratches, so the plaque film re-forms more slowly. It does not bleach intrinsic stain, it cannot reach subgingival calculus (that is the job of curettage), and it does not sterilize the tooth. Polishing is done last, followed by flushing of the sulcus, to remove paste debris.
  152. The first molar is 09 in the dog, so the upper right first molar is 109. The 108 is the carnassial fourth premolar, 209 is on the left, and 409 is a lower right tooth.

Excess traction changes limb position but not pelvic symmetry, and centring only moves the field. Inward stifle rotation affects the patella and femur shape, not the symmetry of the pelvis itself.
  154. The correct answer is D (95 to 100 percent, measuring hemoglobin saturation but not ventilation adequacy): Pulse oximetry estimates the percent of hemoglobin carrying oxygen, with a target of 95 to 100 percent. A reading under 90 percent is hypoxemia. It does not measure the partial pressure of oxygen, which needs a blood gas, and it does not measure ventilation, because on high oxygen a patient can read normally while hypoventilating. CO2 is measured by capnography.

The rod shape seen here points to Gram negative rods, which are common in wound infections. Cocci in clusters or pairs would look round, and Gram positive organisms would be purple.
  156. The correct answer is D (Nonregenerative anemia, with a low absolute reticulocyte count and no polychromasia): A regenerative response in dogs usually shows an absolute reticulocyte count above about 60 x10^9/L, polychromasia, and often a raised MCV. This dog has a low count, no polychromasia and normocytic indices, so the bone marrow is not responding, which points to marrow disease or chronic disease. Hemolysis and blood loss give regeneration after three to five days, and iron deficiency gives microcytosis.
  157. The correct answer is B (Naloxone, a mu antagonist that also reverses the pain relief the opioid gave): Naloxone competes at mu receptors and reverses opioid respiratory depression, but it also removes pain relief, so the dose is titrated. Flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines. Atipamezole and yohimbine reverse alpha-2 agonists such as dexmedetomidine and xylazine. Using the wrong agent leaves the respiratory depression in place.

A general manual or poster describes rules rather than the hazards of one product, and a brochure promotes the product rather than listing its hazards.
  159. Technicians commonly give ordered drugs, take radiographs and scale and polish under direction, although the exact tasks permitted differ by province, so staff should confirm their own provincial rules.
  160. The lid is locked and the container is disposed of as sealed before the new procedure adds more sharps. Waiting until later or pressing the contents down brings fingers close to needle tips.
  161. The correct answer is B (Polypropylene, a synthetic monofilament that remains in tissue and keeps its strength): Polypropylene is a nonabsorbable monofilament that stays in tissue indefinitely and keeps its strength. Polydioxanone, chromic catgut and polyglactin 910 all lose strength and are absorbed, though they differ in how long they hold. Holding strength for a long time does not make a suture nonabsorbable.
  162. The correct answer is A (Unlock and open every hinged instrument and lay them loosely so steam reaches the joints): Steam sterilizes only what it touches, so box locks and ratchets must stay open and the load must be loose enough for steam to circulate. A tightly packed tray with closed ratchets traps air in the joints. Extra wrap lengthens steam penetration time rather than improving sterility, and stacking weight on top of delicate instruments blocks contact and risks damage.
  163. The correct answer is B (50 mL, from 5 kg multiplied by 10 mL/kg): Convert first: 11 lb divided by 2.2 gives 5 kg. Then 5 kg x 10 mL/kg is 50 mL. Using 11 as kg gives 110 mL, which would exceed the safe volume. Dividing gives 5 mL and halving the limit gives 25 mL, neither of which follows the stated protocol.
  164. The correct answer is A (Without coolant water, friction from prolonged bur contact overheated the tooth and injured the pulp): High-speed handpieces generate friction heat, and the water spray carries it away so the pulp stays within a safe temperature. A clogged line plus long continuous contact let heat build and damage the pulp. Bur grit, a slow speed or air pressure do not explain a thermal injury the way a blocked coolant supply does.

Accurate records of volume and character help the veterinarian decide the next step. Removing early, clamping the line, or flushing without an order can cause fluid pooling, infection, or a break in a closed system.

The correct answer is C (Place large-bore IV catheters and start ordered shock fluids while alerting the veterinarian): A dog with a distended abdomen, pale gums and a heart rate of 170 is in shock from gastric dilation and volvulus, so access and shock fluid come first. Radiographs are valuable but follow initial stabilization. An orogastric tube is passed under the veterinarian's direction, and may not pass in a volvulus. Simethicone and walking do nothing for a torsion.
